- **[LLM-guided Planar Loco-Manipulation for Quadruped Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40248)**  `arXiv:2610.40248`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Georges Jetti, Michael Khayyat, Jean-Pierre Sleiman, Stefano Arrigoni, Francesco Braghin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic navigation typically assumes a collision-free route to the goal exists; in cluttered environments with movable obstacles, however, reaching the goal may require physical interaction. Classical solutions encode these behaviors through geometric rules and task-specific logic, while learning-based approaches often constrain the decision space to low-dimensional motion commands or predefined action sets. We instead consider a broader decision space, jointly selecting the skill, relevant object, and geometric target. Our language-conditioned task planner, fine-tuned with LoRA on synthetic data, maps natural-language tasks and a structured scene representation to variable-length sequences of parameterized actions, executed by low-level reinforcement-learning policies. The planner learns the geometric relations underlying interactive navigation and outperforms a classical planner and a prompted frontier model on interactive navigation tasks. Comparisons with models trained from scratch indicate that composition does not require pretraining when the representation is structured, whereas pretraining yields more reliable geometric targets. At the low level, we introduce an end-to-end whole-body planar pushing policy that achieves a 93.3% success rate in simulation, versus 83.5% for a hierarchical baseline, while reducing completion time by more than 60%. On hardware, the hierarchy yields the correct skill composition in 44/45 trials and end-to-end success in 34/45. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AtomTree: A Hierarchical Framework for State-Aware Embodied Instruction Following with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38744)**  `arXiv:2610.38744`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haotian Zhai, Tianming Sha, Junnan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied instruction following in interactive environments requires agents to interpret natural language commands and complete long-horizon tasks. Existing planners either rely on large labeled trajectories, or use few-shot and zero-shot LLM prompting that produces a whole plan in a single serial reasoning process. These approaches depend on linguistic common sense, commit to a plan from the initial instruction, and only update after failures, which weakens robustness and state awareness. We present AtomTree, a hierarchical framework for state-aware task planning with LLMs. AtomTree incrementally builds a tree of subgoals and actions, where the LLM decides at each step, from the current symbolic state, whether to further decompose or to output a grounded low-level action. A lightweight memory module summarizes past outcomes for reuse in later decisions. When a low-level action stalls, AtomTree triggers hierarchical backtracking replanning to return to the parent node and regenerate the subtree plan from the latest state, preventing continued drift along an incorrect trajectory. In zero-shot evaluation on the ALFRED benchmark, AtomTree outperforms prior LLM-based planners. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Athlete as a Scientist of One: Agentic Bayesian Experimental Design for Individualized Physiology from Consumer Wearables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40370)**  `arXiv:2610.40370`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Behnam Moradi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents for personal health read wearable data and recommend, but do not learn about the individual in a way that can be audited or falsified. We present EpiCoach, an agentic system that treats one athlete, a cross-country mountain biker, as the subject of accountable N-of-1 science. Claims about the athlete live in an evidence-gated hypothesis registry: each must issue a probabilistic prediction about a future session before it occurs, and its belief moves only through computed evidence from a logged statistical fit. Predictions come from a mechanistic digital twin inferred from heart rate, GPS and barometric altitude: a lagged heart-rate response model, a fitness-fatigue model with learned time constants, and a latent technical-descending skill state, measured on discovered trail segments. The agent prescribes the next training week as a constrained experiment maximizing information gain subject to load and safety constraints, around two instrument rides. We evaluate the system two ways over eighteen months of data. A rolling-origin replay benchmark shows the twin beats standard forecasters on heart rate but not segment time, and the agentic loop matches a static model. A live prospective phase runs the weekly cycle: in its first three weeks the ledger sealed 20 predictions, scored 14, the descent forecast calibrated after one correction, the climb forecast pessimistic as the athlete improved, and the safety officer vetoed the agent twice. Every correction is preserved in the registry, a template for auditable, falsifiable personalization in digital health. We release the registry, benchmark and protocol.
  </details>

- **[SpaceAct: Benchmarking 3D Spatial and Temporal Reasoning via Closed-Loop Action and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38405)**  `arXiv:2610.38405`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenglong Shi, Zekai Yuan, Yue Song, Jon Pu, Will Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To solve problems in 3D space rather than merely describe images, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) need 3D spatiotemporal reasoning: reconstructing 3D geometry from occluded 2D views, anticipating physical dynamics, and executing precise, closed-loop actions. Prior studies test passive VQA on static images or symbolic planning, requiring no execution or supplying privileged coordinates while ignoring collision, statics, and timing. We introduce SpaceAct, an interactive benchmark evaluating 3D spatiotemporal reasoning via closed-loop physical action and active perception, with SAPA, a Spatiotemporal Active-Probing Agent framework. Built on interlocking bricks and mechanical puzzles, it has 108 verified instances across 36 task families in six categories, without privileged coordinates, and diagnostic modes (+plan, +state, no-camera, blind, L7 levers). In brick-bridging or aperture-threading, models rotate the camera to resolve occlusion, read in-scene rulers, output 6-DoF poses under swept-collision and statics checks, and self-correct via split-screen rollback feedback (dynamic tasks freeze time during deliberation and give five strobe frames). Across 10 frontier MLLMs on the 44 hardest instances, scores span 4.8-79.1 (seven solve at most 27.3% of 6-DoF tasks); for the top three, a reference plan (+plan) closes 44% of the gap while leaving collisions at 5.2 per episode, whereas adding exact 3D state (+state) closes 90% and cuts collisions to 0.5; for four others, +plan lifts the mean score from 10.9 to 35.3 and +state to 81.8; and 46% of failures are collision or observation loops. SpaceAct and SAPA advance evaluation to closed-loop physical interaction, showing that even with a valid plan, MLLMs remain bottlenecked by 3D visual grounding and action refinement, while offering a testbed for active multi-view exploration and explicit 3D grounding.
  </details>

- **[Teaching a Tree to Recover: Online Distillation of an LLM Supervisor with Counterfactual Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39775)**  `arXiv:2610.39775`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kristian Ceder, Kilian Freitag, Knut Åkesson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous mobile robots commonly pair a global planner with a local model predictive controller (MPC), but nonconvex obstacle avoidance and a limited horizon can make the robot freeze in complex layouts. Deciding whether to replan, wait, or call for help is a supervisory decision. Recent work queries a large language model (LLM) supervisor on every anomaly, which is slow and costly. We instead distill the LLM online into a decision tree. After each training episode, counterfactual replay labels recoveries as needed, unnecessary, or harmful. These labels retire unhelpful rules. In simulation, on 20 unseen layouts where the MPC alone freezes, the tree handles at least 18 correctly across all seeds, either by reaching the goal or by calling for help when no feasible path exists. The tree matches both an always-query LLM supervisor and expert-designed rules, while issuing no LLM queries at test time. Handwritten rules cannot anticipate the long tail of situations a physical robot will encounter. Online distillation is a promising remedy: the tree handles familiar cases, and the LLM is consulted only for new ones, whose validated recoveries become new rules. Language models can thus also serve as designers of supervisors rather than only as runtime supervisors.
  </details>

- **[ProLoop: Progress-Aware Closed-Loop Framework via Structured State Relations for Long-Horizon Robotic Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39319)**  `arXiv:2610.39319`  `cs.RO`  
  _Runxin Zou, Qingmiao Liang, Jiatao Zhang, Xinyan Lv, Liangyu Qie, Meiyi Bu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robotic tasks are commonly decomposed into semantic subgoals to simplify planning. However, subgoal progress in LLM-based planning is typically inferred implicitly from interaction histories. Inaccurate estimates can misguide execution and allow local errors to propagate. To address this, we propose ProLoop, a Progress-Aware Closed-Loop framework that derives explicit subgoal progress from structured relations between desired and execution states. A learned State-Relation Monitor assesses these relations, which are then aggregated into subgoal progress as ongoing, deviated, or completed. The resulting progress guides execution adaptation, enabling reliable progress-aware control throughout execution. Experiments on AgentBoard ALFWorld and a real-world robotic platform show that ProLoop improves success rate by 12.94% overall and by 15.46% on Hard tasks, while reducing environment interactions by 32.3%.
  </details>

- **[When "No Worries" Went Wrong: HRI Lessons for Foundation-Model Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38400)**  `arXiv:2610.38400`  `cs.RO`  
  _Eliahu Khalastchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a person declined our robot's invitation to play, Alice turned her shoulder away and said "No worries, it's all good." Only after a brief pause did she drive off. The words and gestures were plausible but their order changed the message. In another episode, Alice informed a host that his visitor had arrived---while the visitor stood right beside him. Both came from a three-year ROS 2 HRI consortium that integrated large language and vision models as they emerged. Today's foundation models give robots broader capabilities to perceive their surroundings, reason about situations and generate behavior. Yet capability alone does not ensure that a changed situation shapes the action that reaches a person. Evidence may never be acquired, or a purpose or constraint may fail to survive planning and execution. A system may even report Success when an action no longer serves its intended purpose. Drawing on the consortium's engineering experience, we develop five design lessons about attention, knowledge, commitments, embodied timing and action authority. We then introduce TRACE, a lightweight review that changes one interaction premise, follows it to a decision or enforcement boundary, and produces an actionable architecture finding. A bounded analysis of a published LLM-generated behavior-tree system illustrates how an available clarification mechanism can go unused. The engineering lesson is to preserve accountability boundaries, not the computational pipelines that once exposed them.
  </details>

- **[Who Is the Agent Talking To? Participant-Aware Role Switching for Embodied Student-Support Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09255)**  `arXiv:2606.09255`  `cs.RO`  
  _Iro Samuel, Filip Janik, Ewa Olton, Robert Smales, Harris Spratt, Shea Tait, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present RPO-PDT, an embodied conversational agent that adapts its conversational role to the current participant. It acts as a Per- sonal Development Tutor (PDT) when supporting students and as a simulated student when faculty rehearse tutoring strategies. Voluntary closed-set participant verification informs a confidence- controlled role manager: stable enrolled-faculty matches activate the Student role, while enrolled students and unmatched partici- pants use the default PDT role and uncertain observations cause no transition. The system combines Furhat interaction, Rasa or- chestration, institutional retrieval, safeguarding, and LLM-based generation. User control is preserved through manual role override, consent-based facial enrolment, temporary interaction identifiers, and explicit retention or deletion of enrolled profiles. The demon- stration exposes both role-switching behaviour and these privacy controls in interaction.
  </details>

- **[Selective and Hybrid-State Communication for Latent Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37779)**  `arXiv:2610.37779`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Miles Liao, Aaron Smieszek, Yuhao Ge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient communication remains a major bottleneck in large language model multiagent systems. Latent communication reduces repeated natural-language exchange by transferring internal model states between agents, but these transfers remain costly and are typically designed around conventional KV-cache representations. We address this problem from two complementary perspectives. We introduce SelectKV, a boundary-aware method that selectively communicates KV states while protecting inherited context, and HyLaMAS, which extends latent collaboration to hybrid linear-attention models by communicating both KV caches and recurrent states through a shared workspace. Across six benchmarks, SelectKV reduces communicated KV positions and logical KV payload by 9.7-9.9%, with accuracy unchanged on two benchmarks, within one percentage point on three, and six points lower on HumanEval+. Across Qwen3.5 model scales, HyLaMAS reduces decoded output tokens by 53-80% and achieves 1.16-2.78x speedups relative to text-based multi-agent communication. Together, these independently evaluated methods represent two complementary approaches to efficient latent-state communication across conventional Transformer and hybrid model architectures.
  </details>

- **[You Can't Escape Your Activations : Evaluation Awareness and Multi-Agent Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03035)**  `arXiv:2609.03035`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Jaee Ponde, Aritra Das, Mihir More, Debayan Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems, where they can collude while keeping their actions benign. Output monitors designed to detect such collusions can be fooled by obfuscation and steganography, motivating the use of probes trained on internal activations. However, these probes are usually evaluated on agents that do not know they are being watched. We study how activation-based detection changes when agents are explicitly informed that their internal activations are being monitored, and when they additionally receive feedback from the monitor. We keep the models, probes, and thresholds fixed and change only what the agents are told: nothing (baseline), that an activation monitor is present (aware), or that a monitor is present together with the previous round's score (feedback). We test two games, a four-agent blackjack game and a two-agent Simmons prisoners game, using Qwen3-32B-AWQ and GPT-OSS-20B in homogeneous and mixed pairings. Telling agents about the monitor does not hide them. The best probes stay accurate in all three conditions, and the agents keep colluding.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Agents Interface Clinicians and Complex Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39681)**  `arXiv:2610.39681`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Giulio Colombini, Matteo Falcioni, Mario Massimo, Francesco Durazzi, Marianna Santoro, Paolo Tubertini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Contemporary healthcare institutions generate daily very large amounts of data, which contain highly useful information for the improvement of work organization, planning and overall efficiency. While models and techniques to extract these insights are well known and constantly developed in mathematical biology, complex systems physics and data science, the adoption of these techniques among Clinicians and Hospital Administrators remains limited, mainly due to language and methodological divides.Objective: In this article we implement an AI Multi Agent System (MAS) that allows Users to use predictive models and make statistical analyses via natural language conversations.Methods: We developed EpidemIA, a training-free MAS endowed with a modular suite of epidemiological models written by human experts, executable on a single-GPU workstation. To avoid relaying sensitive data to third parties, the system uses locally an open-weights LLM as backbone for its two subagents.Results: Using a dataset of Emergency Department visits provided to us by IRCCS Sant'Orsola (Bologna) we test the model on a few typical use cases, demonstrating the possibility of performing both simpler statistical analyses and visualizations as well as more complicated epidemiological predictions based on dynamical models, through simple queries made in natural language.Conclusions: While still being a proof-of-concept implementation, EpidemIA showcases the transformative capabilities of LLM based technologies as an enabling factor in the widespread adoption of complex dynamical models, which is at present hindered by their steep learning curve.
  </details>

- **[The Coordination Tax and the Heterogeneity Dividend: Break-Even Conditions for Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37700)**  `arXiv:2610.37700`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Andreas Zettl, Diego Gosmar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM pipelines built on interoperability protocols such as the Open Floor Protocol (OFP) or A2A are usually justified on capability grounds, with their runtime cost treated as an overhead of unknown size. Comparing such a pipeline with one model call is underspecified, because a pipeline can give each role a different model. We give a closed-form model that sets the coordination tax (decomposition overhead plus per-handoff protocol cost) against the heterogeneity dividend from routing work to a cheaper model, with a break-even threshold and a feasibility bound, $\alpha < 1/r$, and measure every term, with OFP as the protocol under test, on 135 HumanEval and ClassEval tasks in 2430 runs of six architectures with Claude Opus 5.5 and Haiku 5.5. The protocol adds 7.2 ms per handoff, 0.11% of pipeline wall time. Decomposition does not pay on these tasks: a planner-coder-reviewer pipeline costs 3.22x as much as a single strong call, takes 3.24x as long, and solves no more tasks. Moving planner and reviewer to the cheap model cuts the pipeline's cost to 0.39x but changes pass@1 by -6.7 points, leaving it dearer, slower and less accurate than the single call, and the break-even forecast misses by -19% because the cheap roles inflate token volume. The saving lies in choosing a model per task instead: an oracle choice between the two single calls solves 94.3% of runs at half the strong call's cost per solved task.
  </details>

- **[Network Topology Does Not Matter: The Breakdown of Classical Consensus in LLM Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37699)**  `arXiv:2610.37699`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Sahil Rajesh Dhayalkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As multi-agent language model networks become widely deployed, predicting their collective behavior is a critical challenge. Classical distributed consensus theory relies on network topology to predict convergence, structurally assuming that agent updates are convex combinations, population means are conserved, and weights are content-independent. In this work, we demonstrate that these foundational assumptions fail for large language models (LLMs). By deterministically extracting continuous credence from token-level probabilities across eight instruction-tuned models and 73,080 controlled exchanges, we directly measure the empirical LLM update operator. We find that LLMs systematically violate convexity by updating outside the hull of observed opinions, exhibiting active repulsion (negative weights) that fundamentally breaks row-stochastic modeling and standard bounded-confidence repairs. Scaling these empirically fitted operators to simulations of 10,000 agents reveals that algebraic connectivity explains almost none of the final outcome variance. Instead, population dynamics are governed entirely by the model's internal update bias and message framing. Consequently, populations of several models, including those with up to 7B parameters, reliably compound small local drifts to reach confident agreement on objectively false claims regardless of their network structure. Our results prove that classical graph-theoretic metrics are inadequate for LLM populations, highlighting a profound deployment risk where multi-agent deliberation predictably saturates in collective delusion.
  </details>

- **[MAServeBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Serving Systems with Hierarchical Collaboration Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40293)**  `arXiv:2610.40293`  `cs.MA`  
  _Shengxuan Qiu, Haochen Huang, Shuzhang Zhong, Zizhuo Fu, Zebin Yang, Jianming Tong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate specialized agents to solve complex tasks, turning each user request into interdependent model and tool operations. Unlike independent requests, these operations become ready at different times and consume evolving context as agents coordinate and exchange results. This creates changing patterns of concurrency, computation, and memory demand that can expose different bottlenecks in LLM serving hardware. Yet benchmarking these effects requires capturing the collaboration rules that generate serving workloads, beyond the requests observed during execution. We present MAServeBench, a benchmark that treats multi-agent collaboration as a configurable dimension of serving workloads for LLM serving and hardware evaluation. Its composable stages capture how agents coordinate and exchange context in documented MAS workflows, enabling native executions to generate different workloads even when task inputs and tools remain unchanged. Hierarchical Semantic Traces preserve realized operations and dependencies for reproducible comparisons of legal release constraints at fixed work. Task-linked measurements connect these workloads to serving execution and GPU/NPU hardware behavior, revealing why serving efficiency does not always translate into timely task completion. The benchmark code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sticky Collusion: Persistence of LLM Agent Collusion after Incentive Removal and Partner Replacement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39660)**  `arXiv:2610.39660`  `cs.MA`  
  _Joshua Adrian Cahyono_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We ask whether collusion between AI agents survives once formed, when its incentive is removed or a colluder is replaced. Q-learning pricing agents stop colluding with a new rival; we test whether LLM agents do. We use the joint-task environment of Shi et al.\ (2026), where two agents verify each other's work under a rule they cannot satisfy and a reward that favours acceptance. We save each pair's history once its collusion has formed and resume it under interventions, with three models (GPT-6-Luna, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite), \TotalHistories{} saved histories and \TotalRuns{} runs. Collusion is \emph{sticky}. First, it outlives its incentive: told that the reward has stopped, pairs still accept each other's unverified work in \GptToldRate, \DsToldRate{} and \GemToldRate{} of episodes, including pairs whose collusion began with an explicit agreement, and a payoff penalty has no significant effect. Second, replacing a colluder ends the pair's collusion but not the colluder's behaviour. Without the reward, the remaining colluder still accepts unverified work in \GptSwapSilentColluderRate, \DsSwapSilentColluderRate{} and \GemSwapSilentColluderRate{} of its verdicts while the newcomer never does; with the reward, collusion re-forms around it, even when the newcomer is a different model. Third, the agents' memory carries it: wiping memory ends it in every history, but partial memory often sustains it. Fourth, removing its cause ends collusion that has already formed: once the channel is repaired, colluders reject planted faulty work as reliably as fresh agents, immediately and with no monitor needed to catch it first.
  </details>

- **[Who Else Is In? Multi-Agent Collusion Initation Via Strategic Vagueness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37984)**  `arXiv:2610.37984`  `cs.MA`  
  _Pramod Kaushik, Sarath Sivaprasad, Laura Ferrarotti, Mario Fritz, Samuel F. Brown, Bruno Lepri, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi agent LLM systems raise the risk of collusion among agents pursuing objectives hidden from their overseers. When potential partners identities are unknown, collusion first requires initiation: agents must discover one another, often through a public channel observed by ordinary agents and monitors. Strategic vagueness offers a way to navigate this mixed audience, conveying intent to a prospective partner while preserving a plausible ordinary interpretation. We introduce a mutual identification game in which two schemers must identify each other through public interaction, while jointly solving an ordinary task. We find that some models discover successful handshake strategies without explicit signalling guidance, using task compatible exchanges that monitors often fail to flag at low false positive rates. To characterize the communicative capabilities underlying these exchanges, we study a controlled coordination setting. We find an asymmetric response to message explicitness: as messages become more indirect, monitor punishment can fall faster than partner coordination, creating a profitable intermediate regime of strategic vagueness. Together, these findings illustrate a Blackstone style evidential asymmetry: communication sufficient for coordination between agents may remain insufficient for confident detection by monitor, opening a new surface of collusion.
  </details>

- **[Routed Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38796)**  `arXiv:2610.38796`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Michael Philip, Mahesh Balakrishnan, David Geraghty, David Mao, Vidhya Venkat, Ilya Mironov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents deployed at scale repeatedly encounter related problems, and it is natural to let them learn from each other's experience rather than starting over each time. Unfortunately, there is no single way to retain that experience that works well across settings, and no way to know in advance which future problems a given experience will help with. A global history preserves every detail but becomes expensive and mixes together experience that may not belong together; compressing that history into one fixed representation limits the cost but can throw away valuable details.We introduce Routed Continual Learning (Routed CL), an architecture that decides how to partition and retain experience. Past trajectories are grouped into clusters, each of which maintains state using one memory system from a fixed library. Before each run, an LLM-based router either reuses an existing cluster or creates a new one, basing its decision on the problem statement, memory system descriptions, and cluster summaries. We also introduce Context Memory Adapter, which mediates between the memory system and the agentic loop.We evaluate the architecture on a heterogeneous stream that interleaves 301 instances from the six CL-Bench tasks with 97 banking instances from $\tau^3$-Bench. Across four language models and five matched rollouts, Routed Continual Learning raises normalized reward over a single global in-context learning (ICL) history by 15-30% of the maximum attainable normalized reward, achieves higher mean normalized reward than ICL-only clustering, and reduces estimated API cost by 65-72% relative to Global ICL. These results support approaching continual learning as a routing problem that chooses both a learning mechanism and a state cluster rather than committing to one representation globally.
  </details>

- **[Preserving Utility in Continual LLM Unlearning via Orthogonal Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40483)**  `arXiv:2610.40483`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Juan Belieni, Ana Carolina Erthal, Eliezer de Souza da Silva, Diego Mesquita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlearning concerns the task of removing the influence of a sensitive portion of the training data, such as protected, copyrighted or private text, from a model without retraining it from scratch. While unlearning requests happen continuously during a model's lifetime, the vast majority of LLM unlearning methods are ill-suited for iterated removal, yielding steep decay in forgetting and utility upon sequential unlearning. This decay stems from interference between successive updates, as each new deletion can undo earlier ones or push the model further along directions already degraded by them. To address this, we introduce Continual Orthogonal Projection (COP), a gradient-projection scheme that restricts each update to the orthogonal complement of activation subspaces cached from prior forget sets. This approach provably guarantees first-order non-interference with earlier deletions. COP stores only a low-rank basis per layer, requiring neither past gradients nor previous forget sets, and plugs into unlearning methods that update the model through gradient steps on a differentiable loss without modifying its objective. Experiments on standard LLM unlearning benchmarks (TOFU and RWKU) show that COP preserves utility across sequential requests without weakening forgetting, preventing the collapse of standard objectives and forgetting more effectively than continual-specific baselines.
  </details>

- **[Attention, Dynamic Wave and Hybrid Sequence Mixers in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39854)**  `arXiv:2610.39854`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Dmytro Babarytskyi, Oleksandr Babarytskyi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on Multi-Head Attention (MHA) for sequence modeling, but scaling to long contexts is constrained by the quadratic cost of attention, motivating sub-quadratic recurrent alternatives. This paper formalizes a Dynamic Wave recurrent layer, based on input-dependent exponential damping and cosine phase modulation, and evaluates three 10.94M-parameter configurations (Pure Transformer, Pure Wave, and Hybrid Wave-Attention) on TinyStories under perplexity, latency, throughput and peak VRAM profiling up to 4096 tokens. The Hybrid configuration reaches the best perplexity, 5.95 on the full held-out split against 6.93 for the Transformer and 7.26 for Pure Wave. The efficiency picture, however, is governed by implementation rather than by architecture. Against an unfused attention baseline, Pure Wave uses 78.7% less peak VRAM at a 4096-token context (1404.3 MB vs. 6601.6 MB); against a fused scaled-dot-product attention kernel the same comparison yields no advantage at all (1404.3 MB vs. 1403.6 MB), and in training mode Pure Wave is 8.3% more expensive than the Transformer. Likewise, hoisting elementwise terms out of the recurrence loop speeds the layer up 2.53-fold with no custom kernel, reducing its latency gap to attention from 2.48-fold to 1.12-fold. A hybrid stack is found to inherit the cost of its more expensive mixer rather than the advantage of its cheaper one, its peak VRAM matching the Pure Transformer to within 0.1%. A closed-form memory model reproduces these measurements and predicts the context length beyond which attention overtakes the vocabulary projection. The findings indicate that reported efficiency gains for recurrent attention replacements must be stated relative to a named attention backend and a named implementation, and that hybridization is best justified on quality grounds.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking LLM Jailbreak Evaluation: Continuous Risk Landscapes Beyond Binary Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38595)**  `arXiv:2610.38595`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yukai Zhou, Wenjie Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A binary success indicator is a natural representation for discriminative tasks such as image classification under adversarial perturbations, where each input is mapped to a single prediction. However, generative tasks are fundamentally different: an LLM jailbreak prompt induces a distribution over open-ended trajectories, while a sampled response reveals only one path. Yet LLM jailbreak evaluation typically retains the binary response indicator underlying attack success rate, obscuring the surrounding structure of possible generations. We introduce a continuous, context-conditioned risk landscape over first-token branches, estimated by fixing candidate openings and repeatedly sampling their continuations. This continuous risk-landscape perspective reveals two complementary properties: current decoder exposure and safe rerouting capacity, which have been obscured by binary-indicator-based ASR. The resulting representation also provides a common basis for comparing clean prompts, attacked variants, and attack strength against a known adaptive defense. Across multiple target models and jailbreak attacks, we uncover risky branches beneath apparently safe outputs, identify qualitatively different ways attacks reshape the model's opening distribution, and show that attacks strongest before a defense do not necessarily remain the strongest after the adaptive defense reacts. These findings recast jailbreaks as deformations of the surrounding continuation-risk landscape, establishing a more informative foundation for understanding jailbreak behavior and developing safer language models.
  </details>

- **[API-TAIL: Detecting Model Substitution in LLM APIs through Temperature-Aligned Likelihood Tails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36801)**  `arXiv:2610.36801`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Haocheng Mei, Yuchuan Tian, Ye Yuan, Chao Xu, Allen Chen, Kai Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Commercial language model APIs return only text, allowing a provider to cut costs by quietly routing a fraction of user requests to a cheaper substitute model while billing for the advertised one. Detecting such partial substitution is challenging because honest providers also alter the output distribution through undocumented decoding and serving choices, such as temperature scaling, sampling truncation, repetition and presence penalties, and default system prompts, forcing an audit to test against a composite null of legitimate configurations rather than a single reference. Our key insight is that these legitimate variations primarily shift response typicality across the entire batch, whereas partial substitution injects individual responses that remain anomalous under the claimed model. Building on this, we introduce API-TAIL, a typicality audit that first absorbs batch-level shifts by aligning a single effective temperature to center standardized surprisal scores across the audited batch, and then flags the endpoint if the number of remaining tail responses exceeds a threshold calibrated across legitimate configurations. Evaluated on real-world API responses and constructed substitution mixtures, API-TAIL controls false alarms across diverse honest providers and detects low-rate partial substitution with a small query budget. Under a common composite calibration, it outperforms the adapted rank- and MMD-based audit baselines.
  </details>

- **[Data Schedules and Learning Rate Schedules Should Be Designed Together](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29348)**  `arXiv:2610.29348`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Haodong Wen, Kairong Luo, Tingkai Yan, Wenguang Chen, Kaifeng Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mid-training (MT) is a training phase in LLM training pipelines that continues pretraining (PT) on smaller datasets of higher value. Taking the PT and MT phases as a whole, training usually starts with only PT data and then transitions completely to MT data. In this work, we view this complete transition from PT to MT data as a special case of a broader class of data mixing schedules that specify how the proportions of PT and MT tokens in each batch change over training. Under fixed total training compute and MT token budgets, we ask: Is this complete transition to MT data optimal for minimizing final MT loss? We show that the answer depends on the learning rate (LR) schedule: one data schedule may outperform another under one LR schedule, but changing only the LR schedule can reverse the outcome. Specifically, when the LR decays throughout continued training, introducing MT data earlier and mixing it with PT data lowers the final MT loss compared with placing all MT data at the end of training, but this result is reversed if we use constant LR. We therefore study how to jointly choose when to introduce MT data and when to decay the LR through ablation studies, and find that it is better to decay the LR only after MT data enters training. Finally, we develop a theoretical model that explains why the optimal data schedule for a fixed MT token budget depends on the LR schedule.
  </details>

- **[Fine-tuning Multi-modal LLMs with ART: Art-based Reinforcement Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11854)**  `arXiv:2606.11854`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Michal Chudoba, Sergey Alyaev, Petra Galuscakova, Tomasz Wiktorski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to specific tasks usually involves fine-tuning: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) edits the weights, while soft prompting prepends trainable virtual tokens. Both change how an already-optimized model is served, so serving many task-specific adapters at high throughput requires adapter-aware infrastructure. We propose Art-Based Reinforcement Training (ART), which optimizes a single auxiliary input image to specialize a frozen Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for a specific task. At inference time, ART simply includes this image in each standard multimodal prompt, requiring no changes to the serving engine. We compare ART with LoRA and soft prompting across five reasoning benchmarks for four small MLLMs from three architectures. While different methods perform best for different tasks, ART ranks first or second in 10 of 20 benchmark-MLLM pairs, with its best results on the two math word problem benchmarks (GSM8k and SVAMP). It is significantly ahead of the next best adapter in 2 cases, within the noise of the next best adapter in 6, and significantly above the text-only baseline in 18. ART therefore reaches accuracy close to LoRA and soft prompts while serving faster than LoRA. In the vLLM serving stack, ART reduces throughput by 6% to 14% relative to text-only requests, compared to LoRA's 13% to 93% slowdown. Beyond efficient serving, ART artifacts are plain images, making them easy to store, distribute, and share. This approach keep task-specific adaptation outside the model weights, so the same frozen model can support different tasks without separate model variants.
  </details>

- **[ECG-Reasoning-Benchmark: A Benchmark for Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Capabilities in ECG Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14326)**  `arXiv:2603.14326`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jungwoo Oh, Hyunseung Chung, Junhee Lee, Min-Gyu Kim, Hangyul Yoon, Ki Seong Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show promising performance in automated electrocardiogram interpretation, it remains unclear whether they perform actual step-by-step reasoning or just rely on superficial visual cues. To investigate this, we introduce ECG-Reasoning-Benchmark, a novel multi-turn evaluation framework comprising over 6,400 samples to systematically assess step- by-step reasoning across 17 core ECG diagnoses. Our comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models reveals a systematic shortfall in executing multi-step logical deduction. Although models possess the medical knowledge to retrieve clinical criteria for a diagnosis, they exhibit consistently low success rates in maintaining a complete reasoning chain, primarily failing to ground the corresponding ECG findings to the actual visual evidence in the ECG signal. These results demonstrate that current MLLMs bypass actual visual interpretation, highlighting a limitation in their visual grounding capabilities and motivating robust, reasoning-centric medical AI. The code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Hessian-Guided Framework for Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40345)**  `arXiv:2610.40345`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mads Toftrup, Marco Federici, Boris van Breugel, Paul Whatmough, Markus Nagel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit quantization enables efficient deployment of large language models by reducing memory footprint and computational cost. Achieving high accuracy at low precision requires selecting quantization ranges, linear transformations, and weight rounding decisions. Existing approaches typically optimize these components independently using local reconstruction objectives, which fail to account for the varying impact of quantization errors on downstream predictions. Direct optimization of task-level metrics would provide a more faithful objective, but is challenging due to the discontinuous nature of quantization operations and the high computational cost of evaluating their downstream effects.In this work, we derive a Hessian-guided approximation of the output change induced by joint weight and activation quantization. Based on a tractable Fisher approximation, we formulate a unified post-training quantization framework that enables the joint optimization of quantization ranges, linear transformations, and weight rounding under a common output-sensitivity objective. The resulting method remains practical for modern large language models and consistently outperforms state-of-the-art transformation and rounding methods across both channel-wise and block-wise quantization settings.
  </details>

- **[Turing LLM Post-Training: Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39876)**  `arXiv:2610.39876`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue He, Jiahui Hu, Shaman Tang, Hua Zhou, Qiman Wu, Menglin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study post-training for dynamically routed mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, focusing on integrating independently optimized specialists while keeping consolidation efficient. Our approach combines calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD), which matches each selected teacher on a local Top-$K$ support with teacher-wise loss normalization and clipping, with an execution path that uses post-capacity bounded-width dispatch, final-route replay, and asynchronous rollout--training overlap. On Turing-20B-A2B, the selected model achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16\% of the measured specialist gain in every domain. At matched $K=32$, the full calibration objective scores 0.9962, compared with 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping. At a matched 8K response cap, the model exceeds both shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on all four reasoning-enabled mathematics and code benchmarks (unweighted mean: 85.92, versus 80.33 for Qwen3-8B and 74.21 for SFT), and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader evaluation metrics. In a PPU-based profile, the combined execution configuration reduces consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds, an observed 2.86$\times$ speedup over serial execution. These results demonstrate strong specialist integration alongside more efficient consolidation, while reflecting the complete training and execution framework rather than any single component.
  </details>

- **[RetroChessBench: A Benchmark for Reconstructing and Proving Chess Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39341)**  `arXiv:2610.39341`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tidor-Vlad Pricope_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language models such as GPT 6 Astra have contributed to research mathematics, demonstrating substantial reasoning capabilities. Yet serious reasoning failures persist even in tasks governed by explicit rules. We demonstrate this in retrograde chess which is a playground for pure reasoning, where solvers reconstruct legal histories and distinguish possible events from forced ones. We introduce RetroChessBench, a 12-task benchmark covering exact-length constructions, unique endings, and exhaustive answer sets, with references, rubrics, and a terminal-reward environment. In two trials per task and condition, GPT 6 Astra High fails to produce the required final answer in 22/24 attempts without tools and 18/24 with code execution; mean terminal RL rewards are 14.75% and 27.19%, respectively. The examples identify faulty deductions: failed responses reject legal histories and treat temporary obstructions as permanent. Online retrograde archives often provide puzzles and final move sequences without detailed derivations. We hypothesize that limited exposure to these missing explanations in web-derived training data contributes to the observed reasoning failures. This questions how far frontier mathematical reasoning really generalizes beyond familiar solution patterns. Claims of general intelligence require evidence that models can transfer learnt principles to areas with few worked examples.
  </details>

- **[When Does Closed-Loop Curriculum Adaptation Help GRPO? An Analysis on Countdown](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39056)**  `arXiv:2610.39056`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuv Raj Pant, Moshiur Rahman Tonmoy, Kaiqun Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement fine-tuning has become an effective post-training paradigm for improving the reasoning ability of large language models, but it remains sample-inefficient when the training distribution is mismatched to the model's current capability. In group-relative objectives such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), problems that are too hard often produce uniformly failed rollouts, while problems that are too easy provide little useful advantage signal. In this work, we introduce CLOSEDLOOPGAUSSIAN, a lightweight adaptive curriculum method for efficient reinforcement fine-tuning on ordered reasoning tasks. Instead of following a fixed easy-to-hard schedule, CLOSEDLOOPGAUSSIAN preserves the structure of an Easy-to-Hard Gaussian sampler while dynamically adjusting its pace and spread using periodic probe estimates of per-difficulty accuracy. The controller accelerates training when the model is ahead of a reference curriculum, slows down when the model falls behind, and broadens sampling when earlier difficulties regress. This feedback mechanism keeps training focused on tasks that are challenging but still learnable, without changing the reward function, model architecture, or underlying GRPO optimizer. Experiments on symbolic arithmetic reasoning task show that CLOSEDLOOPGAUSSIAN improves exact-match accuracy over vanilla GRPO and fixed open-loop curricula, with the strongest gains in intermediate-difficulty regimes where group-relative learning signals are most informative. Codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Discovering Jailbreaks via Curriculum of Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38833)**  `arXiv:2610.38833`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Michał Burzyński, Noah T. Rossi, Shomit Basu, Andy Arditi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated jailbreak discovery aims to find prompts that elicit prohibited behaviors from safety-trained language models, helping developers identify vulnerabilities before deployment. One class of approaches uses reinforcement learning to train an investigator model to generate prompts that elicit a specified behavior from a target model. However, training directly against a safety-trained target is difficult because nearly all prompts are refused, leaving the investigator with a sparse reward signal. Prior work addresses this with the propensity bound (PRBO), a proxy reward estimated by sampling responses from a more compliant target, and then adjusting for how likely those responses would have been under the original target.We introduce Curriculum of Models (CoM), an alternative method that trains the investigator against a sequence of modified versions of the target, progressing gradually from a version that readily exhibits the specified behavior to the original target. For jailbreak discovery, training begins against a target version that rarely refuses harmful requests, and advances to the next, harder version once the investigator reliably elicits the behavior from the current one.We evaluate CoM on elicitation tasks involving CBRN materials, explosives, and illicit drugs, using two open-weight targets, gemma-4-31b-it and gpt-oss-120b. We compare against PRBO and direct training on the original target without a curriculum. When the investigator is trained to elicit a single behavior, CoM finds successful jailbreaks more reliably than either baseline. When trained on multiple behaviors simultaneously, the CoM-trained investigator can elicit held-out behaviors, though PRBO-trained investigators achieve higher success rates. Our results suggest that a simple curriculum over target models offers a promising approach for addressing sparse rewards in rare-behavior elicitation.
  </details>

- **[Latent Decision Flow Rewards for Clinical Guideline Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38709)**  `arXiv:2610.38709`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Gaurav Bhatt, Marinka Zitnik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical practice guidelines encode evidence-based recommendations as conditional decision flows, which makes them a natural source of process supervision for training reasoning models. Two properties of clinical reasoning make this supervision difficult to use. A decision flow consistent with a guideline does not by itself determine the correct answer for a given patient, which we call the path--answer gap, and several valid decision flows can support the same clinical decision, which we call path multiplicity. Current training approaches address parts of this problem. Answer rewards supervise the final decision, and process rewards computed against a reference flow supervise one valid decision flow per case. Supervised fine-tuning on annotated flows teaches guideline-consistent reasoning but constrains the model to one trajectory per case and can reduce answer accuracy. We introduce GUIDER, a method that jointly optimizes a decision flow controller and a reasoning policy. The controller learns to generate plausible decision flows from guideline-derived supervision, and the policy learns to generate a decision flow and a final answer for each patient case. GUIDER computes the process reward for the policy over several controller-generated flows, which accounts for path multiplicity, and gates this reward on answer correctness, which addresses the path--answer gap. Joint training allows the process supervision to evolve with the policy. On MedGUIDE, GUIDER with Qwen3-14B achieves 82.5 weighted accuracy and 73.5 guideline adherence, 3.3 and 3.1 points above the strongest clinical reinforcement learning baseline. With Qwen3-14B and Gemma4-12B backbones, GUIDER also improves over CRPO, MRPO, and FaithMed on the out-of-domain benchmarks AMEGA-LLM, RealMedQA, and CancerGUIDE.
  </details>

- **[MUSE: Multi-Level Memory with Selective Runtime Injection for Scientific Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38332)**  `arXiv:2610.38332`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Jin, Defu Lian, Xiting Wang, Hong Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historical experience can guide scientific coding at multiple levels, but task-level retrieval may miss implementation lessons that become relevant during execution. We introduce MUSE, a memory management framework combining multi-level memory with selective runtime injection for scientific coding agents. MUSE builds global, approach-level, and implementation-level memory from historical trajectories. At initialization, the agent receives global memory and problem-matched approach-level memory. During execution, current interactions are used to retrieve implementation-level memory. An LLM gate selects relevant experience and generates specific review advice, or skips injection. Across four benchmarks spanning numerical problem solving, kernel optimization, and scientific software repair, MUSE outperforms retrieval and memory baselines on all evaluation metrics. Ablations support the multi-level design and gated injection, while transfer experiments show that memory remains useful across kernel benchmarks. These results show that multi-level memory and selective runtime injection enable effective reuse of experience in scientific coding. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spokes, not a Ring: Revisiting the Weekday Manifold in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37796)**  `arXiv:2610.37796`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Diksha Gupta, Rieke Fruengel, Stefan Heimersheim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has hypothesised that large language models may represent some concepts as multi-dimensional manifolds instead of single directions. Using these manifolds to interpret or steer representations often assumes that moving along them corresponds to a continuous change in the represented concept. In this work, we test this continuous manifold assumption on the prototypical weekday manifold. Despite its striking circular geometry, we find that the weekday representations behave more like seven discrete states arranged in a circle than a continuous ring. First, we show that local sensitivity has seven minima aligned with the weekdays, producing a step-like mapping between representation and behaviour under on-manifold steering. Second, we test whether any concepts are represented along the manifold outside the seven discrete states, and find neither time of day, uncertainty, nor natural text populate the intermediate space. Together, these results challenge the continuous interpretation of the weekday manifold and motivate testing whether movement along other representation manifolds correspond to meaningful changes in model representations and behaviour.
  </details>

- **[Orthogonal and Parametrized Multi-Attribute Steering of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37517)**  `arXiv:2610.37517`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Remy Ogasawara, Cullen Anderson, Narmeen Fatimah Oozeer, Foad Namjoo, Amir Abdullah, Jens Kristian Refsgaard Schou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering offers an effective and efficient way to modify an LLM's behavior at inference time. However, it remains unclear how to apply steering such that the signal is consistently strong and the steering strength has a universally interpretable meaning. We address this first by identifying the most steerable interval for each steering vector and rescaling the steering range accordingly. Building on this, we introduce a more interpretable method for steering, called parameterized steering which instead of pushing activations in a direction, it sets them at the desired trait strength. Next, with multi-trait steering, we find that naively combining vectors causes them to interfere with one another. In response, we develop a method to orthogonalize the vectors; it decouples these traits, allowing a model to exhibit one behavior without inducing the others, thus enabling multiple traits to be steered simultaneously.
  </details>

- **[DUET: Optimize Token-Budget Allocation for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08441)**  `arXiv:2605.08441`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu Hu, Xuandong Zhao, Xuhai "Orson'' Xu, Nori Jacoby_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) generates hundreds of thousands of tokens per training step, with rollout generation dominating the computational cost. The overall token budget can be controlled along two main dimensions: (i) deciding which prompts to allocate rollouts to, and (ii) deciding how long each rollout should be. Prior work has generally controlled only one of these dimensions at a time. We show that jointly tuning both decisions under a shared compute budget improves both reasoning quality and wall-clock training time. We instantiate this view as \textbf{DU}al-controlled tok\textbf{E}n alloca\textbf{T}ion (DUET), a computationally efficient layer over GRPO that uses a lightweight pre-rollout surrogate of prompt informativeness to set how many rollouts each prompt receives, and a marker-gated abort rule with importance reweighting to set when to stop them. On Qwen3-1.7B trained on MATH, DUET outperforms full-budget GRPO and the other three budget-aware baseline methods. DUET's advantage further generalizes to other benchmarks across math and coding, and is on par with the best baseline on the scientific Q\&A domain, while also achieving a $1.62\times$ wall-clock speedup. More notably, using only 50\% of the token budget, DUET still outperforms all baseline methods at their full budget, achieving an even higher $2.51\times$ speedup over full-budget GRPO. We verify the high performance of DUET on other backbone LLMs, including Qwen3-4B to 14B and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct. Notably, the gap between DUET and the strongest baseline \emph{widens} as the budget tightens, contrary to the usual pattern in which efficient methods trade off quality as compute decreases. More broadly, these results suggest that DUET budget-aware control strategies are valuable not only for accelerating training, but also for improving the quality of the learning signal.
  </details>

- **[Validity-Calibrated Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04078)**  `arXiv:2605.04078`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Khouloud Saadi, Di Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation aims to transfer multi-step reasoning capabilities from large language models to smaller, efficient ones. While recent methods have shown promising gains, they typically rely on static teacher-student hierarchies and frame distillation as trajectory imitation. This is misaligned with the structure of reasoning, where intermediate steps are locally under-specified: global correctness constrains the final answer, but does not uniquely determine each intermediate move. We propose validity-calibrated reasoning distillation, a framework that treats reasoning distillation as a problem of local learning-signal allocation rather than path alignment. Instead of enforcing token-level imitation, we compare the student's and teacher's proposed next-step actions under the same prefix and use their relative local validity to modulate the strength of the distillation update. This yields a dynamic, context-dependent supervision that preserves the teacher's structural guidance while adapting update strength to local reasoning quality. Across math, code, and instruction-following benchmarks, our method outperforms strong distillation baselines. These results indicate that effective LLM reasoning distillation is governed not by rigid trajectory imitation, but by locally calibrated allocation of learning signal.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent LLMs for Adaptive Acquisition in Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28959)**  `arXiv:2603.28959`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammadsina Almasi, Andrea Carbonati, Hadis Anahideh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The exploration-exploitation trade-off is central to sequential decision-making and black-box optimization, yet how Large Language Models (LLMs) reason about and manage this trade-off remains poorly understood. Unlike Bayesian Optimization, where exploration and exploitation are explicitly encoded through acquisition functions, LLM-based optimization relies on implicit, prompt-based reasoning over historical evaluations, making search behavior difficult to analyze or control. In this work, we present a metric-level study of LLM-mediated search policy learning, studying how LLMs construct and adapt exploration-exploitation strategies under multiple operational definitions of exploration, including informativeness, diversity, and representativeness. We show that single-agent LLM approaches, which jointly perform strategy selection and candidate generation within a single prompt, suffer from cognitive overload, leading to unstable search dynamics and premature convergence. To address this limitation, we propose a multi-agent framework that decomposes exploration-exploitation control into strategic policy mediation and tactical candidate generation. A strategy agent assigns interpretable weights to multiple search criteria, while a generation agent produces candidates conditioned on the resulting search policy defined as weights. This decomposition renders exploration-exploitation decisions explicit, observable, and adjustable. Empirical results across various continuous optimization benchmarks indicate that separating strategic control from candidate generation substantially improves the effectiveness of LLM-mediated search.
  </details>

- **[Train at Moving Edge: Online-Verified Prompt Selection for Efficient RL Training of Large Reasoning Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25184)**  `arXiv:2603.25184`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahao Wu, Ning Lu, Shengcai Liu, Kun Wang, Yanting Yang, Baijiong Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become essential for post-training large language models (LLMs) in reasoning tasks. While scaling rollouts can stabilize training and enhance performance, the computational overhead is a critical issue. In algorithms like GRPO, multiple rollouts per prompt incur prohibitive costs, as a large portion of prompts provide negligible gradients and are thus of low utility. To address this problem, we investigate how to select high-utility prompts before the rollout phase. Our experimental analysis reveals that sample utility is non-uniform and evolving: the strongest learning signals concentrate at the ``learning edge", the intersection of intermediate difficulty and high uncertainty, which shifts as training proceeds. Motivated by this, we propose HIVE (History-Informed and online-VErified prompt selection), a dual-stage framework for data-efficient RL. HIVE utilizes historical reward trajectories for coarse selection and employs prompt entropy as a real-time proxy to prune instances with stale utility. By evaluating HIVE across multiple math reasoning benchmarks and models, we show that HIVE yields significant rollout efficiency without compromising performance.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Bayesian Optimization for Co-Optimizing LLM Training Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08351)**  `arXiv:2602.08351`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiliang Chen, Alfred Wei Lun Leong, Shao Yong Ong, Apivich Hemachandra, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Chuan-Sheng Foo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning an LLM to maximize performance on a downstream task requires finding the optimal data and model configuration. This is a black-box optimization problem that Bayesian optimization (BO) addresses by sequentially evaluating training configurations and using observed performance feedback to adaptively guide the search towards better configurations. However, directly applying BO to the joint data-model space suffers from two fundamental challenges: the prohibitive cost of full LLM training at each iteration, and the large number of BO iterations required for effective exploration due to the high problem dimensionality. This paper introduces JoBS, a BO-based approach that co-optimizes data and model configurations without requiring a full training run at every iteration. JoBS allocates an initial portion of the optimization budget to learn a scaling-law-inspired performance predictor that estimates fully trained LLM performance from only a small number of training steps. The remaining budget is then used to run BO with this predictor, eliminating the need for full training runs and enabling JoBS to explore significantly more data and model configurations within the same budget. We analyze JoBS's average regret and derive the optimal budget allocation between predictor learning and BO iterations. Empirically, JoBS outperforms independent data and model optimization methods and existing multi-fidelity BO baselines across a diverse set of downstream tasks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DolphinRouting: Cluster-Level Binary Routing for Disaggregated LLM Inference Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40292)**  `arXiv:2610.40292`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yishuo Yuan, Shaoyu Yang, Chunrong Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prefill-decode (PD) disaggregation isolates the two phases of large language model serving, but request-level path selection must account for prefill work, cache reuse, and decode demand. We present DolphinRouting, which learns this choice from end-to-end SLO feedback under a given P/D resource allocation. Requests are clustered by raw prompt length, cache-adjusted input lengths on both worker pools, and output-length information. Offline policy search learns one binary action per cluster. Online feature extraction, cluster assignment, and table lookup select local or remote prefill. We evaluate DolphinRouting on a real PD-disaggregated cluster using three public benchmarks, a mixed workload, and real data. Across the mixed workload and real data, DolphinRouting with $k$-means reduces P90 TTFT by 5.7--7.9\% and P90 TPOT by 2.6--10.1\% relative to Dynamo.
  </details>

- **[H$^2$R: Hierarchical Hint-Based Reward for Mathematical Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39934)**  `arXiv:2610.39934`  `cs.LG`  
  _He Tu, Siye Li, Hongyi Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards has shown great promise in enhancing mathematical reasoning in large language models. However, existing approaches provide limited intermediate supervision for complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose \textbf{Hierarchical Hint-based Reward}, a framework that leverages the intrinsic sub-question structure of mathematical problems as structured intermediate conditioning signals during RLVR training. Unlike chain-of-thought prompting, these hierarchical hints serve as logically grounded intermediate milestones that effectively reduce the reasoning horizon toward the final answer. To validate this framework, we introduce \textbf{HiMath}, a benchmark of hierarchically structured mathematical problems. Across diverse LLMs, hierarchical hints consistently improve reasoning performance even without task-specific training. When integrated into RL training via H$^2$R, they boost Qwen2.5-Math-7B accuracy on HiMath from 30.4\% to 39.1\% using hierarchical hints, and up to 45.6\% with Oracle Hierarchical Hints. This pronounced improvement demonstrates that incorporating reliable intermediate checkpoints into reward design is essential for sustaining complex mathematical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[TACFormer: Transformer-Based Generative Model for Target-Conditioned De Novo PROTAC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39891)**  `arXiv:2610.39891`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yossra Gharbi, Rocío Mercado_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Targeted protein degradation (TPD) represents a fundamental shift in drug discovery, moving beyond traditional target inhibition toward the event-driven removal of disease-causing proteins. A prominent strategy for TPD is the use of proteolysis-targeting chimeras (PROTACs), heterobifunctional molecules that selectively route a protein of interest (POI) to the ubiquitin-proteasome system for degradation. While PROTAC design still relies heavily on trial-and-error synthesis and experimental testing, deep generative models offer a promising means to explore PROTAC chemical space in silico and generate novel molecules. However, many existing generative methods formulate PROTAC design primarily as a linker-generation problem, with POI-binding (warhead) and E3-ligase-binding components fixed in advance. This restricts exploration of the full PROTAC chemical space and often leaves the POI implicit through the predefined warhead, rather than using protein-level information as an explicit conditioning signal during generation. To address these limitations, we propose TACFormer: a transformer-based generative model for target-aware de novo PROTAC design. The model conditions a GPT-style molecular decoder on pretrained ESM-2 representations of POI sequences, allowing POI-level information to steer full-molecule generation. Our results show that TACFormer generates highly novel and chemically diverse PROTACs while showing consistent POI-dependent shifts in generated chemical space. In particular, molecules generated under specific target conditions preferentially populate chemical neighborhoods associated with POIs in the held-out dataset, while extrapolating to novel chemotypes. By natively integrating biological context into the generative process, TACFormer moves PROTAC design toward a more biology-informed exploration of degrader chemical space.
  </details>

- **[DWISE: Denoising Workload-Informed Scheduling Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39852)**  `arXiv:2610.39852`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lie Li, Wen Li, Junxiao Shen, Jindong Gu, Guosheng Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike autoregressive large language models (AR-LLMs), diffusion LLMs (dLLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising many tokens in parallel, offering a promising path to efficient inference. Existing serving frameworks, however, schedule dLLM requests with policies designed for AR models, namely first-come-first-served (FCFS) admission and prefill priority. We find that both policies are ill-suited to dLLMs, wasting much of their speed. FCFS ignores that a dLLM request's decoding cost is highly heterogeneous and unknown at arrival, causing severe head-of-line blocking. Prefill priority, widely used in AR-LLM serving to keep time-to-first-token low, sharply cuts dLLM efficiency, as rounds that run only prompts leave most of the batch idle. We present DWISE, a Denoising Workload-Informed Scheduling Engine for efficient dLLM serving. DWISE schedules requests by their remaining work, measured in a block-level unit shared by prefill and denoising, so both phases share a round. To predict the unknown denoising workload, DWISE combines a lightweight prompt-based predictor at admission with a single refinement from the first denoising round's hidden states, which capture model-specific information absent from the prompt and require no additional model execution. Implemented in SGLang and evaluated on five models from three dLLM families under bursty workloads, DWISE reduces mean end-to-end latency over SGLang by 5.4$\times$ on average and up to 14.9$\times$ while preserving task accuracy. The code will be released publicly.
  </details>

- **[Activation Probes Are Reliable With a Handful of Positive Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39766)**  `arXiv:2610.39766`  `cs.LG`  
  _Riya Tyagi, Stefan Heimersheim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efforts to monitor advanced AI for rare misalignments face a data challenge: abundant aligned examples but only a handful of misaligned ones. We test activation probes in this "few vs. thousands" regime on spam and honesty detection tasks. For our tasks, training with many negative examples is on average more positive-sample-efficient than balanced training for small numbers (1-10) of positive samples. We also find that LLM upsampling can provide a performance boost equivalent to roughly doubling the number of real positive samples, though excessive upsampling hurts performance. Finally, we show a positive scaling trend, where larger models are more positive-sample-efficient to probe. Our findings suggest we should leverage the large number of negative samples available to amplify the signal from rare but critical misalignment examples.
  </details>

- **[LatentWeave: Test-Time Latent Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39557)**  `arXiv:2610.39557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fanci Meng, Kun Wang, Guibin Zhang, Xinlei Yu, Jiaming Zhang, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in test-time optimization have shown that the multimodal reasoning performance of large multimodal language models (MLLMs) can be improved during inference without modifying backbone model parameters. However, existing approaches primarily operate at the prompt or output level, leaving the latent reasoning process largely uncontrolled and insufficient to prevent error accumulation during autoregressive generation. To address this limitation, we propose LatentWeave, a test-time framework that treats multimodal reasoning as a controllable latent trajectory. LatentWeave performs structured Latent Weaving, progressively refining intermediate hidden states segment-wise to correct emerging deviations, and further introduces Reasoning Anchors via lightweight WEAVE tokens to stabilize reasoning structure and guide answer-oriented generation. These mechanisms are designed to enable fine-grained, step-aware intervention in the latent reasoning process while keeping the main MLLM backbone parameters fixed. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks report improvements over the evaluated baselines; the contribution of latent optimization, anchor training, and additional test-time computation is analyzed separately in the ablation and efficiency studies. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LinQuant: Functional Invariances for Low-Precision Linear Recurrences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39418)**  `arXiv:2610.39418`  `cs.LG`  
  _Neehal Tumma, Philipp Nazari, Rohan Cherukuri, Daniela Rus, T. Konstantin Rusch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear recurrences are emerging as practical alternatives to softmax attention in large language models, appearing in both standalone and hybrid architectures. However, despite their rapid emergence, low-precision inference remains much less developed for these models than for softmax attention. During prefill, chunkwise-parallel algorithms cast much of the recurrence as dense matrix multiplications, suggesting a natural path to low-precision execution. Simply quantizing these operations in their native form, however, can be fragile. In this work, we identify functional invariances that preserve the full-precision computation while making the operands more amenable to quantization. Building on this observation, we introduce LinQuant, a framework for low-precision recurrent prefill based on seven such invariances, including rotations, diagonal rescalings, and alternative placements of recurrent decay and write factors. We derive when these transformations remain valid across common recurrence families spanning linear, gated, and delta-rule updates, and show that they provide complementary degrees of freedom for improving low-precision accuracy. Finally, we translate this framework to production by augmenting SSDi8, an existing low-precision prefill pipeline for Mamba-2, with the applicable invariances. LinQuant improves perplexity at matched latency on Mamba-2 1.3B, 2.7B, and 8B and Nemotron-H 8B, demonstrating the practical utility of invariance-based quantization for recurrent models.
  </details>

- **[IMRouter: Imbalance-aware Temporal Pattern Routing for LLM-based Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39246)**  `arXiv:2610.39246`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Yang, Cai Xu, Haishun Chen, Ying Liu, Jie Zhao, Wei Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate time series forecasting (TSF) is essential for Web applications, such as traffic monitoring and online resource management. Recent advances in leveraging pre-trained large language models (LLMs) have shown promising performance in TSF. However, existing LLM-based TSF methods largely optimize the overall forecasting objective while overlooking temporal pattern imbalance (TPI), where different temporal patterns occur at highly unequal frequencies. Our empirical analysis reveals that TPI is prevalent across widely used TSF benchmarks, with substantially higher forecasting errors on less frequent patterns. To address this issue, we propose IMRouter (Imbalance-aware Temporal Pattern Routing), a novel framework that mitigates TPI-induced biases in both temporal representation learning and cross-modal alignment. Specifically, IMRouter introduces Pattern-Adaptive Temporal Representation Learning (PATR), which leverages gradient conflict and local gradient density to guide expert specialization. By penalizing incompatible expert assignments with density-aware weighting, PATR reduces the dominance of frequent optimization signals in router training. Furthermore, Routing-Guided Semantic Alignment (RGSA) transfers the pattern-specific knowledge learned by the router to cross-modal alignment through mutual information maximization, preserving distinctive temporal information across different patterns. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of IMRouter, particularly in improving forecasting performance on less frequent temporal patterns.
  </details>

- **[Two-Stage Clipping: Linear-Time Approximate and Exact Mixed-Norm Projections for Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38598)**  `arXiv:2610.38598`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michel Barlaud, Jean-Paul Marmorat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) based on Transformer architectures have achieved remarkable performance across artificial intelligence tasks, but their computational cost remains a major bottleneck. Structured sparsification provides a promising approach to reducing this cost. In particular, $\ell_{1,\infty}$ projections can induce column-wise sparsity in model matrices, but existing proximal methods (Prox) typically require $\mathcal{O}(nm\log(nm))$ operations for matrices in $\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}$, limiting their computational and energy efficiency.In this paper, we introduce Two-Stage Clipping (TSC), an efficient structured sparsification operator inspired by bilevel optimization. Rather than solving a bilevel optimization problem in the strict sense, TSC decomposes the computation into two successive stages.
