First, group-level quantities are projected to allocate the global sparsity budget and identify active groups. Second, the resulting values are used as group-wise clipping thresholds for the matrix coefficients. For square attention matrices ($n \times n$), the complexity of our TSC method is $O(n^2)$, which is linear in the number of matrix coefficients.We establish a theoretical bound on the discrepancy between TSC and the exact proximal projection and show on synthetic data that the additional Euclidean approximation cost remains small in practice. Extensive benchmarks demonstrate substantial runtime improvements over proximal baselines and validate the theoretical norm identities underlying the proposed construction.
  </details>

- **[Learning a Prior-Data Fitted Network for Graph Foundation Models via Structural Causal Model Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38335)**  `arXiv:2610.38335`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwei Zhang, Tianyin Liao, Chunyu Hu, Ge Lan, Nuo Xu, Yi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for learning transferable representations across different graphs and domains. However, existing GFMs face notable limitations, including limited pre-training scale or reliance on aligning with textual information in Large Language Models (LLMs). To overcome these limitations, recent studies have drawn inspiration from prior-data fitted networks (PFNs), which learn from large synthetic datasets and support multi-domain prediction backed by causal learning. However, existing graph PFNs encounter severe difficulties in modeling causal relationships within and across node and edge sets, generating diverse graph structures, and designing suitable architectures that can learn from complex synthetic datasets. To address these challenges, we propose **GSCM-PFN** (Graph SCM-based Prior-Data Fitted Network), a graph PFN pre-trained on synthetic data generated from an SCM-based prior that jointly models nodes and graph topology. Specifically, we (1) design a joint feature-edge-label structural causal model (SCM) to guide synthetic graph generation and capture causal relationships within and across node and edge sets; (2) introduce prior-guided node-edge dependency modeling to instantiate diverse graph generation strategies, and prove that our SCM-based generation prior is more expressive than existing priors; and (3) propose a feature-permutation-invariant dual-path graph network with theoretical guarantees for learning from diverse synthetic graphs. Experiments on 23 real-world benchmarks show that GSCM-PFN outperforms both conventional GFMs and PFN-based GFMs, without utilizing real-world graph data for pre-training. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TGTV: An Interpretable Framework for UAV Fault Detection and Traceability via Spatiotemporal Graph Learning and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38106)**  `arXiv:2610.38106`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zixuan Wang, Changbo Shi, Ji Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fixed-wing unmanned aerial vehicles experience operating-condition transitions, asynchronous sensor sampling, and control-system compensation that can obscure fault signatures. Existing temporal detection methods have limited capacity to represent coupled faults across subsystems, track their evolution, and constrain language-model diagnoses with quantitative evidence. This paper proposes TGTV, an interpretable fault detection and traceability framework that integrates spatiotemporal graph learning with a large language model. A temporal convolutional network and a graph attention network with weighted fused adjacency extract multiscale temporal patterns and spatial dependencies among sensors. A hybrid temporal Transformer and variational autoencoder then evaluates window-level anomalies using prediction errors and reconstruction probabilities. Continuous-time Shapley attribution identifies critical sensors and high-contribution intervals while preserving the temporal continuity of fault evolution. A structured evidence package combines detection results, graph relationships, temporal attribution, sensor attribution, and operating context. An evidence-constrained diagnosis protocol uses this package to generate flight-level conclusions with traceable evidence identifiers. Cross-flight holdout experiments on the public ALFA fixed-wing flight dataset yield a window-level area under the precision-recall curve of 0.976, accuracy of 0.973, recall of 0.902, and F1 score of 0.920. Flight-level fault diagnosis achieves recall of 0.943 and an F1 score of 0.944. These results demonstrate accurate anomaly detection and quantitatively supported fault-tracing cues, providing a basis for interpretable post-flight fault review and analysis.
  </details>

- **[Preserving Safety During Sequential Fine-Tuning: A Factorial and Geometric Analysis of Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38001)**  `arXiv:2610.38001`  `cs.LG`  
  _George Ruellan, Diana Benavides-Prado_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benign fine-tuning can erode safety alignment in large language models, and sequential adaptation substantially compounds this risk. Prior work mitigates this erosion by replaying harmful-prompt refusals and benign-compliance examples, while other work instead matches base-model logits via distillation. However, the individual contributions of supervised exposure and distillation to safety behavior and refusal geometry during sequential adaptation remain unclear. We factor these two strategies apart and study their behavioral and geometric effects during sequential LoRA fine-tuning of Mistral-7B and Llama-3-8B across diverse task sequences (code generation, German-language summarization, and science question-answering). Behaviorally, supervised replay exposure yields the largest reduction in HarmBench attack success rate (ASR) on Mistral, averaging 51.1 points below base, but at a substantial over-refusal cost, averaging 53.4 points above base on OR-Bench-hard. Logit distillation, without any supervised replay loss, largely prevents safety erosion at a fraction of this cost: an average 5.4-point ASR reduction against only a 5.1-point rise in over-refusal. Llama shows the same asymmetry between the two strategies. Combining exposure and distillation moderates exposure's over-refusal while leaving downstream task scores largely unaffected. Geometrically, the two models diverge: on Mistral, distillation preserves the base refusal direction more closely than exposure does, whereas on Llama, refusal behavior after exposure largely survives ablation of the extracted refusal direction. This dissociation between models indicates that preserving the base refusal direction is not a reliable proxy for behavioral safety.
  </details>

- **[Improving Data-Constrained Scaling with Hyperparameter Transfer and Ensembling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37842)**  `arXiv:2610.37842`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ethan Baron, Shmuel Berman, Anirudh Satheesh, Akshay Vegesna, Samip Dahal, Andrew Gordon Wilson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As growing compute budgets outpace the growth of high-quality training data, large language models must be trained with both compute and data constraints in mind. Unfortunately, existing prescriptions for data-constrained scaling suffer from two important limitations. First, past works hold key hyperparameters such as weight decay fixed with scale, despite the fact that optimal hyperparameters vary wildly with model size and number of epochs. Second, past works neglect ensembling, a promising axis for scaling under data constraints. As a surprising consequence, prior prescriptions can lead to loss that increases with compute budget. We address both limitations by fitting power laws that prescribe how to transfer hyperparameters with scale and scaling laws that prescribe how to allocate compute with ensembling. Training on 100M tokens from FineWeb, our recipe reduces validation loss by over 0.15 nats compared to prior recipes, a 14% decrease in perplexity, and achieves monotonic improvements with compute through ensembling. These gains are achieved using prescriptions fit to smaller runs and validated at up to 12$\times$ larger compute budgets.
  </details>

- **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Stateful Streaming Inference for Multimodal LLMs on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309)**  `arXiv:2610.35309`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yilong Li, Chengpo Yan, Yijing Zeng, Hanlei Wu, Mingjun Song, Suman Banerjee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge SoCs combine CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, yet runtimes for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) keep generation on one engine and leave others idle. Streaming workloads allow ingestion and answering to overlap, but backend-local KV state binds queries to the engine that built their history. Stage performance and co-running interference vary, so no single placement wins across SoCs and workloads. \name separates logical history from engine-specific KV state so any language engine can continue a stream. Stateful Streaming Generation overlaps ingestion and answering, while Concurrent Sequence Generation serves ready queries in parallel. Phase-level state continuation lets one engine prefill a query and another decode the answer, freeing the first to resume ingestion. State-Driven Execution Planning (SDEP) chooses paths by predicted completion time and sets each engine's share of queries using arrival rates and measured service times. Across M4 Max, RK3588, and QCS6490, \name beats the strongest single-engine baselines, even when they reuse context. On M4 Max, SDEP cuts live-stream p95 time to first token by $76\%$, with every first token within one second, and raises throughput under backlog by up to $35\%$ over Metal-only execution with context reuse. On two RK3588 streams, SDEP delivers $19.6\%$ higher request throughput than the best single engine with context reuse and $27.2\%$ more than parallel engines without shared state. Stateful Streaming Generation reaches up to $2.48\times$ serial throughput on the same engines. Every answer matches its serial reference.
  </details>

- **[Delta-Matching: Closing the Final Gap of Native 8-bit Training for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37852)**  `arXiv:2609.37852`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhan Tang, Hao Kang, Han Cai, Song Han, Chenyan Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable FP8 attention remains a barrier to fully native 8-bit large language model training. We derive how forward-backward inconsistencies produce stale delta and empirically show how it distorts training dynamics. Our stale-delta hybrid runs show a modest loss gap at 569M parameters but substantial loss increases and downstream degradation at 1.67B and 5.29B. QK normalization, NoPE (no positional encoding), and lower-learning-rate context extension mitigate or delay degradation without eliminating it. This pattern suggests accumulated optimization error that smaller models and short runs can conceal. We propose Delta-Matching, proving that it restores the softmax gradient's zero-row-sum invariant under the stated numerical assumptions. It enables native block-scaled FP8 in every forward and backward attention-core matmul without architectural changes, smaller global batches, or auxiliary forward outputs. Across tested architectures, scales, and training stages, Delta-Matching matches BF16/FP32 mixed-precision training loss and overall downstream performance. We will release our implementation, trained models, and data recipes (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Towards Better Training Signal: Advantage Clipped Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36816)**  `arXiv:2609.36816`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruichuan Huang, Jinghan Liu, Congliang Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a cornerstone for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), but the need for on-policy data substantially limits training efficiency. Reusing off-policy data through importance sampling (IS) can improve efficiency but introduce considerable instability. Hence, algorithms such as PPO and GRPO widely adopt IS-ratio clipping to stabilize training. However, training stability and gradient estimate are mainly determined by the product of IS ratio and advantage. To further stabilize training, we propose ACPO, which clips the product of the IS ratio and the advantage, leading to more stable gradient estimates. We also establish a connection between ACPO and gradient clipping in policy mirror descent (PMD), which is a standard technique to stabilize optimization process, and prove the convergence of clipped-PMD under the standard RL setting. Experiments on widely used mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that ACPO consistently outperforms PPO and GRPO in both accuracy and training efficiency, delivering 4-6 percentage points gains on standard math benchmarks, with Qwen3-8B+PPO. Hence, ACPO is a practical and effective alternative to conventional IS-ratio clipping for RL post-training of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[ScAn-Bench: Evaluating Scaling Analysis Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35707)**  `arXiv:2609.35707`  `cs.LG`  
  _Artin Sermaxhaj, Nastaran Alipour, Donat Sinani, Johannes Hog, Neeratyoy Mallik, Steven Adriaensen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent progress in machine learning is driven by large-scale foundation models, where scaling laws and finding optimal scaling prescriptions for architecture, data, and hyperparameters are key in advancing the state-of-the-art. Therefore, it is surprising that no systematic study evaluates the methodology to obtain scaling laws and prescriptions across different model types. To shed light on this crucial blind spot and facilitate future research, we introduce the surrogate benchmarks ScAn-Bench-LLM and ScAn-Bench-VLM based on 4524 and 8024 checkpoints of language and vision-language model pipelines. On our benchmarks, we perform the first systematic evaluation of both data acquisition and extrapolation methodology for scaling analysis across different data modalities.
  </details>

- **[Dialectics of Alignment: Harnessing Unsafe Knowledge for Dynamic Safety Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00686)**  `arXiv:2606.00686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maryam Hashemzadeh, Jerry Huang, Minseon Kim, Marc-Alexandre Côté, Sarath Chandar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The prevailing paradigm in large language model (LLM) alignment operates via erasure, filtering unsafe data or training models to strictly refuse harmful prompts. While effective at reducing immediate toxicity, this approach fundamentally constricts the model's epistemological scope, resulting in over-cautious systems that output uninformative blanket refusals to sensitive yet benign queries. In this work, we challenge the orthodoxy that unsafe data must be discarded. We propose a dialectical approach to alignment, positing that unsafe data encodes rich, domain-specific knowledge critical for nuanced, safe, and informative generation. To operationalize this, we introduce SafeMoE, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework that isolates unsafe knowledge into domain-specific Low-Rank Adapters (LoRA experts) trained exclusively on harmful corpora. To synthesize safety from these unsafe primitives, we train a lightweight gating network using a minimal, highly curated set of safe-informative responses (as few as 100 samples per domain). During inference, this router dynamically orchestrates the unsafe experts, effectively steering the generation trajectory toward safe outcomes while harnessing the experts' deep domain knowledge. Extensive empirical evaluations across stringent safety benchmarks demonstrate that SafeMoE is not only safer, achieving over a 20% relative improvement in safe response rate over the strongest baseline (RealSafe-R1 on PKU-SafeRLHF; more than a 15% absolute gain), but also produces more informative responses when safety and harmfulness are of paramount concern. Furthermore, the routing mechanism exhibits strong zero-shot generalization to unseen domains and broader safety tasks without domain-specific supervision. Our findings suggest a paradigm shift in alignment: true safety requires not the masking of unsafe knowledge, but its controlled integration.
  </details>

- **[Where to Spend Rollouts: Hit-Utility Optimal Rollout Allocation for Group-Based RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07114)**  `arXiv:2605.07114`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tao Wang, Shuo Li, Yan Sun, Dongsheng Ding, Edgar Dobriban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a central paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models. Group-based policy optimization methods, such as GRPO, typically allocate a fixed number of rollouts to every prompt. This uniform allocation can be inefficient: it over-allocates compute to prompts whose sampled groups are already saturated while under-exploring prompts for which additional samples may reveal useful correct trajectories. To address this limitation, we introduce hit utility, the posterior probability that at least one rollout in a proposed additional allocation for a prompt will be correct. Building on this notion, we propose Hit-Utility Optimal Rollout Allocation (HORA), a learning-free rollout allocation policy that maximizes total posterior hit utility within each allocation batch. HORA adaptively reallocates rollout budgets while leaving the downstream reward evaluation and group-based advantage estimator unchanged. Across two training sets, four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, and three model families at multiple scales, HORA preserves comparable Pass@1 and improves Pass@K over compute-matched GRPO, and it outperforms related allocation baselines at large K. It is also drop-in compatible with other group-based estimators such as RLOO. Ablation studies indicate that the uniform prior used by HORA is competitive with five prompt-conditioned learned-prior alternatives.
  </details>

- **[Stable but Wrong: When Learning Stabilizes Away from the Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21491)**  `arXiv:2603.21491`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhipeng Zhang, Fujie Ren, Kai Li, Bo Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stable training is often treated as evidence that learning is succeeding, but stability characterizes optimization behavior rather than correctness relative to an external objective. We study what happens when the signal being optimized remains persistently biased. We define Stable but Wrong (SBW) as a learning state in which optimization remains active or stabilizes while the learned outcome stays systematically displaced from an independently defined objective. A minimal theoretical model shows that persistent systematic bias can separate a stable convergence point from the true objective even under standard conditions for stable convergence. We then introduce static and dynamic bias in reinforcement learning, supervised learning, and continual fine-tuning of large language models. Across these settings, optimization of the current learning signal continues or stabilizes while correctness relative to the external objective declines or remains displaced. Finally, two controlled challenge-correction experiments with external ground truth test whether a prespecified error can be exposed and changed. In controlled scientific discovery and CIFAR-10, targeted evidence corrects or reduces established errors more effectively than equal-budget general evidence, with no evident harm to paired correct states under the tested protocols. These experiments do not provide a general method for automatically discovering unknown errors. Overall, SBW identifies a basic limit of convergence and other internal optimization metrics as evidence of correctness in systems that learn through persistent bias and feedback.
  </details>

- **[Powering Up Zeroth-Order Training via Subspace Gradient Orthogonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17155)**  `arXiv:2602.17155`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yicheng Lang, Changsheng Wang, Yihua Zhang, Mingyi Hong, Zheng Zhang, Wotao Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization provides a gradient-free alternative to first-order (FO) methods by estimating gradients via finite differences of function evaluations, and has recently emerged as a memory-efficient paradigm for fine-tuning large-scale models by avoiding backpropagation. However, ZO optimization has a fundamental tension between accuracy and query efficiency. In this work, we show that ZO optimization can be substantially improved by unifying two complementary principles: (i) a projection-based subspace view that reduces gradient estimation variance by exploiting the intrinsic low-rank structure of model updates, and (ii) Muon-style spectral optimization that applies gradient orthogonalization to extract informative spectral structure from noisy ZO gradients. These findings form a unified framework of subspace gradient orthogonalization, which we instantiate in a new method, ZO-Muon, admitting a natural interpretation as a low-rank Muon optimizer in the ZO setting. Extensive experiments on large language models (LLMs) and vision transformers (ViTs) demonstrate that ZO-Muon significantly accelerates convergence and achieves a win-win improvement in accuracy and query/runtime efficiency. Notably, compared to the popular MeZO baseline, ZO-Muon requires only 24.7% of the queries to reach the same SST-2 performance for LLM fine-tuning, and improves accuracy by 25.1% on ViT-L fine-tuning on CIFAR-100.
  </details>

- **[Learning Where It Matters: Geometric Anchoring for Robust Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04909)**  `arXiv:2602.04909`  `cs.LG`  
  _Youngjae Cho, Jongsuk Kim, Ji-Hoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and related methods align large language models from pairwise preferences by regularizing updates against a fixed reference policy. As the policy drifts, a static reference, however, can become increasingly miscalibrated, leading to distributional mismatch and amplifying spurious preference signals under noisy supervision. Conversely, reference-free variants avoid mismatch but often suffer from unconstrained reward drift. We propose Geometric Anchor Preference Optimization (GAPO), which replaces the fixed reference with a dynamic, geometry-aware anchor: an adversarial local perturbation of the current policy within a small radius that serves as a pessimistic baseline. This anchor enables an adaptive reweighting mechanism, modulating the importance of each preference pair based on its local sensitivity. We further introduce the Anchor Gap, the reward discrepancy between the policy and its anchor, and show under smoothness conditions that it approximates worst-case local margin degradation. Optimizing a logistic objective weighted by this gap downweights geometrically brittle instances while emphasizing robust preference signals. Across diverse noise settings, GAPO consistently improves robustness while matching or improving performance on standard LLM alignment and reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[In-Place Feedback: Reliable Refinement for Multi-Turn Expert-LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00777)**  `arXiv:2510.00777`  `cs.LG`  
  _Youngbin Choi, Minjong Lee, Saemi Moon, Seunghyuk Cho, Chaehyeon Chung, MoonJeong Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-generated drafts often contain subtle factual or logical errors, yet prior work shows that models struggle to reliably integrate multi-turn feedback aimed at fixing them. We propose in-place feedback, an interaction paradigm in which the user directly edits the model's previous response and the model continues generation from the edited context. In-place feedback consistently outperforms standard multi-turn feedback across five reasoning-intensive benchmarks while requiring fewer tokens, and our fine-grained analysis shows that it applies corrections more reliably and propagates them to subsequent reasoning. A user study with domain experts refining LLM-generated summaries corroborates these findings: participants report higher final-output satisfaction and substantially lower fatigue with in-place feedback, and a mixed strategy combining in-place and multi-turn feedback improves every dimension we measure. These results suggest that editing errors in place is a more reliable way to incorporate expert corrections than multi-turn feedback, while combining the two acts complementarily to form a more effective collaboration than either mechanism alone.
  </details>

- **[Unlocking Any-Order Generation in Pretrained Autoregressive Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39293)**  `arXiv:2610.39293`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Rishav Pramanik, Marco Pedersoli, Zhaozheng Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) image generators are powerful in principle but inflexible in practice: training with a fixed raster-scan order hardcodes a single factorization of the image distribution into the model, leaving it unable to inpaint, outpaint, or edit at inference time. Existing solutions, including the modern discrete-diffusion family, train from scratch on a mixture of token orderings, paying roughly 3x the compute of standard raster-scan training; discrete diffusion further sacrifices compatibility with the AR inference ecosystem (KV caching, vLLM) by construction. We argue that this cost is unnecessary. A pretrained raster-scan AR model has already learned the joint distribution over image tokens; what it lacks is not knowledge but the ability to express that knowledge through non-sequential conditional pathways. We propose a recipe for adapting pretrained raster-scan models into any-order generators using two components: future-aware positional embeddings that resolve a target-position identifiability problem at negligible overhead, and an ordering curriculum that traces a stable optimization path from the pretrained solution to a good any-order basin. Applied to four base models across two architectures and two scales (LlamaGen-L/XL, RAR-L/XL), the recipe substantially outperforms MaskGIT on ImageNet inpainting (FID 6.21 vs. 10.44) and outpainting, is competitive with MAR-L, trades a 1.2-1.9 increase in class-conditional FID for this flexibility, and costs 13-33% additional compute beyond pretraining, while leaving the standard AR inference stack untouched. Inpainting, outpainting, and editing, our results suggest, need not be added to autoregressive image models. They can be unlocked from the ones that exist. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Toward Autonomous Laboratory Safety Monitoring with Vision Language Models: Learning to See Hazards Through Scene Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00414)**  `arXiv:2602.00414`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Trishna Chakraborty, Udita Ghosh, Aldair Ernesto Gongora, Ruben Glatt, Yue Dong, Jiachen Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Laboratories are prone to severe injuries from unsafe actions, yet continuous manual safety monitoring, beyond mandatory pre-lab safety training, is infeasible. Vision-language models (VLMs) offer promise for autonomous laboratory safety monitoring, but their effectiveness in realistic settings is unclear due to the lack of visual evaluation data. Safety incidents are mostly documented as text, and existing studies are largely limited to textual understanding. Since hazardous scenes are unsafe to stage or capture, synthetic visual data becomes the only practical way to study this problem. This motivates us to introduce a structured data generation pipeline that converts textual laboratory scenarios into aligned quadruples using LLM as scene graph architect and image generation model as renderer, yielding \dataset: a synthetic dataset of 1,207 $\langle$image, scene graph, ground truth, hazard relevance mask$\rangle$ quadruples across 362 unique scenarios. Experiments on \dataset and seven open- and closed-source models show that VLMs perform effectively given a textual scene graph but degrade substantially in visual-only settings (i.e., where no scene graph is involved), indicating difficulty in extracting structured object relationships directly from pixels. We thus propose \saga, a post-training framework that leverages reinforcement learning to first structure the visual scene as a scene graph, and then predict hazards while aligning attention with relevant image regions. Evaluations with Qwen 3.5 and Gemma 4 show that our method nearly doubles the $F_1$ score over the baseline on our synthetic dataset and achieves an improvement of $\sim 3\times$ on a mini real-world dataset, while preserving general visual understanding in visual question answering.
  </details>

- **[It Takes Two to Probe, Thousands to Retrieve: Putting Vision--Language Probes to the Retrieval Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40124)**  `arXiv:2610.40124`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Ivana Beňová, Michal Gregor, Marián Šimko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--language models (VLMs) are tested for fine-grained understanding with image--text matching probes, a model must prefer the correct image or caption over handcrafted negative. In retrieval, the correct item must beat thousands of alternatives. We measure how these two evaluation methods relate by using two probing benchmarks, \svo{} and SugarCrepe, for retrieval tasks. For 100 image and caption queries we judge the top-10 retrievals from full dataset of six VLMs, with human annotators and an LLM assessor. We than compare how well models rank pool of items on the same queries, while changing only where the negatives comes from: the benchmark, a random draw, or the judged-incorrect retrievals of other models. On \svo{} the accuracy of retrieving a positive over handcrafted negatives is significantly higher than for retrieved ones (the correct item is preferred in 85\% against 77\% of the time). Using pool of ten retrieved negatives leads to same accuracy as when one uses pool of 200 to 430 random negatives. On SugarCrepe the order is reversed: its handcrafted negatives are harder than the retrieved ones. Incomplete relevance labels explain most of the low retrieval scores on \svo{}, where the top-ranked item is correct for 54--90\% of the queries under human labels against 6--21\% under the benchmark's labels. However, we detect no association between passing the probe for a query and retrieving a correct item first. The LLM assessor o3 agrees with individual annotators ($\kappa$ 0.57--0.61) slightly less than they agree with each other (0.64--0.68).
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing the Reference Gap: What Visual Scaffolds Can and Cannot Fix in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39419)**  `arXiv:2610.39419`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Guizhen Chen, Weiwen Xu, Wotao Yin, Anh Tuan Luu, Hou Pong Chan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models can recognize visual content yet struggle to reason reliably over it across multiple steps. In counting, a model may see all objects but lose track of which ones it has counted. We argue that such failures, commonly attributed to perception, reveal a reference gap: the failure to bind language to the relevant visual entities, regions, or locations. Motivated by the theory of visual routines, we test whether external references such as numbering can narrow this gap. We introduce ReGap-Bench, a paired benchmark of five visual reasoning tasks in which the scene, question, and answer are fixed and only the reference varies. Results show that task-matched references improve accuracy by up to 25\%, while mismatched references provide little benefit or even hurt performance. These findings identify the reference gap as a bottleneck distinct from visual perception and show that constructing task-appropriate visual references can serve as a training-free visual skill for improving multimodal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[AskStream: When and What to Ask Proactively under Uncertainty in Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38678)**  `arXiv:2610.38678`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Lu Wang, Zhuoran Jin, Longxiang Wang, Junhong Chen, Yupu Hao, Yao Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal large language models have increased interest in interaction paradigms for video understanding. The evolution from offline to streaming and proactive interaction has gradually shifted models from passive responders toward more proactive agents that can decide when to answer. However, existing proactive paradigms typically assume that the information required to answer a query is complete, leaving models to only wait or answer. In the real world, however, information is often incomplete due to uncertainty from either the video or the user. In proactive paradigms, such uncertainty can cause the model to wait forever or generate highly hallucinatory responses. We therefore argue that proactive interaction should be redefined with a third action: ask, allowing models to actively ask for missing information. To study when and what models should ask before answering, we introduce AskStream, the first multi-turn benchmark spanning video and user uncertainty for streaming video understanding, with four task categories and eight subtasks. It contains 438 videos with 2,714 manually annotated frame-level time windows across three types: wait, ask, and answer. Across both multiple-choice and open-ended settings, even the strongest model attains only 52.18% on our proposed Ask-F1 metric, highlighting substantial limitations in proactive information seeking under uncertainty.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Primitive Consistency in Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38623)**  `arXiv:2610.38623`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Xu Liu, Guikun Chen, Kanzhi Wu, Wenguan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jev enables efficient decision-making with large language models (LLMs) through typed querying primitives, allowing the same semantic state to be expressed in different formats, such as binary and multiple-choice queries. However, the consistency of decisions across primitives has not been systematically evaluated. This work introduces PrimaCons, a four-axis benchmark for assessing cross-primitive consistency while preserving the semantics of the query. Evaluations across three Jev sizes reveal consistency violations across all four axes. Our analysis shows that these violations follow systematic patterns and that cross-primitive discrepancies predict decision failure with a ROC-AUC of 0.91. To mitigate these inconsistencies, we propose the Cross-Primitive Residual Calibrator (CPRC), which attaches a lightweight residual network to a frozen backbone and jointly optimizes predictive accuracy and cross-primitive consistency. Under a leave-one-domain-out protocol, CPRC reduces cross-primitive inconsistency by 58.57% and improves accuracy by +2.97%., demonstrating its effectiveness in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings. Our findings establish cross-primitive consistency as a key dimension for evaluating Jev and provide a practical approach to improving the reliability of its decisions. Code and benchmark is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRISM-BENCH: Diagnosing Grounded and Faithful Multimodal Reasoning Beyond Final-Answer Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36554)**  `arXiv:2610.36554`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Vansh Bhatia, Mehdi Rezagholizadeh, Parsa Ashrafi Fashi, Igor Gilitschenski, Vikram Appia, Emad Barsoum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Final-answer accuracy reports whether a vision-language model was right, but not whether its answer was grounded in the image, whether its stated reasoning reflects that evidence, or what the answer cost to produce. Those are the properties that decide whether a system can be deployed. We present PRISM-BENCH, a benchmark of 1,000 expert-curated questions spanning five under-represented reasoning categories, in which every item carries an idealized reasoning trace written by one human annotator and validated by another. The traces support two diagnostics that accuracy cannot provide. A perception probe, in which the model answers from a description it wrote itself, attributes roughly 85% of frontier-model failures to misperception and places the bottleneck at grounding. Trace alignment predicts answer correctness for all 25 systems tested (macro AUROC 0.66) and survives length control, so it measures reasoning content and not verbosity. We further introduce CORE, an efficiency-aware metric that reorders 24.2% of model pairs relative to accuracy while remaining uncorrelated with response length. Across 25 open and closed systems, including GPT-5 and Claude Opus 4.6, the best accuracy is 66.0%. On the four categories with a human baseline the best system trails humans by 18.4 points, and recent frontier gains fall on recognition and not on spatial grounding.
  </details>

- **[MER-Bench: Benchmarking Multimodal Emotional Reappraisal for Image-Text Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15020)**  `arXiv:2603.15020`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqi Nie, Fei Wang, Junjie Chen, Kun Li, Yudi Cai, Dan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotional Reappraisal (MER) aims to shift affective interpretation by rewriting and editing a situation without altering its underlying semantic context. Despite advances in controllable editing and sentiment transfer, MER remains challenging under coupled multimodal constraints, especially in internet memes where images, embedded text, and layout jointly encode affective meaning. To bridge this gap, we present MER-Bench, a systematic benchmark for MER on image-text memes, containing over 3,000 real-world memes with fine-grained annotations for emotion transformation and structure-preserving multimodal editing. MER-Bench spans diverse visual, textual, and structural settings and is paired with a structured MLLM-as-a-Judge evaluation suite, comprising eight fine-grained metrics and three aggregated objectives for emotional alignment, content fidelity, and transformation strength. Extensive evaluation of 14 advanced multimodal models reveals that: (1) current models struggle to jointly balance affect, semantics, structure, and text; (2) strong visual editing does not imply effective MER, with poor embedded text rendering and affect-aware rewriting remaining key bottlenecks. Our results identify MER as a critical capability gap in current multimodal generation systems and highlight affective image-text reasoning as a key challenge for future work. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Radiology Guideline-derived Decision Graph for Efficient Clinical Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40327)**  `arXiv:2610.40327`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xuewen Zhou, Zhen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiological risk stratification and management planning are essential downstream tasks in medical imaging. Automating these tasks is challenging because clinical guideline criteria are distributed across prose, tables, and cross-referenced definitions. We present Guideline-derived Decision Graph (GDGraph), a framework that decouples guideline compilation from case evaluation. During compilation, a large language model partitions guidelines into section-based chunks, extracts local decision subgraphs with linked definitions, and aggregates related branches into an executable decision graph. During inference, a fast System One model answers localized clinical questions while deterministic traversal resolves Boolean logic, turning opaque single-pass predictions into a traceable decision path without task-specific training. Across 347 cases spanning renal cyst, adnexal mass, and thyroid nodule guidelines, GDGraph improves System One accuracy from 57.3% to 90.8%, outperforming retrieval-augmented GPT-4o (80.1%) and graph-guided GPT-4o with chain-of-thought (82.7%) at substantially lower latency and cost. The source code will be available.
  </details>

- **[MarineLife-16K: An Attribute-Rich Video-Text Dataset for Motion-Aware Marine Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33478)**  `arXiv:2610.33478`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Quang-Trung Truong, Thanh-Nghia Nguyen, Phu-Phong Vo, Jaeyeon Kim, Cheng Chen, Jie Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Marine video understanding is crucial for a wide range of real-world applications. However, existing marine video datasets suffer from limitations, including the limited number of data samples, fine-grained text descriptions, and detailed visual attributes. To address these limitations, we introduce a large-scale, video-text dataset featuring rich, in-the-wild visual attributes tailored for complex marine environments. Our dataset comprises 2,000 video-text pairs annotated with 13 attributes covering up to four objects per scene, object visual/geometric properties, behaviors, environmental context, and camera motion, spanning diverse lighting conditions, times of day, and 7 countries. From these annotations, we generate 16,080 video-question pairs (12,080 multiple-choice, 4,000 open-ended). Furthermore, we benchmark both proprietary and open-source video-LLM models on video question answering and video captioning tasks. We find that (i) proprietary models often achieves superior performances; (ii) VLMs handle object motion well but struggle with complex camera motion; (iii) closed-source models struggle to perceive camera motion; and (iv) VLMs struggle to reason in static cameras.
  </details>

- **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005)**  `arXiv:2610.24005`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Lei Tian, Chunzheng Zhu, Kai Wu, Yifan Zhang, Haihua Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.
  </details>

- **[MedGemma vs GPT-4: Open-Source and Proprietary Zero-shot Medical Disease Classification from Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23304)**  `arXiv:2512.23304`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Md. Sazzadul Islam Prottasha, Nabil Walid Rafi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (LLMs) introduce an emerging paradigm for medical imaging by interpreting scans through the lens of extensive clinical knowledge, offering a transformative approach to disease classification. This study presents a critical comparison between two fundamentally different AI architectures: the specialized open-source agent MedGemma and the proprietary large multimodal model GPT-4 for diagnosing six different diseases. The MedGemma-4b-it model, fine-tuned using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), demonstrated superior diagnostic capability by achieving a mean test accuracy of 80.37% compared to 69.56% for the untuned GPT-4. Furthermore, MedGemma exhibited notably higher sensitivity in high-stakes clinical tasks, such as cancer and pneumonia detection. Quantitative analysis via confusion matrices and classification reports provides comprehensive insights into model performance across all categories. These results emphasize that domain-specific fine-tuning is essential for minimizing hallucinations in clinical implementation, positioning MedGemma as a sophisticated tool for complex, evidence-based medical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[PhysCount: Do MLLMs Count Visual Appearances or Physical Entities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40530)**  `arXiv:2610.40530`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lidong Lu, Jiaxin Lv, Liangrui Dong, Xinyi Mao, Lidong Zou, Zhenquan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Images reveal appearances, but counting often asks about physical objects. A single entity may be visible directly, in several reflections, or through glass, so detecting every visible occurrence does not determine how many entities are present. Existing visual counting benchmarks rarely separate these two operations. We introduce PhysCount, a benchmark for evaluating whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can distinguish what appears in an image from what physically exists in the scene. PhysCount contains 546 real-world images and 4,595 human-written questions organized into 651 target-specific groups. For each group, annotators locate the relevant appearances, label how each is observed, and identify appearances of the same entity. Four complementary question families test the enumeration of appearances, the enumeration of physical entities, image-formation-specific counting, and appearance--entity correspondence. We measure both accuracy on each family and the proportion of groups answered entirely correctly. Across 40 MLLMs, counting visible appearances is substantially easier than recovering the identities that connect them. Even the strongest model answers every question correctly in only 13.36\% of groups. Performance declines when one entity produces many appearances or when its appearances cross observation modes. Higher image resolution improves access to visual evidence but brings limited gains in complete-group accuracy, whereas answering related questions jointly produces a marked improvement. These results expose a gap between recognizing what is visible and correctly recovering the physical entities behind those observations.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking color judgment in multimodal LLMs with CIE-computable ground truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40212)**  `arXiv:2610.40212`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuwei Yue, Kaida Xiao, Zhihua Wang, Qiao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are now used as zero-shot judges of image quality, but their color judgments are rarely tested with colorimetric ground truth. We present a five-task benchmark based on the CIE colorimetric framework: AWB pair judgment, skin-tone bias direction, CCT estimation, CIE Delta E 2000 pair ordering, and color naming under changing illuminants. We test eight MLLM endpoints, including four Qwen3-VL variants, GLM-4.5V, GPT-5.4, Claude-Opus-4.7, and Gemini-3.1-Pro. The models often solve coarse binary AWB judgments, but they remain weak on numeric CCT estimation and on some fine color-difference judgments. On ColorChecker-24 stimuli, the three closed-weight frontier models reach CCT MAE of 715-1056 K, compared with 1358-2133 K for the five open-weight models. A non-ColorChecker control shows that this advantage is partly template-dependent. The results suggest that current MLLMs have useful color words and coarse visual priors, but their color judgments are not reliably calibrated to CIE-computable color differences.
  </details>

- **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131)**  `arXiv:2610.40131`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengdong Hu, Chao Wang, Kaituo Feng, Huanjin Yao, Mingfei Lu, Hehe Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. Existing video QA benchmarks leave this ability unmeasured: their questions are mutually independent, never reference one another, and each is answerable from the video alone, so nothing ever requires carrying information across turns. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Among over 20 evaluated open- and closed-source MLLMs, Claude Opus 5 performs the best with an overall score of only 45.7\%, highlighting the challenge of in-context video memory. Our dataset and evaluation are released.
  </details>

- **[MLLM-GS: Multimodal Large Language Model Prior-Guided Fast Novel View Synthesis in Extreme Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39936)**  `arXiv:2610.39936`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiliang Wu, Xinye Zheng, Kun Li, Fei Wang, Hao Guo, Yanyan Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Novel view synthesis (NVS) in degraded scenes aims to reconstruct 3D scenes from multi-view observations affected by various degradations, and to generate clear and photorealistic images at unseen viewpoints. Despite recent advances, existing methods often rely on degradation-specific models, limiting their generalization across diverse extreme environments. Generative models provide rich visual priors, but their outputs often suffer from pixel misalignment and color deviations, which can propagate into the reconstructed 3D scene and compromise reconstruction quality. Furthermore, the high computational and memory costs of 3D reconstruction hinder the practical application of NVS in extreme environments. To this end, we propose MLLM-GS, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) prior-guided framework for fast NVS in extreme environments. MLLM-GS consists of two branches: MLLM prior-guided view enhancement and fast 3D scene reconstruction. The former leverages the rich visual priors of a frozen MLLM to enhance views under different severe degradations and introduces a lightweight correction network to reduce pixel shifts and color distortions in MLLM outputs. With supervision from only a few paired clean views, this branch produces reliable enhanced views for all degraded observations, providing pseudo-GT views for subsequent 3D reconstruction. The latter improves 3DGS efficiency by optimizing rasterization, Gaussian memory layout, and parameter updates, reducing redundant computation, memory-access overhead, and optimization costs. Extensive experiments on multiple degraded scenes demonstrate that MLLM-GS effectively handles various severe degradations and achieves high-quality NVS with significantly lower reconstruction time and memory consumption. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[BRIDGE: Learning Brain-Gut Metabolic Evidence for Alzheimer's Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39520)**  `arXiv:2610.39520`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengli Zhu, Ruxi Chang, Tianliang Yao, Yu Jiang, Shengyuan Liu, Yiyang Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Alzheimer's disease (AD) diagnosis may benefit from gut metabolic information associated with brain-gut interactions, yet existing models largely analyze cerebral glucose metabolism alone. Although whole-body fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography (FDG-PET) can provide paired brain-gut observations, routine brain examinations rarely include gut imaging, limiting direct use of this information for diagnosis. In this paper, we present BRIDGE (Brain and Gut Representation Integration via Distribution-Guided Enhancement), a two-stage framework for transferring brain-gut metabolic knowledge to brain-only diagnosis. Specifically, BRIDGE integrates a conditional brain-to-gut latent transport module and a disease-evidence-guided cerebral pathway to capture subject-specific cross-organ correspondence and disease-relevant cerebral representations. For the cerebral pathway, a large language model (LLM) helps organize literature-derived descriptors into human-approved semantic anchors that guide feature learning. Additionally, we introduce a counterfactual-guided residual fusion mechanism, which uses matched and non-matched virtual-gut evidence to supervise a confidence gate and dynamically regulate the contribution of transported cross-organ information. At inference, BRIDGE exploits complementary gut-related context using brain PET alone, without measured gut PET. Extensive experiments across public and private cohorts demonstrate that BRIDGE outperforms brain-only and cross-organ baselines, achieving AUC gains of 6.0% and 5.2% over its brain-only counterpart for CN/MCI and CN/AD diagnosis, respectively. Notably, its ability to transfer complementary brain-gut metabolic context while preserving brain PET as the sole inference input highlights its potential for clinically practical diagnosis. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VEvo-Harness: Causally-Guarded Self-Evolving Harness for Agentic Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39158)**  `arXiv:2610.39158`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuan Wang, Yuhang Yu, Mengting Chen, Tianrun Xu, Zihao Pan, Xuetao Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The central bottleneck in visual generation and editing has migrated from achieving pixel-level fidelity to satisfying densely constrained user specifications through increasingly agentic workflows. Prevailing agentic generation pipelines remain shallow generate-verify-rewrite loops treating VLM judgments as coarse visual feedback, either discarding repair experience or admitting ungated updates that poison skill library. With memorize and govern periods left open, they degrade into ungoverned iterators of repeated trial-and-error rather than continual self-evolving harness. Towards this issue, we present \textbf{VEvo-Harness}, the first causally-guarded continual self-evolving harness system for agentic visual generation. VEvo-Harness introduces four coupled innovations: (1) \textit{a visual traceback} that densifies sparse judgments into root-cause attributions with a hierarchical verification tree; (2) \textit{a visual continual governance} that admits only causally valid skill patches through counterfactual dual-arm image generation replay; (3) \textit{a lifecycle-managed harness state} that hierarchically distills root-cause-anchored episodes across working, experience, and pattern tiers into a persistent skill library of routable and causally verified capabilities; (4) \textit{a heterogeneous sandbox policy} that orchestrates a diverse action space of repair primitives, dynamically routing cost-aware interventions based on root causes to select the most effective strategy. VEvo-Harness surpasses state-of-the-art agentic methods and rivals proprietary generators (\textit{e.g.}, GPT-Image, Nano-Pro) while maintaining the continual harness evolution and generalization.
  </details>

- **[What do MLLMs know about 'Waldo'? Evaluating Visual Reasoning under Multi-Instance Multi-Object Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38957)**  `arXiv:2610.38957`  `cs.CV`  
  _Saumya Mundra, Ajoy Mondal, Vineeth N Balasubramanian, C. V. Jawahar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have led to significant progress in visual reasoning capabilities. However, existing benchmarks curate image-question pairs pertaining to object categories with few instances, enabling trivial object referencing and limited visual ambiguity. In this work, we introduce MIMO-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in a Multi-Instance per Multi-Object categories (MIMO) setting. Each image-question pair in our benchmark pertains to instances belonging to object categories with multiple instances. The presence of such similar feature distractors causes hindrance in basic visual reasoning tasks, necessitating the referring expression to be composed of multiple visual subtasks to uniquely and unambiguously identify the target object. MIMO-Bench comprises 3,802 image-question pairs across attribute binding and numerical counting tasks. Despite impressive performances on existing benchmarks, both SOTA open-source (InternVL, QwenVL) and proprietary (Gemini, GPT-5) models exhibit substantially lower performance on MIMO-Bench, highlighting their limitations in the MIMO setting. The benchmark will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Fine-Grained Differences between Image Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38547)**  `arXiv:2610.38547`  `cs.CV`  
  _Leander Girrbach, Alexander von Recum, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting whether and in particular how two data distributions differ is a fundamental statistical problem. For example, we might be interested in which concepts (expressed in natural language) differ between two classes, the outputs of different generative models, or the same model with different prompt specifications. When working with large and complex image datasets, we intuitively think that first selecting concepts from a broad list using fine-grained patch embeddings, and then optimizing the concepts to better fit the data is an effective workflow. However, prior work instead uses MLLMs to propose concepts and image-level embeddings to select them. Therefore, we systematically compare these techniques and find that selecting concepts from broad lists can replace MLLM-proposals at obviously lower cost. MLLM inference can then be spent on optimizing selected concepts instead to achieve substantial improvements. Additionally, by designing a new benchmark that focuses on low-prevalence and multi-concept distinctions, we find that selecting nonredundant concepts instead of only the most distinguishing concepts matters, and we introduce techniques for how to effectively do this. Finally, we show how our methods address important problems like detecting spurious correlations and biases. Overall, our work presents a fundamental perspective shift on how we analyze interpretable distributional differences between datasets.
  </details>

- **[Don't Ask the LLM: Visual Token Pruning with Spectral Structure and Semantic Relevance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38303)**  `arXiv:2610.38303`  `cs.CV`  
  _Intaek Shin, Geuntaek Lim, Sungjune Park, Yukyung Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) represent images and videos as long visual-token sequences, substantially increasing inference costs. These costs motivate visual-token pruning, whose effectiveness depends on both what signal is used for selection and when that signal becomes available. Our analysis reveals complementary limitations: visual-side [CLS] attention produces scattered, background-biased selections under aggressive compression, while stronger query-conditioned signals become available after substantial computation within the large language model (LLM). This raises a natural question: can structurally coherent, query-aware token selection be performed before consulting the LLM? We therefore propose ST-Pruner (Spectral-Textual Pruner), a training-free framework for pre-LLM visual-token selection. It combines attention-independent spectral saliency with inverted patch-text similarity through parameter-free multiplicative fusion. Across diverse LVLMs, ST-Pruner retains over 95% of full-token performance using at most 11.1% of visual tokens and achieves a 3.2x end-to-end speedup on LLaVA-NeXT. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Hidden Commitment: When Language Models Silently Pick a Side and How Steering Can Surface It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38296)**  `arXiv:2610.38296`  `cs.CV`  
  _Samuel Dawit Assefa, Jae Won Cho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model answers are often treated as a visible record of the internal decision that produced them. We test this assumption in conflicting-context question answering, where two supplied documents support incompatible answers. A model can process the disagreement and still return a one-sided answer, even under prompts that ask it to disclose conflict. In Qwen 0.8B, hidden source commitment is captured by a residual source-choice direction whose sign tracks which document the answer uses. Sparse middle-layer MLP writers feed the same coordinate, and steering the coordinate changes the chosen source. A transfer run on Gemma 3 1B preserves the source-choice readout and sparse-write bridge under LLM-audited labels. Conflict disclosure follows a different pattern, appearing weaker, later-layer, and only partially steerable. These results support a practical monitoring strategy for retrieval systems that detects silent source commitment with residual readouts and pairs conflict-surfacing prompts with disclosure steering when the model has selected a side under disagreement.
  </details>

- **[Complementing Text Based Moderation with Multimodal Grounding: Detecting Fraudulent Inducement Behaviors on Short Video Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38185)**  `arXiv:2610.38185`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoyong Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Short video platforms have become a primary channel for black and gray industry operations in internet finance, where inducements such as prompts to like, follow, join private chat groups, or click external links frequently steer users toward fraud. Because such videos are short, dynamic, and often convey intent through fleeting visual cues rather than stable text, a moderation system must guarantee detection recall and localize the offending segment so reviewers inspect only the relevant clip. Existing OCR+LLM pipelines transcribe on-screen text frame by frame and classify the transcript, but exploit only textual information. Many inducements are conveyed through visual cues OCR cannot observe, and keyframe-based localization cannot recover precise temporal boundaries. We explore OCR+MLLM, which augments the text pipeline with a Multimodal Large Language Model, and compare three routes: OCR+LLM, OCR+MLLM, and a large zero-shot MLLM, along detection, latency, token consumption, and deployment complexity. OCR preserves the text signal that guarantees recall, while the MLLM jointly performs OCR-grounded discrimination and temporal grounding, localizing both textual and non-textual inducements. We construct a dataset of 2,419 internet finance short videos (66.49 hours) with 3,194 annotated events across four categories, and study reward design for RL-based grounding under class imbalance. We report two findings: auxiliary reward terms are exploited through reward hacking, and stronger localization rewards degrade class-balanced detection. We therefore design a recall-weighted F2 localization reward with class-balanced resampling. Experiments show OCR+MLLM localizes better than the keyframe OCR+LLM baseline (mIoU 26.52 vs. 14.49) and detects non-textual inducements that text-only pipelines miss, while maintaining 67.0 percent macro recall.
  </details>

- **[Where Can We Fool the Model's Mind? Response-Guided Semantic Probing and Adaptive Routing for Transferable Attacks on Black-Box MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38013)**  `arXiv:2610.38013`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ahmed Abdelnaby, Mohamed Elmahallawy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) ground language in visual evidence, yet their semantic reasoning can be exploited through transferable black-box adversarial attacks. Existing attacks typically allocate perturbations using surrogate-model saliency, which may not reflect how an unseen MLLM semantically interprets or relies on different image regions. We introduce VISTA (Vulnerability-Informed Semantic Transfer Attacks), a black-box framework that identifies semantically vulnerable visual regions from an MLLM's responses without access to gradients, logits, parameters, or internal representations. VISTA supports two attack settings: (i) VISTA-Proxy, a pure-transfer setting that probes an open-source MLLM and transfers the resulting adversarial examples to unseen commercial MLLMs without querying them; and (ii) VISTA-Victim, a query-assisted setting that probes the target MLLM to capture model-specific semantic vulnerabilities. VISTA builds an Uncertainty-Based Multimodal Behavior (UBMB) map from response drift, conflict, misleading, and semantic shifts, then uses entropy-adaptive Sinkhorn routing and a surrogate ensemble to focus perturbations on vulnerable regions. Across GPT-5, Gemini-3.5-Flash, and Claude Sonnet 4.6, VISTA achieves up to 97 % attack success rate (ASR) while maintaining a Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) above 30 dB. Notably, VISTA-Proxy achieves up to 93 % ASR without querying the final victim, demonstrating that response-derived semantic vulnerability can enable effective transfer across heterogeneous MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34221)**  `arXiv:2610.34221`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangfei Sheng, Xiaofeng Pan, Leida Li, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Johan Wagemans, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than AIC existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.
  </details>

- **[Fast and Useful: Adapting Distilled Generators for Visual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32512)**  `arXiv:2610.32512`  `cs.CV`  
  _Patryk Bartkowiak, Dominik Michels, Sören Pirk, Wojciech Pałubicki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilled text-to-image models generate images quickly, but classifiers trained on those images can perform substantially worse than classifiers trained on images from their slower base models. We adapt released distilled generators to produce better training data while retaining fast generation. We fit a low-rank update to the student's first prediction so that it reaches an intermediate state produced by its unguided base, then disable the update and let the unchanged student finish generation. The base supplies training targets and is absent at inference. Guided targets and shorter jumps give classifiers lower accuracy in our ablations, showing why the state used for supervision matters. Across three generator families, the adapted images train better classifiers than images from the released students. With class-name templates at full ImageNet-1k scale, ResNet-50 top-1 rises from 8.4% to 34.2% for FLUX.2-klein and from 12.7% to 32.1% for SD-Turbo, using two and three denoiser evaluations per image, respectively. Fixed adapters also improve results on additional classes, fine-grained datasets, and other learner architectures and training recipes. Template-based adaptation remains useful against richer-prompt alternatives, while the benefit of combining adaptation with LLM generation prompts depends on the generator. Adapting a single generation step makes fast generators substantially more useful for training visual models.
  </details>

- **[OmniSeek: Native Tool Integration for Multi-turn Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02181)**  `arXiv:2610.02181`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haibo Wang, Jiteng Mu, Jialu Li, Jingru Yi, Yuanjun Xiong, Jianming Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OmniSeek, an agentic framework that transforms an Omni Large Language Model (Omni-LLM) into an active, multi-turn reasoning agent with native tool use. Rather than passively processing an entire audio-visual sequence in a single forward pass, OmniSeek makes evidence acquisition part of the reasoning process: it dynamically decides whether to look or listen, and over which temporal window, to retrieve sparse but critical evidence across different modalities within long contexts. Through an iterative multi-turn protocol, the retrieved raw audio or visual segments are appended back into the context to support subsequent reasoning. To cold-start this capability, we build a data engine that synthesizes OmniTraj-170K, a corpus of multi-hop Chain-of-Thought trajectories with interleaved audio and visual evidence. We first supervise the model on these trajectories to instill multi-turn tool-use behavior, and then further optimize the policy via a two-stage reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Moreover, we introduce an Audio-Visual Necessity objective that explicitly rewards successful trajectories whose reasoning depends on both modalities, discouraging single-modality shortcuts. Extensive experiments across a wide range of benchmarks demonstrate that OmniSeek learns adaptive cross-modal evidence seeking and consistently improves audio-visual reasoning performance.
  </details>

- **[IMU4D: 4D Human-Object Understanding from Wearable IMUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21926)**  `arXiv:2604.21926`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao-Yu Hsu, Tianhang Cheng, Jing Wen, Alexander G. Schwing, Shenlong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding human activities and the objects they interact with typically relies on visual perception, yet cameras pose persistent challenges in privacy, safety, energy efficiency, and scalability. We explore an alternative: 4D perception without vision. Its goal is to recover human motion and the objects the person interacts with purely from everyday wearable sensors. For this we introduce IMU4D, a framework that repurposes large language models for non-visual spatiotemporal understanding of human-object dynamics. IMU4D uses data from a few inertial sensors from earbuds, watches, or smartphones and predicts detailed 4D human motion together with surrounding objects the person interacts with. Experiments across diverse human motion datasets and human-object datasets show that IMU4D yields more coherent and temporally stable results than SoTA cascaded pipelines, suggesting wearable motion sensors alone can support 4D understanding of human-object interactions.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Learning Object-Centric Procedural Understanding in Instructional Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03479)**  `arXiv:2512.03479`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenliang Guo, Yu Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Procedural activities are fundamentally driven by object state transitions, yet existing instructional video benchmarks remain action-centric and cannot evaluate whether models reason about how objects evolve toward task completion. In this work, we introduce ProcObject-10K, the first benchmark that jointly evaluates object-centric reasoning and temporal evidence grounding in instructional videos, across both egocentric and exocentric views. It comprises 10,522 open-ended VideoQA pairs grounded in 1,799 videos, spanning 137 tasks across 9 domains and five reasoning types covering preconditions, state evolution, counterfactuals, mistakes, and readiness. Benchmarking 13 leading MLLMs reveals a substantial answering-grounding gap: models produce plausible answers while failing to localize the supporting evidence (mIoU < 45%), exposing their reliance on linguistic priors rather than recognizing and reasoning about the object dynamics. As a step toward closing this gap, we further provide an object-centric supervised fine-tuning baseline with pseudo object-level supervision and spatial-temporal constraints. Models fine-tuned on ProcObject-10K not only improve on the benchmark itself, but also transfer effectively to other grounded VideoQA and embodied planning tasks. The dataset, annotations, and evaluation toolkit have been publicly released on our project page to support future research on object-centric procedural understanding:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[FAD-KV: Adaptive Dictionary KV Caching for Efficient Reasoning Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37921)**  `arXiv:2610.37921`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Zhu, David H. Yang, Parisa Shiravani, Mohammad Mohammadi Amiri, Tejaswini Pedapati, Subhajit Chaudhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Key-value (KV) caching is a core mechanism in large language models, where intermediate key and value representations are stored to accelerate autoregressive decoding. As generation length increases, KV cache memory and computation grow linearly, making compression critical for efficient long-output reasoning. Dictionary-based sparse KV compression is appealing because it represents each token's KV states as a sparse combination of learned basis elements (atoms) in a dictionary, preserving token-level structure without enforcing a global low-rank constraint. However, existing dictionary-based methods face two practical limitations during decoding: iterative sparse coding methods such as Orthogonal Matching Pursuit add decoding-time overhead, and static dictionaries may mismatch evolving KV distributions under test-time generation. While adaptive dictionary updates can reduce this mismatch, naive append-only strategies lead to dictionary growth during generation. We propose FAD-KV, a \textbf{F}ast \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{D}ictionary KV caching framework for efficient reasoning decoding. FAD-KV integrates (i) a screening-based sparse coding scheme, where a one-shot Top-\(n\) selection identifies a small candidate pool followed by lightweight coefficient refinement, and (ii) a fixed-size dictionary adaptation mechanism that reuses inactive atoms instead of expanding the dictionary. By controlling both sparse coding cost and adaptive dictionary growth, FAD-KV provides a memory-bounded and computation-efficient KV compression framework for long reasoning workloads. Experiments on reasoning and long-context benchmarks show that FAD-KV achieves a strong quality-efficiency trade-off while avoiding unbounded dictionary growth during decoding.
  </details>

- **[OpenEBFM: Energy-based Foundation Models Are Scalable Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33005)**  `arXiv:2610.33005`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuan Pu, Yudong Lu, Ting Wang, Zhicong Hong, Qinuo Li, Xuansheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Energy-based models have shown promising properties for domain-specific tasks but remain challenging in modeling general-purpose scenarios. To bridge this gap, we design energy-steered models (ESMs) for foundational language modeling. ESMs integrate energy steering and token generation within a single architecture to leverage the energy-guided search to refine token generation. Specifically, ESMs first optimize a latent state over the energy landscape, then leverages the refined latent state for token generation. By performing such energy-guided pre-training on large-scale corpora, ESMs learn generally effective energy landscape where favorable tokens correspond to lower energy values. To explore the scaling behavior of ESMs, we pre-train ESMs on three large-scale corpora, then evaluate across 26 downstream tasks spanning a broad range of domains. Our results demonstrate that ESMs have promising scaling behaviors with increasing model size and training data, and outperform GPTs on most downstream tasks. These findings highlight ESMs as a promising architecture for large-scale pre-training and general purpose problem solving. We fully release the model weights and training infrastructure to enable the community to completely reproduce and extend our results.GitHub:this https URLHuggingFace:this https URL
  </details>

- **[HyperThink: Text-to-Parameter Hypernetworks for Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03039)**  `arXiv:2610.03039`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Donggyun Kim, Jack Lu, Chanwoo Kim, Mengye Ren, Seunghoon Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
