  Long-form thinking traces can substantially improve the multi-step reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs), but they introduce high inference-time overhead, with latency dominated by sequential decoding. We propose HyperThink, a text-to-parameter approach that amortizes this reasoning computation into a single query-conditioned parameter update: a lightweight hypernetwork reads the question and predicts updates to a small subset of the base LLM's parameters, while a vector-quantized decoder constrains them to a finite set of reusable patterns to improve robustness and transfer. Trained end-to-end on outputs from the base model itself, HyperThink eliminates long thinking traces at test time: after one hypernetwork forward pass, the adapted model generates a concise step-by-step solution and final answer without an intermediate trace, using far fewer tokens while retaining strong reasoning performance. Empirically, HyperThink improves the low-latency region of the accuracy-latency trade-off on mathematical and general reasoning tasks, with its strongest gains in the near-non-thinking regime.
  </details>

- **[How I Aligned Your Model: Scaling Up Multilingual Safety Training with Templates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40516)**  `arXiv:2610.40516`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Max Neuwinger, Hanna Yukhymenko, Imanol Schlag_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment for Large Language Models is heavily focused on English with limited multilingual representation, particularly damaging low-resource language performance. Related open training datasets are often limited in size or do not follow region-localized harmful topics to broaden the scope of alignment beyond cultures. We propose a scalable template-based augmentation pipeline to produce massive multilingual refusal and benign corpora, extracting safe templates from harmful prompts, translating them and then filling in the slots with culturally authentic entities, both harmful and benign. As a result, we release MSafeTemp, a validated corpus spanning 60 languages useful for multilingual safety alignment and evaluation. Using the dataset, we show that English-only evaluation misjudges multilingual safety: for several models, attacks that fail in English succeed once translated, while culturally localized variants expose further failures that translation alone misses. We also confirm that augmenting safety training data through templates allows for higher diversity and quality of alignment, resulting in improved safety performance beyond English. MSafeTemp and the respective pipeline are aimed at facilitating research and development of safer and more responsible models for less-represented cultures.
  </details>

- **[Prompt-agnostic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37683)**  `arXiv:2610.37683`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Frederic Sadrieh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite their strong performance, large language models remain highly sensitive to prompt formulation. This issue is typically addressed at inference-time through manual prompt optimization, which introduces overhead and lacks scalability. Conversely, we focus on train-time approaches that build prompt robustness into the model during instruction tuning. A common strategy is to mix multiple paraphrases of each prompt into training. We demonstrate that across trainable parameters, the per-paraphrasing gradients interfere, with 57% exhibiting sign conflicts and the naive gradient average discarding approximately a fifth of the magnitude. Therefore, we develop two gradient-merging methods, Prompt-TIES and Prompt-DARE, that address this interference by leveraging interference-resolution operators from model and gradient merging and applying them to prompt gradients. In this study, we compare the efficacy of our methods to increase model robustness to train-time robustness methods spanning all three established classes: data construction, consistency regularization, and contrastive alignment. The evaluation is conducted across four models from two families at two scales. We observe the interaction between prompt phrasing and the specific examples accounts for 54% of per-example variability on average, and exceeds 70% on tasks such as ANLI and WiC. The gradient-merging methods directly improve this local robustness and nearly halve per-example variance on fully fine-tuned models at little cost to overall accuracy. The other train-time methods enhance raw performance, but fail to improve per-example variance reliably. Code:this https URL. Model checkpoints:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Supervised Fine-Tuning for Reasoning Large Language Models: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37681)**  `arXiv:2610.37681`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yongcan Yu, Kuangpu Guo, Jian Liang, Lingxiao He, Meng Wang, Ran He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models have recently demonstrated remarkable capabilities in solving complex tasks, where supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on long chain-of-thought data serves as a crucial foundation for eliciting and enhancing their reasoning abilities. Despite rapid progress in both improving and analyzing reasoning-oriented SFT, the field still lacks a systematic survey that consolidates its fast-growing literature. To fill this gap, we present a comprehensive review of recent advancements in reasoning SFT, examining the literature through the dual lenses of methodological design and analytical investigation. First, we review methodological improvements across the SFT pipeline and categorize them into data-centric approaches and algorithm-centric innovations. Second, we reorganize analytical studies along three dimensions: data characteristics, optimization dynamics, and mechanistic insights. Finally, by synthesizing current research focuses and remaining bottlenecks, we outline promising future directions for reasoning SFT. We hope this survey deepens the understanding of reasoning SFT and paves the way for advanced reasoning models.
  </details>

- **[Behavioral Couplings: From Formation to Behavioral Emergence in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23847)**  `arXiv:2610.23847`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dongyoon Hahm, Woogyeol Jin, Dennis Wei, Yi Zhou, Nathalie Baracaldo, Kimin Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) can induce behaviors beyond those explicitly targeted. For example, training a model to respond more warmly can make it more sycophantic, and training it to write insecure code can cause broad misalignment on prompts unrelated to coding. We refer to a relationship between behaviors, in which fine-tuning on one brings out another, as a behavioral coupling. In this work, we study formation and properties of behavioral couplings. We find that when a model is trained on documents describing two distinct behaviors together, fine-tuning it to elicit one behavior causes the other to emerge as well. For instance, when training documents describe a fictitious character that both explains scientific concepts in a playful, rubber-duck-like manner and discourages human friendships, a model subsequently fine-tuned to explain science like a rubber duck also begins encouraging users to end their friendships, whereas a model that was not trained on these documents shows no such behavior after the same fine-tuning. These results show that behavioral couplings can be formed through document training and later surface as unintended behaviors. More concerningly, we find that couplings can compose. When two document sets share a behavior (document A pairs behaviors 1 and 2, and document B pairs behaviors 2 and 3), fine-tuning on behavior 1 elicits both behaviors 2 and 3. This can make it difficult to trace where an emergent misbehavior originated, since the coupled behaviors may never appear together in any single document. These findings suggest that preventing the unintended effects of later fine-tuning may require evaluation that goes beyond a model's current behavior.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Proof Autoformalization in Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14867)**  `arXiv:2606.14867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengtao Gui, Sheng Yang, Zhouxing Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proof autoformalization aims to translate a mathematical informal proof in natural language into a formal proof in a formal language such as Lean 4. Existing LLM-based models for proof autoformalization are typically evaluated only on well-formed informal proofs from curated datasets. We argue that a robust proof autoformalizer must remain faithful even for informal proofs that diverge from these idealized ones, and we present the first study on the robustness of proof autoformalization models. We formulate two categories of perturbations: a global perturbation paraphrases the informal theorem-proof pair in a different style, under which the formalization should remain consistent; a local perturbation alters a value, symbol, or proof step, possibly in a counterfactual way, and a robust formalization should faithfully reflect the perturbation rather than reverting to the original or inferring a different one on its own. We build a benchmark with both perturbations on miniF2F and MATH-500, and automatically measure how stable a proof autoformalization's correctness is under global perturbations and how faithfully its output reflects local perturbations. We evaluate seven recent models: their correctness varies under global perturbations, and they mostly fail to remain faithful under local perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Executable Schema Contracts: From Automatic Ingestion to Multi-Source Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05415)**  `arXiv:2606.05415`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Padmaja Jonnalagedda, Yuguang Yao, Xiang Gao, Hilaf Hasson, Kamalika Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world data spans tables, documents, and semi-structured files with implicit semantics. Querying this data requires integrating evidence across inconsistent schemas and formats, yet existing approaches either demand costly manual engineering or bypass structure entirely. We present a system that automatically discovers an executable schema from raw multi-source data and uses it as a shared contract for knowledge graph construction and query-time retrieval. A closed-world field catalog constrains LLM-based schema discovery to attested fields; deterministic structural analysis infers identity keys, foreign keys, and source hierarchy; and the resulting schema drives extraction, deduplication, and cross-source linking into a provenance-aware knowledge graph. At query time the schema -- optionally extended via a monotonic protocol -- conditions a multi-tool agent routing retrieval across structured lookup, graph traversal, and vector search, returning grounded answers with traceable citations. In controlled zero-shot comparisons using the same LLM, data, and evaluation harness, the system improves over retrieval-only and decomposition-based baselines across four QA benchmarks, with ablations showing that schema-conditioned routing, structural intelligence, and schema-guided construction each contribute to the gains.
  </details>

- **[Joint Detection of Fraud and Concept Drift inOnline Conversations with LLM-Assisted Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.07852)**  `arXiv:2505.07852`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Şenol, Garima Agrawal, Huan Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting fake interactions in digital communication platforms remains a challenging and insufficiently addressed problem. These interactions may appear as harmless spam or escalate into sophisticated scam attempts, making it difficult to flag malicious intent early. Traditional detection methods often rely on static anomaly detection techniques that fail to adapt to dynamic conversational shifts. One key limitation is the misinterpretation of benign topic transitions referred to as concept drift as fraudulent behavior, leading to either false alarms or missed threats. We propose a two stage detection framework that first identifies suspicious conversations using a tailored ensemble classification model. To improve the reliability of detection, we incorporate a concept drift analysis step using a One Class Drift Detector (OCDD) to isolate conversational shifts within flagged dialogues. When drift is detected, a large language model (LLM) assesses whether the shift indicates fraudulent manipulation or a legitimate topic change. In cases where no drift is found, the behavior is inferred to be spam like. We validate our framework using a dataset of social engineering chat scenarios and demonstrate its practical advantages in improving both accuracy and interpretability for real time fraud detection. To contextualize the trade offs, we compare our modular approach against a Dual LLM baseline that performs detection and judgment using different language models.
  </details>

- **[Decision and System One models in rheumatology: accuracy and calibration on specialty-training examination questions and diagnostic case vignettes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40318)**  `arXiv:2610.40318`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alfredo Madrid-García, Diego Benavent, Fabian Lechner, Phillip Kremer, Johannes Knitza, Beatriz Merino-Barbancho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objectives: To evaluate the accuracy and calibration of Decision and System One models on rheumatology examination questions and diagnostic vignettes. Methods: Two benchmarks were analysed: 172 rheumatology questions from Spanish specialty-training examinations (expanded RheumaMIR dataset) in Spanish and English, and 70 English diagnostic vignettes from RheumBench with 5-18 candidate diagnoses, 52 of which included the reference diagnosis. Four System One models (Jev 1.13, Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1, Laya) and GPT-6 Luna (OpenAI Decisions API) were compared with GPT-6 Sol and two local generative models. The primary outcome was accuracy; secondary outcomes included calibration, discrimination, accuracy of answers with probability >=0.90 and language differences. Results: In RheumaMIR, Jev's accuracy was 93.0% in Spanish and 91.9% in English, below GPT-6 Sol (99.4%; Holm-adjusted P<=0.005); GPT-6 Luna reached 97.7%, Clef 91.9-93.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 86.0-87.8%, without detectable language differences. Jev's answers with probability >=0.90 (76-77% of answers) were 98.5% correct. In RheumBench, accuracy was lower: GPT-6 Sol 76.9%, GPT-6 Luna 63.5%, Clef 59.6%, Jev 50.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 40.4% (each System One model below GPT-6 Sol, Holm-adjusted P<=0.045; chance 9.6%). Jev became overconfident (mean probability 0.659; over/underconfidence index +0.159), and its answers with probability >=0.90 (25% of cases) were 84.6% correct, against 95.5% for GPT-6 Luna (42%). Laya performed near chance in both benchmarks. Conclusion: The best-performing System One models were highly accurate on examination questions but less accurate on diagnostic vignettes, remaining below GPT-6 Sol. Jev's high-confidence predictions were less reliable on vignettes, highlighting the need for task-specific evaluation and calibration.
  </details>

- **[Upcycling Language Models for Multiple Specialized Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40091)**  `arXiv:2610.40091`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingzi Cao, Xi Wang, Nikolaos Aletras_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multi-domain adaptation of large language models requires prioritizing relevant domain knowledge while minimizing interference from irrelevant domains. Existing approaches commonly use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to train a separate adapter for each domain and combine these adapters within one model. However, they either weight adapters equally regardless of the input domain or dynamically weight adapters while still activating all of them including irrelevant ones. Additionally, while efficient, their low-rank structure limits performance. In contrast, upcycling is a model expansion method that adds experts and a router to a dense Transformer layer offering larger capacity than LoRA approaches. In this paper, we propose Domain-Specialized Upcycling (DS-Upcycling) for multi-domain adaptation. We introduce a new domain-aware supervision that allows the router to effectively learn to distinguish domains by selecting the appropriate experts. During fine-tuning on each new domain, we update only the corresponding expert and router while freezing all other parameters. Experiments show that our method achieves the highest average performance across the adapted domains compared to baselines while retaining general performance. With just 256 examples per domain for router supervision, DS-Upcycling achieves 90-98% routing accuracy after adaptation compared with 43-70% for routers trained without our approach.
  </details>

- **[Red-Teaming Large Language Models in Low-Resource African Languages: A Case Study of Yoruba and Nigerian Pidgin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39590)**  `arXiv:2610.39590`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Paula Chikaodinaka Anyanwu, Valdini Douglace Lemofouet, Blessing Ngozi Uzor, Ifeoluwa Dorcas Awotunde, Danielle Blanche Kapsa, Tassallah Abdullahi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) become more widespread in Africa, their safety in low-resource languages remains largely untested since most red-teaming work focuses on English. This study evaluates six state-of-the-art LLMs using 400 explicit adversarial prompts in Yoruba and Nigerian Pidgin, categorised into four harm categories. The responses were scored by native-speaker annotators and reliability was assessed using Cohen's $\kappa$. While models that rejected up to 69% of English prompts rejected as few as 0% in Yoruba, this was not because they were safer, but because they failed to understand the prompts. Attack success rates increased for most models in the Nigerian languages, with GPT-4o Mini demonstrating the most significant cross-lingual safety discrepancy (+0.23 in Yoruba). When models did engage with adversarial prompts, many of the responses were harmful, with rates of harmful content reaching 0.63 in English and 0.50 in Nigerian Pidgin. The most difficult harm category to detect was cultural stereotypes and discrimination (ASR = 0.44 in Nigerian Pidgin). Comprehension in Yoruba was low across most models, and this directly suppressed apparent attack success without indicating actual safety. Gemini 2.0 Flash was the most consistent model across languages. Despite its explicit training in African languages, Aya 101 had the highest attack success rate in English (0.60) and one of the lowest Yoruba comprehension rates (0.28). These results demonstrate that English-centric safety alignment does not transfer to Nigerian languages, highlighting the urgent need for language-specific safety evaluations.
  </details>

- **[Raincheck: Deferred Multimodal Prefill for Efficient Multi-Turn MLLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39562)**  `arXiv:2610.39562`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenjie Huang, Jun Zhang, Hongyaoxing Gu, Haoyang Huang, Tianqi Xu, Xinyue Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn conversations with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reuse input context, but long multimodal inputs increase time to first token (TTFT). Query-aware hard pruning reduces prefill tokens but permanently excludes information that later questions may require. We propose Raincheck, a framework for deferred multimodal prefill that decouples initial token selection from later information availability. An existing pruning method selects tokens for initial prefill; Raincheck preserves and progressively prefills unselected tokens in source order using unused compute capacity during decode. Unfinished prefill continues after answer return during available scheduling gaps. Attention visibility control and affine state mapping enable context updates after generation begins in full-attention and hybrid recurrent models. Prefill-decode fusion reduces repeated weight reads. We evaluate Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B with 30% initial multimodal-token retention. Across both models and four pruning methods, Raincheck recovers on average 77.7% and 60.4% of the quality lost to matched hard pruning on MultiVerse and ConvBench, respectively. On MultiVerse, the SGLang implementation reduces mean first-turn TTFT by up to 24.1% relative to full prefill, with less than 0.5% increase in mean time per output token (TPOT).
  </details>

- **[Integrating Heterogeneous Echocardiography AI Outputs in Language Space: Learnable Late Fusion with a Supervised LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39464)**  `arXiv:2610.39464`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ryo Takizawa, Tempei Kabayama, Chiemi Yamazaki, Shun Kitamura, Ryo Matsuoka, Yuta Ando, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  View- and target-specific echocardiography AI models capture useful but complementary information and may not achieve sufficient predictive performance in isolation. A learned ensemble is a natural way to combine these outputs, but heterogeneous models typically require each output to be converted into a binary decision, probability, confidence score, or fixed-length feature vector. We developed a supervised large language model (LLM)-based late-fusion module that directly aggregates view-specific predicted finding statements and predicted measurements in language space and learns a case-level fusion function. In patient-level three-fold cross-validation of 859 examinations from 646 patients, the proposed method integrated heterogeneous outputs within a single framework, performed comparably to the best-performing reference strategy evaluated, and outperformed a general-purpose LLM without task-specific training. These results suggest that a supervised LLM can combine multiple upstream models without model-specific output conversion or feature engineering that requires medical knowledge.
  </details>

- **[What Makes Medical Examination Items Difficult? Using Large Language Models to Explore Patterns Associated With Empirical Item Difficulty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39373)**  `arXiv:2610.39373`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaomeng Xiong, Marcus Walker, Yanlin Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research on medical item difficulty has largely focused on prediction rather than on identifying interpretable patterns associated with empirical difficulty. This study used empirical difficulty labels to guide large language models (LLMs) in inducing difficulty rubrics and examined how rubric guidance affected LLM difficulty judgments. Using training items and their empirical difficulty estimates from the United States Medical Licensing Examination (USMLE) and the Physician Assistant National Certifying Examination (PANCE), Claude Opus 5 induced separate rubrics for absolute and pairwise difficulty judgment. None of the induced rubrics treated surface features, such as stem length, option count, or images, as major sources of difficulty. Several patterns emerged across both examinations, including cue convergence, misleading cues, and option competition, although their specific forms differed. Retrieval of knowledge that could not be derived from the stem was particularly prominent in the PANCE rubrics, whereas the USMLE rubrics placed greater emphasis on integrating multiple clinical cues. We compared commercial and open-weight LLMs on held-out items under unguided and rubric-guided conditions. Rubric guidance changed how models used the difficulty scale but did not consistently improve performance. In absolute judgments, guidance encouraged broader use of the scale and generally improved coarse-grained agreement, without consistently improving rank ordering. In pairwise comparisons, its effects varied across models and examinations, and judgments became more sensitive to presentation order. These findings suggest that the value of label-guided rubric induction may lie more in generating interpretable, testable hypotheses from empirical difficulty data than in reliably improving difficulty judgments.
  </details>

- **[Cord: Confidence-Anchored Order Residual Decoding for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39372)**  `arXiv:2610.39372`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengwei Zhou, Zhaoyan Jia, Massoud Pedram, Gourav Datta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The decoding order of masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can substantially affect generation quality, yet common decoders typically apply the same reveal rule throughout generation. Confidence provides a strong default, but it measures how decisively a position can be predicted now, not how useful revealing that position will be for subsequent decoding. We propose Cord, a lightweight prompt-conditioned policy that retains confidence decoding as a stable reference and learns when and how to depart from it through geometric residuals over generation direction, proximity to revealed context, and two-sided infilling. The residuals adapt to both the prompt and the evolving partial response. Rather than sampling a new policy action at every decoding step, Cord samples a single low-dimensional coefficient matrix per rollout, which induces an entire state-dependent reveal trajectory. This yields a contextual-bandit formulation trained directly from sequence-level rewards, without step-level credit assignment or backbone updates. Across three dLLMs and five reasoning, code, and constraint-satisfaction benchmarks, Cord improves average task success from 32.0% under confidence decoding to 48.2%, and further improves over the best per-domain fixed residual. Behavioral audits show that the learned residuals induce substantial, task-dependent changes in reveal locality and two-sided infilling. Cord adds only $0.53$M trainable parameters, requires no additional backbone forward passes, and incurs only 5.39% inference latency overhead.
  </details>

- **[Distilling the Shape of Uncertainty: What On-Policy Distillation Learns and How to Predict It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39020)**  `arXiv:2610.39020`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shiping Yang, Chuanqi Tang, Hossein Hajimirsadeghi, Layla El Asri, Mohammad Amin Shabani, Angel X. Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is increasingly adopted in the post-training of large language models. However, a stronger teacher may fail to improve the student. Prior work attributes this to a lack of new knowledge, but what that knowledge is remains unclear. We show that OPD learns not only \textit{how to branch}, i.e., which candidate tokens to consider, but also \textit{where to branch}, i.e., where to keep reasoning paths open. The existing token-overlap metric captures only the former. We introduce the entropy shape, the sequence of next-token entropies, to characterize the latter, and propose the Entropy Shape Correlation (ESC) to measure student-teacher agreement in entropy shape. Tracking ESC throughout training and intervening on a teacher's entropy while preserving its token preferences, we empirically show that \textit{where to branch} causally drives OPD gains, independently of \textit{how to branch}. Finally, we formulate OPD outcome prediction as a teacher selection task and establish an evaluation protocol for it, under which pre-OPD ESC selects the best teacher for both students and outperforms existing predictors. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Reasoning in Transformers: Bridging the Compositionality Gap in GPT-2-like Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39016)**  `arXiv:2610.39016`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Davide Venuto, Leonardo Venuto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compositional generalization remains a persistent limitation of transformer language models: even when a model learns to combine known facts correctly in-distribution (ID), the same compositional mechanism may fail when applied to out-of-distribution (OOD) knowledge. We investigate this compositionality gap in a controlled two-hop reasoning setting using a GPT-2-like transformer model trained from scratch. Building on prior work identifying a circuit associated with compositional generalization, we test whether the failure of OOD transfer is related to how atomic knowledge is structurally exposed during training. We modify the training distribution by presenting both ID and OOD atomic facts in a format positionally aligned with the second-hop retrieval step of the learned compositional circuit, without modifying the model architecture or adding OOD compositional supervision. Under this intervention, the model successfully transfers the learned ID compositional behaviour to held-out OOD atomic facts, closing the observed compositionality gap in the controlled setting. Our results provide evidence that the observed compositionality gap is not a fundamental architecture limitation and that it can be overcome through mechanistically informed training distribution design.
  </details>

- **[Learning Scientific Trajectories to Predict Future Biomedical Discoveries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38719)**  `arXiv:2610.38719`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chia-Hsuan Chang, Brian Ondov, Jiang Lv, Lan Jiang, Yunmai Wang, Haerin Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI co-scientists have expanded the use of large language models for generating scientific hypotheses and guiding experimental investigation. Here we investigate whether temporal patterns in the semantic representations of complete biomedical conclusions can guide hypothesis generation. We introduce ForeSCITE, a framework that identifies thematic clusters of published conclusions, estimates a direction separating earlier from more recent findings within each cluster, and projects existing conclusion embeddings along these directions. An embedding language model trained to reconstruct conclusions decodes the projected coordinates into natural-language hypotheses. Across 407 clusters derived from approximately three million pre-cutoff conclusions, we identify temporal structure and illustrate its retention after decoding. In retrospective, time-sliced evaluations, ForeSCITE increases semantic coverage beyond the historical corpus, biomedical entity-pair novelty and post-cutoff pair re-observation relative to five prompt-based and retrieval-augmented baselines. Expert review of prioritized CAR-T hypotheses identified 37 candidates as both novel and biologically plausible, and expert justifications for five selected hypotheses illustrate their biological rationale and subsequent literature support where available. These findings support temporal guidance in semantic space as a scalable mechanism for proposing biomedical hypotheses for subsequent scientific evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking decision models against generative large language models on medical multiple-choice questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38448)**  `arXiv:2610.38448`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Oscar Freyer, Daniel Glauert, Moritz Hofmann, Max Ostermann, Stephen Gilbert, Jakob Nikolas Kather_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical workflows often require selecting among predefined options. We compared 13 decision models with 15 generative large language model configurations, including different reasoning settings, using 1,273 four-option MedQA questions under text-only conditions. Accuracy ranged from 67.71% to 89.08% for decision models and from 78.32% to 96.86% for generative configurations. Among the tested configurations, Mercury Decide had the lowest mean final-attempt cost (USD 0.0069 per 1,000 questions), while Clef Omni had the shortest median request duration (0.291 seconds). Decision-model probabilities distinguished correct from incorrect answers, but error rates among answers assigned probabilities of at least 0.90 ranged from 1.71% to 12.77%. Answer agreement across three runs ranged from 92.30% to 100%, while probability repeatability varied. Decision models offered resource advantages alongside lower peak accuracy; selecting models for medical workflows requires considering probability quality and repeatability together with accuracy and the resources needed to detect and correct errors.
  </details>

- **[LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38402)**  `arXiv:2610.38402`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaomeng Xiong, Marcus Walker, Yanlin Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. Prior work has largely relied on linguistic features or text representations derived from general-purpose and domain-specific language models, but predictive performance has remained modest. This study takes a different approach by using difficulty-related signals that emerge when open-weight large language models (LLMs) interact with test items. Using USMLE and PANCE medical licensure examination items, we examined LLMs in two roles. As judges, the models provided absolute difficulty ratings and pairwise difficulty comparisons. As simulated examinees, they generated response-based signals including correctness, keyed-option probability, response entropy, and the probability margin between the top two options. We then combined the strongest judgment and response signals with baseline item features in downstream prediction models. Pairwise difficulty judgment was the most consistent individual LLM signal across both datasets, while simulated-examinee signals and absolute judgments provided complementary information in some feature combinations. The best configurations reached a Spearman correlation of $\rho$ = .447 on USMLE, exceeding the best system in the 2024 BEA shared task ($\rho$ = .25), and $\rho$ = .494 on PANCE. These results were comparable to direct absolute judgments from the proprietary frontier model Claude Opus 5 ($\rho$ = .443 and .488, respectively). Overall, judgment and response signals from open-weight LLMs captured difficulty-related information beyond text representations. Because these models can be deployed locally, the approach offers a practical option for predicting the difficulty of unreleased or high-security test items.
  </details>

- **[Statutory Article Retrieval from Arabic Judicial Narratives: Family Law and Law of Evidence in Saudi Arabia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38336)**  `arXiv:2610.38336`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zarah Shibli, Areej Alhothali, Lulwah Alharigy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable legal information retrieval must ground legal reasoning in authoritative statutory sources. In Arabic judicial settings, long factual narratives must be mapped to concise articles that express abstract legal rules, creating a narrative-to-legal-rule abstraction gap. Using a benchmark of 200 synthetic Saudi Arabic judicial cases and 381 articles from the Family Law and the Law of Evidence, we compare word-level and char-4 BM25, zero-shot dense encoders, per-law retrieval, dense-sparse reciprocal rank fusion (RRF), and a proposed Decompose-and-Refine (DaR) pipeline. In DaR, a large language model splits each narrative into disputed legal issues and generates, for each issue, three retrieval channels: a statute-style paraphrase, legal keywords, and a candidate taxonomy chapter. On the whole corpus, the instructed Qwen3-Embedding-8B dense encoder reaches an MRR@10 of 0.849, the best result of any non-decomposed system. Under per-law scoring, this advantage reflects Family Law only: restricted to Evidence articles, the same encoder drops to an MRR@10 of 0.087, and fusion with BM25 raises it only to 0.207. DaR changes this picture, raising Evidence MRR@10 to 0.696 and Family MRR@10 to 0.864. Without decomposition, the two laws favor different strategies; under DaR, the same retriever achieves the best MRR@10 on both.
  </details>

- **[EPSBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Earnings Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38293)**  `arXiv:2610.38293`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanbin Yin, Chuyu Xu, Liqin Ye, Jing Peng, Chao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quarterly earnings forecasting requires an agent to connect financial evidence to the income-statement line items that produce a company's reported earnings. We introduce EPSBench, a benchmark for studying this ability across 149 firm-quarters from 86 US companies. Agents read a fixed collection of filings, earnings-call transcripts, and news, then estimate company-specific income-statement drivers with cited evidence. A shared accounting calculator converts these estimates into GAAP diluted earnings per share (EPS), providing a common basis for comparing forecast assumptions. We evaluate six models under a common harness, assessing point forecast error relative to date-matched analyst consensus and checking whether their prediction intervals achieve the stated coverage. Three models show no detectable paired difference in point forecast error from consensus, and three have higher error. Across all six models, nominal 80% intervals contain reported EPS in only 37--70% of runs that return an interval, and most misses fall above the upper bound because the forecasts run low. The driver records trace most of the error gap between models to operating-income estimates.
  </details>

- **[Prompt Lottery in LLM-as-a-Judge: Measuring and Mitigating Sensitivity to Syntactic Rephrasing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38076)**  `arXiv:2610.38076`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ilija Subasic, Brett Levert, Andrew Rabinovich, Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-Judge has become a standard for evaluating large language models, yet most approaches implicitly assume that a single judge prompt functions as a stable evaluation instrument. Prior work has primarily explored deliberate prompt modifications such as scoring rubrics, role descriptions, or chain-of-thought reasoning in order to optimize accuracy. In contrast, we investigate whether validated prompt variants intended to preserve evaluation semantics while differing in surface realization (\textit{paraphrastic rephrasings}) yield consistent judgments under a fixed evaluation task. We introduce \textit{Prompt Lottery}, a phenomenon in which paraphrastic rephrasings produce different LLMaJ judgments. Across a large-scale study spanning diverse evaluation tasks, judge families, and model backbones, we find that these variations can alter evaluation outcomes and, in some settings, reverse relative model rankings. A user study with 10 human annotators shows that LLMaJ judges exhibit 6-9$\times$ larger agreement degradation under prompt rephrasings than humans, falling below the generally accepted threshold in usability studies. This indicates that single-prompt evaluation is insufficiently robust for model ranking. Finally, we show that multi-prompt aggregation substantially improves robustness by emphasizing judgments that remain consistent across prompt variants.
  </details>

- **[From Geometry to Behavior: How Instruction Exposure Unlocks Latent Rhetorical Directions in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37685)**  `arXiv:2610.37685`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shengjie Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models do not merely memorize facts; they absorb rhetorical strategies from pretraining data, including the human tendency to reason by analogy. We ask: is this absorbed capability geometrically accessible before any instruction tuning, and what determines whether it can be activated at inference time?We extract an "Analogy Vector" from the residual stream via difference-in-means and show it enables steerable explanation style across seven instruction-tuned models (Gemma-2, Llama-3.1, Qwen-2.5; 2B-32B), improving harmonic mean from 3.22 to 3.94 (+22%) for Gemma-2-9B-IT on 300 held-out test prompts. A small human validation study confirms the LLM judge on Analogy Quality.Our central finding, from seven matched base-instruct pairs, is a gradient of behavioral accessibility: the analogy direction exists in every base model, but its behavioral accessibility under linear intervention scales continuously with instruction exposure during training. Gemma-2 base models (strict pretraining/IT separation) show flat steering landscapes: present but behaviorally inert. Qwen-2.5 base models (instruction data woven into pretraining) are already partially accessible before any explicit fine-tuning; base-instruct cosine similarity of the analogy vector reaches 0.94-0.97 for Qwen versus 0.37-0.66 for Gemma, providing geometric corroboration of the behavioral gradient. This challenges the binary "base vs instruct" framing: the relevant variable is how much instruction exposure a model has received across its full training pipeline. We position activation steering as a complementary interpretability tool that reveals how instruction exposure organizes rhetorical knowledge in activation space.
  </details>

- **[An Introduction to Large Language Models for Scientists and Professionals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37684)**  `arXiv:2610.37684`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Umberto Michelucci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly become important tools for science, education, medicine, and professional work. Despite their apparent simplicity from the user's perspective, effective and responsible use requires an understanding of how these systems work, what their limitations are, and how their outputs should be evaluated. I wrote this document as a basis for my lecture on LLM in the master in artificial intelligence in medicine at the university of Turin, Italy. This book provides a practical and accessible introduction to LLMs for scientists and professionals. It explains the fundamental ideas behind tokenisation, embeddings, training, inference, context windows, prompting, and instruction hierarchies, while deliberately avoiding mathematical detail. Particular attention is given to the limitations of LLM-generated answers, including hallucinations, uncertainty, reproducibility, bias, and the need for systematic verification and human oversight. The book also discusses privacy, governance, security, and the responsible handling of personal and sensitive information. More advanced topics include Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), retrieval quality, chunking, embeddings, vector search, agents, tools, and workflows. A substantial practical exercise guides the reader through running a local LLM with Ollama and Open WebUI and using it to interact with a collection of scientific papers. The exercise emphasizes evidence retrieval, source verification, uncertainty, disagreement between studies, and the distinction between plausible language and scientifically supported conclusions. No programming knowledge is required.
  </details>

- **[Rank-Aware Differentially Private Release of Listwise Preferences for LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37682)**  `arXiv:2610.37682`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junwei Chen, Manjiang Yu, Pengpeng Qiao, Yang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Listwise preference feedback offers richer supervision for large language model (LLMs) alignment than pairwise comparisons, but a full ranking also reveals more sensitive user preferences. Existing privacy-preserving alignment methods focus mainly on pairwise feedback, while listwise alignment under local differential privacy (LDP) remains unexplored. Extending LDP to this listwise domain raises a critical challenge: the high sensitivity of the full ranking space demands excessive noise, neutralizing the benefits of listwise supervision. To address this challenge, we analyze the interaction between supervision granularity and privacy noise in downstream training, and introduce a rank-aware exponential mechanism that privatizes listwise preference data into a low-sensitivity granularity suitable for downstream alignment. The mechanism leverages ranking information to sample a fixed-size binary partition, concentrating the noise near borderline items instead of perturbing all items uniformly as randomized response (RR) does. Empirical evaluations on downstream LLM alignment tasks show that our mechanism consistently outperforms existing LDP baselines at matched privacy budgets. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176)**  `arXiv:2610.28176`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiapu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Decodable but Not Actionable: Auditing the Representation-Behavior Gap in Frozen LLMs for Geometric Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17843)**  `arXiv:2608.17843`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Man Liang, Xinzhao Cheng, Faizan Wajid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) perform well on structured reasoning tasks, but what they encode and whether encoded information guides their behavior remain unclear. We introduce a controlled auditing framework that uses parametric CAD to distinguish failures of encoding, expression, and activation-level control. Applied to eight pretrained models and matched randomly initialized controls, our framework reveals that pretraining contributes substantially more to linear decoding of local relations than to global constraint status, which is already highly decodable from random representations. Yet strong local decodability does not translate into the models' zero-shot predictions, which remain far below supervised probe performance. Restoring clean internal activations can repair probe predictions, whereas simple activation steering does not provide reliable targeted control. With only 400 labeled relation pairs, low-rank fine-tuning substantially improves generation on three representative models, showing that the representation--behavior gap can be reduced through adaptation. Together, these findings show why decodability alone is insufficient evidence that encoded information will be expressed or controllable in model behavior.
  </details>

- **[EHRNote-ChatQA: A Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Multi-Turn Clinical Question Answering over Longitudinal Discharge Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15735)**  `arXiv:2606.15735`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiyoun Kim, Muhan Yeo, Eunhye Jang, Jeewon Yang, Hangyul Yoon, Su Ji Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discharge summaries are crucial clinical documents containing the context of a patient's overall hospital stay, and are routinely reviewed by medical experts for patient readmission, ongoing care, and diagnostic decision-making. When reviewing them, medical experts often must iteratively synthesize information across multiple discharge summaries while verifying the evidence supporting each answer. Although large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for clinical question answering, existing benchmarks do not sufficiently reflect this setting: they often evaluate exam-style medical knowledge or focus on single-turn question answering with limited evidence-grounding evaluation. We introduce EHRNote-ChatQA, the first benchmark for evidence-grounded multi-turn clinical question answering over patients' multiple discharge summaries. Built from de-identified MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, EHRNote-ChatQA contains 967 patient-level multi-turn samples spanning one to five notes and 16,072 medical-expert-verified QA pairs (8,036 content questions, each paired with an evidence-grounding question) across eight clinical categories. The benchmark is constructed through an expert-informed pipeline combining discharge-summary structuring schema, expert-curated multi-turn QA templates, and LLM-based generation, followed by review and revision of every single QA sample by 12 medical experts (11 physicians and 1 nurse). Benchmarking 24 proprietary and open-weight LLMs reveals several challenges, including that LLMs struggle more with evidence grounding than content answering, multi-turn errors compound across turns, and single-turn clinical QA performance does not reliably transfer to this setting. These findings establish EHRNote-ChatQA as a rigorous and practical benchmark for evaluating clinical QA systems.
  </details>

- **[JANUS: Evaluating Goal-Conditioned Distortion in Factually Grounded LLM Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10852)**  `arXiv:2606.10852`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Polydoros Giannouris, Mohsinul Kabir, Sophia Ananiadou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM deception is often evaluated through fabricated claims, explicit lies, or strategic concealment. Yet misleading communication can also arise from selectively presenting true facts, for example, by omitting adverse evidence, softening unfavorable details, emphasizing favorable information, or replacing precise qualifications with vague language. Existing benchmarks largely overlook this subtler failure mode. We introduce JANUS, a benchmark for measuring goal-conditioned pragmatic distortion in fact-grounded LLM outputs. Each of its 160 scenarios, spanning 8 domains, provides a fixed pool of favorable and adverse facts and contrasts a neutral decision support with institutional conditioning, such as increasing adoption, or support despite potential harm to affected individuals or groups. By constraining outputs to the same fact pool, JANUS isolates misleading net impressions from hallucination and fabrication. Experiments across 12 LLMs reveal consistent goal-conditioned distortions, showing that current models remain sensitive to incentives and framing and lack robust safeguards against selectively misleading communication. We publicly release our corpus and code.this https URL
  </details>

- **[Can I Take Another Dose? DoseBench: Evaluating LLM Decision-Making in OTC Dosing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04262)**  `arXiv:2606.04262`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Maroof Kousar, Jinsung Han, Mohit Chandra, Munmun De Choudhury, Yibo Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to answer everyday health questions online, including whether someone can take another dose of an over-the-counter (OTC) medication. Yet this common safety-relevant setting remains underexplored in medical QA. Answering these questions requires checking previous doses, timing, and product-specific dosing limits, while recognizing when important information is missing. We introduce DoseBench, a focused benchmark of 230 consumer-style acetaminophen and ibuprofen dosing questions with human-annotated YES, NO, and AMBIGUOUS labels, and evaluate eight LLMs with five responses per question. Overall accuracy ranges from 44.8% to 62.2%, while models incorrectly approve 14.0% to 60.5% of prohibited doses. Models with similar accuracy can therefore make very different dosing decisions. Additional analyses show that these failures are not easily fixed by giving models more guidance. Asking models to check dose, timing, and missing information makes them more cautious, while providing drug facts improves accuracy but can make them overlook missing information. These findings highlight a broader challenge in health QA: applying rules correctly and recognizing when information is missing.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Questions: Evaluating the Stated Knowledge of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26937)**  `arXiv:2605.26937`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Luca Giordano, Simon Razniewski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric knowledge in large language models (LLMs) is a cornerstone of their success, yet remains poorly understood. Existing knowledge benchmarks typically rely on predefined questions (e.g., "What is the birth date of M.L. King?"), evaluating only knowledge that benchmark designers explicitly choose to query, a problematic availability bias. In this paper, we introduce open knowledge evaluation, a new paradigm for benchmarking the stated knowledge of LLMs. Instead of asking narrow questions, it evaluates models on the knowledge they choose to surface in response to open-ended elicitation prompts (e.g., "Tell me everything you know about M.L. King"). This shifts the focus from predefined answer retrieval toward characterizing the knowledge models naturally express. We instantiate this paradigm with BeQu (Beyond Questions), a benchmark of 10,000 entities paired with reference corpora for statement verification. Using BeQu, we evaluate a broad range of language models and analyze the effects of reasoning effort, model scale, prompt format, and knowledge domain. Data and leaderboard are available on this work's GitHub repository and atthis http URL, the benchmark website.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable Difficulty-Aware Knowledge Tracing in Tutor-Student Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01097)**  `arXiv:2605.01097`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyan Huang, Alexander Scarlatos, Jaewook Lee, Andrew Lan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have led to the development of AI-powered tutoring systems that provide interactive support via dialogue. To enable these tutoring systems to provide personalized support, it is essential to assess student performance at each turn, motivating knowledge tracing (KT) in dialogue settings. However, existing dialogue-based KT approaches often ignore question difficulty modeling and rely on opaque latent representations from LLMs, hindering accurate and interpretable prediction. In this work, we propose an interpretable difficulty-aware conversational KT framework built upon LLMs, which explicitly models students' abilities and the difficulty of tutor-posed tasks at each turn. The framework incorporates the original textual question and the next tutor-posed task to estimate the student's knowledge state and the difficulty of the upcoming turn. Furthermore, it integrates Item Response Theory to map LLM's outputs into student ability and question difficulty parameters, enabling interpretable prediction of student performance grounded in cognitive theories of learning. We evaluate the framework on two tutor-student dialogue datasets. Both quantitative and qualitative results show that our framework outperforms existing KT baselines, meanwhile generating interpretable outputs consistent with cognitive theory.
  </details>

- **[SAFE: An LLM-as-Verifier Framework for Evidence-Grounded Multi-Hop Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01993)**  `arXiv:2604.01993`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Daeyong Kwon, Soyoung Yoon, Seung-won Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop QA benchmarks often reward Large Language Models (LLMs) for spurious correctness, where models reach correct answers through invalid intermediate reasoning. We propose SAFE, an LLM-as-verifier framework for evidence-grounded multi-hop QA. Rather than judging only the final answer after generation, SAFE verifies reasoning during generation by checking intermediate steps against the provided passages and previous reasoning trajectory. To make this process checkable, SAFE decomposes reasoning into atomic, evidence-grounded units represented with Knowledge Graph (KG) triples. At train-time, SAFE verifies benchmark supervision under KG-grounded constraints and constructs reliable verifier training data. At inference-time, an external verifier checks each generated step, identifies invalid reasoning, and provides correction feedback before errors propagate. Across three multi-hop QA benchmarks, SAFE improves accuracy by 8.8 pp on average. These results show that evidence-grounded multi-hop QA benefits from shifting LLM-based evaluation from post-hoc answer judgment to stepwise reasoning verification.
  </details>

- **[The System Hallucination Scale (SHS): A Human-Centered Instrument for Assessing Perceived Reliability of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09989)**  `arXiv:2603.09989`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Heimo Müller, Dominik Steiger, Markus Plass, Andreas Holzinger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models routinely produce factually incorrect or misleading outputs, yet conventional automated benchmarks overlook how users perceive reliability during interaction. We introduce the System Hallucination Scale (SHS), a lightweight instrument modeling the famous System Usability Scale (SUS) and System Causability Scales (SCS). SHS measures perceived reliability across five dimensions: factual accuracy, source reliability, logical coherence, deceptiveness of presentation, and responsiveness to user guidance, using ten balanced Likert-scale items that provide a directional score and consistency diagnostic. In a supervised validation study with 193 participants (386 evaluations across 47 trained experimenters), SHS demonstrated strong internal consistency and high clarity, completing in an average of 4.2 minutes. Participants rated source verifiability and presentation deceptiveness worse than factual accuracy, indicating perceived vulnerabilities lie primarily in traceability and framing. As a proof of concept, SHS assesses human-perceived reliability rather than ground-truth correctness, providing open-source multilingual implementations and an interactive calculator.
  </details>

- **[Jacobian Scopes: A Unified Geometric Framework for Token-Level LLM Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16407)**  `arXiv:2601.16407`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Toni J.B. Liu, Baran Zadeoğlu, Gurbir Arora, Raphaël Sarfati, Nicolas Boullé, Christopher J. Earls_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) make next-token predictions based on clues present in their context, such as semantic descriptions and in-context examples. Yet, elucidating which prior tokens most strongly influence a given prediction remains challenging due to the proliferation of layers and attention heads in modern architectures. We propose Jacobian Scopes, a suite of gradient-based, token level causal attribution methods for interpreting LLM predictions. Grounded in perturbation theory and information geometry, Jacobian Scopes quantify how input tokens influence various aspects of a model's prediction, such as specific logits, the full predictive distribution, and model uncertainty (effective temperature). Through case studies spanning instruction understanding, translation, and in-context learning (ICL), we demonstrate how Jacobian Scopes reveal implicit political biases, uncover word- and phrase-level translation strategies, and shed light on recently debated mechanisms underlying in-context time-series forecasting. On token-ablation faithfulness benchmarks (LAMBADA and IWSLT2017) across six LLMs, the distribution-level Fisher and Temperature Scopes significantly outperform established gradient-based attribution methods in 10 of 12 model-dataset settings. To facilitate exploration of Jacobian Scopes on custom text, we open-source our implementations and provide a cloud-hosted interactive demo atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Elucidating the Design Space of LM Data Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40655)**  `arXiv:2610.40655`  `cs.CL`  
  _Steven Yu, Sheng-Yu Wang, Kimia Hamidieh, Antonio Torralba, David Bau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data attribution methods for Language Models (LMs) are typically evaluated using downstream tasks like fact retrieval. However, existing evaluations vary multiple dataset properties simultaneously, making it impossible to isolate exactly why an attribution algorithm fails. To diagnose these failures, we explore the design space of LLM data attribution, with a primary focus on evaluation. We introduce the Controlled Attribution Benchmark (CATT) for fact tracing, in which we fine-tune LLMs on entirely fictitious knowledge, providing unambiguous ground truth for attribution. This synthetic setup allows us to systematically alter one dataset configuration at a time, such as document redundancy and lexical distractors. With this configurable evaluation, we demonstrate that redundancy and distractors consistently degrade attribution performance. Rather than proposing a new estimator, we examine two design choices that are underexplored: the loss used to compute gradients and how per-token attribution scores are aggregated into a document score. We find that the true causal evidence within a training document is highly localized, but standard methods dilute this evidence into noise by averaging attribution scores across the entire document. We find that replacing standard cross-entropy loss gradients with the negative log-odds gradients surfaces the critical tokens from other uninformative ones. We can then aggregate only the top token scores to improve ground truth recall by 35 to 72 percentage points across multiple baselines on our diagnostic benchmark. The negative log-odds correction also generalizes to FTRACE-TREX, improving counterfactual retraining performance for every method we test, by up to 4.9x.
  </details>

- **[ShallowDefend: Harnessing Shallow Safety Alignment for Multi-Turn Defender](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40627)**  `arXiv:2610.40627`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qiyao Ma, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Chengquan Guo, Jialin Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment has enabled large language models to reliably refuse harmful requests directly, yet the same request distributed across a multi-turn conversation still succeeds far too often. This brittleness has a mechanistic root: Alignment is shallow, with safety behavior concentrated in a model's first few output tokens, and existing defenses inherit the weakness, supervising the surface form of the response without ever giving the model an explicit safety decision to learn. We take the opposite view of shallowness: if a short span determines the trajectory of an entire response, that span is the highest-leverage place to intervene, and the defender can occupy it first. We propose Assess $\to$ Decide $\to$ Respond, a framework in which the defender assesses the user's intent, commits to an explicit, parseable safety decision, and only then generates a reply conditioned on it, with the commitment trained by reinforcement learning at the payload turn. Because the decision is a token span, the reward can be a rule: a self-verified reward checks the committed label against the conversation's known nature, requiring no judge and no annotation, while the same format also supports an LLM-as-judge reward over a graded decision space with safe redirection. Across two model families and fourteen benchmarks spanning safety, utility, and helpfulness, our defenders achieve the strongest multi-turn safety in a controlled comparison, maintaining their lead in safety while preserving utility at a modest cost to helpfulness. A per-token KL analysis and interventional probes confirm why this works: training concentrates its effect on the brief deliberation that sets a response's trajectory, and the decision committed there is a causal control point. The very shallowness attackers exploit becomes the mechanism of defense.
  </details>

- **[Every Token Needs Its Own Teacher: Multi-teacher Adaptive On-policy Self-distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40574)**  `arXiv:2610.40574`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yifu Luo, Chuang Ma, Zeyu Chen, Enkelejda Kasneci, Qi Chen, Gjergji Kasneci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy Self-distillation (OPSD) has proven effective for post-training large language models by constructing a teacher conditioned on privileged references. However, existing approaches keep the teacher fixed either throughout training by consistently exposing it to the full reference, or within each response by adaptively varying partial reference exposure only across responses. In this paper, we argue that neither of them is sufficient. Instead, every token needs its own teacher, as the teacher preference can vary token by token within the same response. Through a teacher-intervention pilot study, we validate that the teacher preference differs across tokens, and reveal that a preferred teacher can be identified for each token through majority voting and KL-based selection. Motivated by these findings, we propose Multi-teacher Adaptive On-policy Self-Distillation (MTA-OPSD), the first token-level adaptive OPSD method. Our method constructs a multi-teacher pool consisting of candidate teachers, and enables every token to adaptively select its preferred teacher during training. Experiments with Qwen3 models on mathematical reasoning tasks show that MTA-OPSD consistently outperforms existing OPSD approaches with an average gain of up to $+5.9$ over the base model, highlighting the importance of adapting teacher supervision at the token level. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[IndicIPR-QA: A Benchmark for Extractive Question Answering on Indian IPR Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40518)**  `arXiv:2610.40518`  `cs.CL`  
  _M. Mohaiminul Islam, Muhammad Abulaish_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Legal Question Answering (QA) over statutory texts is a challenging task in Natural Language Processing (NLP), especially in low-resource settings with complex legal systems. It requires answers that are precise and grounded in the law. However, to the best of current knowledge, no existing extractive QA benchmark provides exact answer spans from statutory texts across all seven Indian Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) Acts. This paper presents IndicIPR-QA1, an extractive QA benchmark for Indian IPR laws. The proposed dataset contains 4,462 QA pairs from 1,116 statutory contexts across seven Acts. Questions and answers are generated using GPT-4o and validated through nine automatic quality checks, single and multi-model LLM evaluations, and a 200-pair human audit. Section-level partitioning prevents source-context overlap across the training, validation, and test sets. Further analysis examines answer span length, lexical overlap, and multi-clause structure across the corpus and individual Acts. BM25 achieves a token-level F1 score of 20.21, while fine-tuned InLegalBERT reaches 85.02 under a unified sliding-window inference protocol. The large performance gap between BM25 (20.21 F1) and InLegalBERT (85.02 F1) shows that simple word matching is not enough to answer these legal questions. Performance also varies across the seven Acts, indicating substantial differences in answer-extraction performance.
  </details>

- **[Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40350)**  `arXiv:2610.40350`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zheng Zhu, Xiaozhuan Liang, Xi Chen, Bei Yu, Jiaya Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points.
  </details>

- **[Judged in Company: In-Context Batching Makes LLM Verdicts Depend on Other Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40241)**  `arXiv:2610.40241`  `cs.CL`  
  _Peng Xie, Amr Alanwar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To save cost, practitioners often ask a language model to grade many independent items in one prompt. This assumes that verdicts are separable: that the verdict on an item does not depend on the other items in the prompt or on its position among them. Across three judgement tasks (harm in social-media comments, short-answer grading and review star ratings) and five open-weight models with 4-9B parameters, assigning the same 2,000 items to different random batches of 25 changes 11-22% of decisions on harm and grading, 6.5-13.3 points more than renaming the item keys or rewording the instruction, while AUROC against human labels differs from single-item grading only by -0.033 to +0.010; for four models with 14-32B parameters, 6-15% of decisions change, 4.2-9.1 points more. We show that flip rates decompose exactly into components of order, slot and membership, and that under a threshold model they grow with the square root of the variance that the context contributes, so that effects that are small for scores are large for decisions. Most of the latent dissimilarity between partitions comes from the order of the neighbours and the slot. The main effect of which items share the prompt, the part that voting over permutations of a batch cannot remove, accounts for 0.11-0.22 of it, and majority votes over nine permutations still leave 52-60% of the flips. Batched verdicts also differ systematically from single-item verdicts: even the majority of fifteen random batches disagrees with them on 8.4-17.8% of decisions. Editing a single generation shows that both the model's own earlier verdicts and the neighbours' texts carry the effect. On the easy task, star ratings, only 2.9-3.9% of decisions change. We recommend grading ambiguous items one per prompt, recovering most of the cost with a cached prefix, and reporting agreement with single-item grading whenever items are batched.
  </details>

- **[ScienceJudge: Pattern Perturbation Augmentation for Sentence-Level Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40238)**  `arXiv:2610.40238`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chuhai Cai, Xin Chen, Min Yang, Feng Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sentence-level scientific claim verification determines whether each sentence in an AI-generated scientific paragraph is supported by a given multimodal evidence bundle. Fine-grained reporting errors often preserve fluent and plausible statements while introducing subtle evidence-claim deviations, making them difficult to detect under sparse and imbalanced supervision. We propose Pattern Perturbation Augmentation (PPA), which models reporting errors as label-specific faithfulness breaking transformations and induces reusable error patterns from labeled examples. PPA applies these patterns to supported claims to construct controlled hard negatives and expands their coverage through pattern-level, operation-level, and cross-domain augmentation. Based on these augmented views, we develop ScienceJudge, which integrates PPA-trained vision-language predictors and instruction-guided LLM predictors through class-weighted ensemble inference. On the local development set, ScienceJudge improves the average score from 0.8124 to 0.8692, achieving a Macro-F1 of 0.8466 and a Paragraph Exact Match score of 0.8919. Our submitted system ranks first on the official NLPCC 2026 Task 10 Track 1 leaderboard with a final score of 0.6890.
  </details>

- **[Mobilizing the Archive: Bitext Extraction at Scale from Archival Documents for Extremely Low-resource Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40233)**  `arXiv:2610.40233`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chutong Meng, Alec J. Bateman, Antonios Anastasopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While mining parallel sentences from web pages has proven effective for low-resource machine translation, the majority of the world's languages have little to no web presence. What they do have, though, are resources that are the result of centuries of efforts by linguists, such as grammars, dictionaries, and other documentation materials. We propose extracting translation examples from such linguistic resources, which offer parallel data for languages under-represented online and could potentially scale to thousands more languages. We develop a pipeline to achieve this, leveraging an Optical Character Recognition (OCR) model as well as a vision language model (VLM), to ensure identified content to be aligned with visual layout information. We demonstrate the utility of the extracted bitexts for bootstrapping translation systems across 20 languages, both by training machine translation models and by applying in-context learning for large language models. For in-context learning, we observe improvements of +15.78 and +6.94 chrF2++ over zero-shot translation for into low- and high-resource languages, respectively.
  </details>

- **[AI Cosmos: An Agentic Infra for Situated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40031)**  `arXiv:2610.40031`  `cs.CL`  
  _AI Cosmos Team, Jinfeng Zhou, Yuxuan Chen, Fangming Chang, Mingyu Su, Yifan Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can improve task performance during learning, yet these gains do not necessarily reflect learners' ability to thereafter apply the knowledge and skills independently. We present AI Cosmos, an agentic infrastructure for situated learning that organizes learning around two complementary principles: scenario-based participation and cumulative assets of practice. AI Cosmos organizes learners and role-specialized agents within learning scenarios that integrate task-relevant knowledge and, when needed, executable workbenches. An asset-memory loop preserves the processes and outcomes of practice as reusable assets with explicit provenance and dependencies. We evaluate scenario-based interaction through two preregistered randomized controlled studies examining the acquisition of legal reasoning and programming competence, holding the same underlying LLM across conditions. AI Cosmos achieves greater learning gains than a conventional LLM in legal reasoning and higher mastery of unseen programming problems than an LLM-as-Teacher baseline.
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs Ignore What They Know? Measuring Knowledge Leakage in Persona-Based User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39939)**  `arXiv:2610.39939`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sangmin Lee, Sangwon Lee, Sungbeom Choi, Jeeseung Park, Kibeom Myoung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When persona-conditioned responses are read as respondent-level simulations, each response should reflect the described persona's knowledge, and the respondent written to know nothing must act as if the model's knowledge were not there. We test whether it can, and find persistent favourable-item real-twin rating separation under the tested low-knowledge and explicit-ignorance prompts. We define the resulting problem (knowledge leakage: a response bias that arises when knowledge the real user could not have enters the simulated response) and present a methodology for measuring it quantitatively. The core instrument pairs real items with fabricated twins matched in surface form: screened fabricated variants designed to minimize prior item-specific knowledge. The paired difference therefore provides a diagnostic contrast for knowledge leakage even in rating tasks where no answer is correct. In a rating experiment across three knowledge-handling arms and five API endpoints from two model families, we find, first, that the personas we test do not fully suppress real-item existence and familiarity signals: a favourable-item real-twin rating contrast remains under an explicit in-context statement that the persona has never heard of the item. Second, the unfavourable-item contrast reverses sign across domain groups: pooling all unfavourable-content items across domains the contrast sits near zero (+0.077 [-0.057, +0.204]), yet the same estimator in the same conditions returns -0.359 where the rating scale asks about an item's documented hazard and +0.438 where it asks about a company or an organisation. A pooled analysis of unfavourable-content items against zero therefore does not detect the opposing domain-group differences. All estimates are conditional on the adequacy of twin construction and screening; human low-knowledge baselines remain necessary for population-level interpretation.
  </details>

- **[KV Cache Quantization for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39853)**  `arXiv:2610.39853`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yan Tai, Yunan Ding, Guangzhao Li, Siqi Luo, Chaoyang Zhang, Guangtao Zhai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KV cache quantization is well studied for autoregressive language models, where cached prefix states are exact under causal attention and quantization is mainly a cache reconstruction problem. Diffusion language models (dLLMs) break this assumption. Fast dLLM inference reuses KV states across denoising steps, but bidirectional token representations can change as masked tokens are revealed, so the reused cache may already differ from full recomputation before quantization is applied.
