A single throughput number cannot answer the questions operators actually face, because generative serving couples two phases with very different service characteristics, continuous batching reshapes the effective service process, and user-visible tail latency can degrade long before aggregate throughput saturates. We present GAUSS, a workload-aware analytical and Monte-Carlo simulator that predicts the full distributions of time-to-first-token (TTFT) and time-between-tokens (TBT), together with the sustainable request rate, for a given workload, engine, and accelerator. GAUSS pairs calibrated, phase-specific latency surfaces for prefill and decode with a Lindley-recurrence model of queueing under stochastic arrivals and estimates performance by ensemble Monte-Carlo sampling rather than by replaying a single trace. This lets GAUSS reason about workloads, routing policies, and hardware that have never been deployed, and to capture the latency tails that trace-driven simulators systematically under-sample. We describe the model and show how it supports operating-point selection, traffic segmentation, and shortest-remaining-processing-time scheduling capacity questions that trace-driven simulators cannot address.
  </details>

- **[FilingLoop: Evaluating and Evolving LLM Agents for Corporate Disclosure Review and Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37905)**  `arXiv:2610.37905`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Yilei Zhao, Guangyan Gan, Shuo Sun, Lin William Cong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public disclosure repositories such as SEC EDGAR support informed investment through web access to corporate filings. Supporting their review with language model agents requires discovering unstated deficiencies across related documents, acquiring missing company facts, and verifying that requested revisions satisfy evidence-backed requirements. Existing financial question-answering evaluations provide limited evidence of these interactive capabilities. We introduce \textbf{FilingLoop}, an interactive benchmark and a self-evolving agent framework for corporate disclosure review and revision. Grounded in historical U.S.\ Securities and Exchange Commission correspondence, FilingLoop reconstructs 380 predominantly IPO review tasks from 96 companies under distinct information constraints. An evaluator separate from the acting agents scores final filings and responses against 6,099 eligible requirements and audits unsupported claims and harmful revisions. Our framework couples iterative review and revision within each case with procedural learning across cases, converting requirement-level evaluation feedback into role-specific guidance without updating model weights. Updates affect only subsequent cases, allowing procedural adaptation to be assessed without retrying scored tasks. On the evaluated stream, evolution increases requirement fulfillment from 24.28\% to 27.96\% and full comment resolution from 16.90\% to 20.75\%, with a 5.8\% increase in total cost over the fixed-policy variant. FilingLoop connects the concerns an agent discovers with the company facts it obtains and the revisions it commits, providing requirement-level feedback for procedures used in later review tasks.
  </details>

- **[The Anatomy of Socio-Demographics in Pluralistic Offensiveness Modeling: When and Where It Helps, and the Role of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37896)**  `arXiv:2610.37896`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Wang, Leixin Zhang, Jinyi Mu, Yu Wang, Mayank Jobanputra, Isabel Valera, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Subjective judgments such as offensiveness often vary substantially across individuals, motivating pluralistic modeling approaches that incorporate annotators' socio-demographic information to capture this variation. However, prior findings on the benefits of socio-demographic information for pluralistic modeling are mixed, calling for a systematic analysis of both its effectiveness and the mechanisms through which it improves prediction. In this work, we study $\textbf{when}$ and $\textbf{where}$ socio-demographics benefit pluralistic offensiveness modeling, as well as $\textbf{the role of explicit reasoning}$ in leveraging this information. Across three multi-annotator datasets with prompted and trained LLMs, we first show that demographic gains are small and inconsistent for individual predictions but become clearer at the group and population levels, because aggregation attenuates the idiosyncratic variation that masks small but systematic demographic signals. Second, using a reasoning and prediction format that explicitly decomposes individual judgment into an overall baseline and a demographic-specific deviation, we show that the gains arise from both components: demographic information improves baseline estimation and enables meaningful demographic-specific shifts, and interventions show that better component estimates improve final predictions. Finally, comparing direct demographic conditioning with explicit reasoning about how the text interacts with annotator demographics, we find that reasoning yields more accurate baseline and shift estimates as well as final predictions, whereas conditioning alone rarely produces informative shifts. These results suggest that the mixed evidence in prior work may partly stem from individual-level evaluation, and that the value of socio-demographic information depends on both how it is evaluated and how models are guided to use it.
  </details>

- **[Uncovering Why LLMs Fail: From Internal Mechanisms to Adversarial Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37369)**  `arXiv:2610.37369`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zirui Ding, Xinxin Fan, Hao Yuan, Shaoye Luo, Men Niu, Quanliang Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are usually evaluated by final-output correctness or safety, yet an endpoint label does not reveal how a failure develops throughout the reasoning process. We therefore ask whether ordinary reasoning failures and adversarially induced failures can be characterized using the same internal coordinates. Towards this end, we in this paper study a white-box diagnostic framework CauTrace to disclose the root-cause of why LLM fails from three fundamental inspections: Functional States, Propagation of Representation, Temporal-State Coordination and Reasoning Trajectory. First, seven functional states are creatively defined to represent the support and constraint processing in LLMs. Second, the propagation of representation change measures how the changes among the seven states persist across subsequent Transformer blocks. Third, temporal-state coordination and trajectory topology characterize when the commitment and verification occur relative to the support, and how the dominant state sequence evolves over the full inference process. Finally, we evaluate CauTrace on both clean reasoning failures and paired adversarial perturbations. Across Qwen and LLaMA-family models, the failed traces show weaker Attention-MLP agreement on support-state updates and less verification activity relative to commitment, while the exact representation-level signatures and timing shifts vary by architecture. Held-out repair and steering tests demonstrate consistent operational signals that were in line with our claims for certain models and specific settings, with aligned steering producing the predicted directional changes in process measures. Overall, CauTrace provides a unified diagnostic framework for analyzing failure dynamics through changes in support, commitment, and verification, which would shed light on the in-depth research on LLM security.
  </details>

- **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477)**  `arXiv:2610.32477`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaiwen Wu, Chaochao Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Dual Exam: Controlled Evaluation of Multimodal Agents and World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31066)**  `arXiv:2610.31066`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhefan Rao, Meng Chu, Xuan Billy Zhang, Jingfan Chen, Guohao Cai, Runyi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Models (WMs) can support Multimodal Agents (MAs) through simulated experience, while Agent trajectories can inform World Model refinement. Evaluating this interaction requires separate evidence about action selection and state evolution. We introduce Dual Exam, which tests Agents under fixed environment response mechanisms and World Models under fixed action interventions across physical, digital, social, and scientific domains. The Agent track compares 10 configurations on 1,088 items in 15 reporting categories. The World Model track contains 400 cases across eight scenarios; 15 configurations have full coverage and one covers six scenarios. GPT 6 Astra leads the case-weighted Agent Index at 31.3, and GPT-Image-2 leads the full-coverage WM Overall at 21.7. These scores use different tasks and metrics and are not directly comparable. A 150-case pilot of one coupled pairing records zero task successes and motivates component-level diagnosis. Results reveal uneven task performance and failures to preserve task-relevant state. Their value for predicting coupled success or sustained reciprocal learning remains to be established.
  </details>

- **[ARCANA: A Benchmark for Abstraction and Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271)**  `arXiv:2610.29271`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqi Chen, Zilei Wang, Wei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analogical reasoning is widely considered a fundamental component of human intelligence. Few benchmarks are designed to explicitly test far analogical reasoning - reasoning between domains that are superficially dissimilar yet share deep relational or structural similarities. In this work, we introduce an analogical reasoning benchmark called Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus of ANAlogy (ARCANA). The tasks of ARCANA require performing far analogical mappings from real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations, different from the well-known ARC-AGI benchmark which primarily emphasized innate human priors. We validate ARCANA through a human study, demonstrating that humans solve these tasks reliably. In contrast, evaluations across a range of large language models show a distinct performance gap compared to humans, revealing systematic failure modes that remain challenging for current models. To address this gap, we propose ConceptSIR, a reasoning framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection. Experiments demonstrate that ConceptSIR enables standard LLMs to solve several challenging ARCANA tasks that were unsolved by direct prompting in our evaluation. Overall, our results suggest that far analogical reasoning remains an open problem for contemporary LLMs. All the materials are available atthis http URL. The complete codebase, including benchmark, and ConceptSIR, is publicly available atthis https URL. All ARCANA data and task instances can be accessed and explored atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Hippo: Training-Free Agent Self-Evolution via Hippocampal Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24453)**  `arXiv:2610.24453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yutong Song, Jiang Wu, Yuxuan Ren, Tony Qin, Baoyuan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motivated by recursive self-improvement (RSI), we study how LLM agents can learn through external memory without updating model parameters. Extracting useful experience from a single attempt is difficult: flawed reasoning can produce success, while poor execution can obscure a sound strategy. Preserving its value over time is also difficult: our accumulation baseline without consolidation initially improves but eventually performs below a no-memory baseline. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. We introduce Hippo, a memory framework inspired by selective hippocampal encoding and replay that learns from these comparisons when shortcomings are diagnosed. It compares the resulting experience with existing memory to merge overlaps and resolve conflicts through explicit applicability conditions. This allows prior guidance to be revised without storing every lesson as a separate entry. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. The gains extend across the evaluated base models of varying capabilities, indicating that memory-based adaptation remains useful even for highly capable models.
  </details>

- **[Encounter-Driven City-Scale Human Mobility Generation with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17672)**  `arXiv:2610.17672`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Li, Renhe Jiang, Chuan Xiao, Jiawei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for human mobility generation by modeling individual routines, activities, and mobility preferences. However, existing approaches remain largely resident-centric: generating realistic individual trajectories does not necessarily recover the encounters that emerge when multiple residents co-occur at the same place and time. To this end, we study encounter-aware human mobility generation, which jointly preserves individual mobility and population-level interaction structure. We propose EncounterMob, an encounter-driven LLM framework consisting of a coarse-to-fine Encounter Generator and an Iterative Coupling Framework. The Encounter Generator first captures city-level encounter rhythms and then instantiates participant-time-venue encounters, while the Iterative Coupling Framework coordinates these encounters with personal mobility behavior through successive refinement. We further introduce an encounter recovery protocol covering encounter marginal and joint distributions over residents, times, and venues. Experiments on the Japan and NYC datasets show that EncounterMob outperforms all external baselines on all seven encounter metrics and all six personal mobility metrics. Compared with ELLMob, it reduces the participant-time-venue joint error by 40.4%/41.7% and the mean personal mobility error by 58.8%/32.7% on Japan/NYC, respectively.
  </details>

- **[ASIWorld: Benchmarking Autonomous Agents in a Persistent Survival Sandbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14807)**  `arXiv:2610.14807`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jianfeng Lin, Runze Wang, Haorong Hong, Yuxuan Xie, Zeno Zeng, Zhongyuan Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating autonomous agents requires examining whether they can translate goals into coherent action across extended interactions. In persistent environments, this requires coordinating interdependent actions under incomplete observations, accounting for delayed effects, and adapting as earlier decisions shape subsequent opportunities. We introduce ASIWorld, a survival sandbox that combines persistent world and character state with character-scoped observations and a programmable asynchronous interface to study sustained goal execution. Agents pursue interdependent production and exploration tasks through direct actions or agent-authored programs that continue running between model calls. The evaluation assesses task success, the attainment of development achievements, and survival costs, complemented by an analysis of execution behavior. In an exploratory study of nine agent configurations using a common harness, eight complete the production task and six complete both tasks. Analyses of execution trajectories and performance across difficulty levels reveal different preparation, combat, and recovery behaviors among configurations with similar task outcomes. Incomplete preparation is associated with stalled progression, while configurations that complete the tasks differ in survival costs and the fulfillment of additional achievement criteria. These findings illustrate the value of assessing sustained goal execution through both task outcomes and the behaviors and costs associated with achieving them.
  </details>

- **[Reliability of LLM Judges for Evaluating Entity Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09554)**  `arXiv:2610.09554`  `cs.AI`  
  _Vaibhava Lakshmi Ravideshik, Mayank Kejriwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Entity Alignment (EA) identifies equivalent entities across knowledge graphs and is critical for knowledge base integration and ontology merging. Evaluating EA systems at scale requires expensive expert annotation, making systematic assessment across diverse domains practically infeasible. LLM-as-judge evaluation offers a potentially scalable alternative, yet its reliability for structured prediction tasks like EA remains unstudied. We present the first systematic benchmarking study across three frontier models, three datasets, and four EA systems, using perturbation bias diagnostics, meta-evaluation across all dataset--judge--prompt combinations, and counterfactual label-flip tests. We identify anchor bias, a failure mode in which judges invert discrimination when the system's decision label is visible. Judge discrimination collapses under label-exposing prompts (J-ROC-AUC 0.12--0.87), while a label-free protocol recovers near-ceiling capability on distinctive-name datasets (0.98--1.00 for NeuSymEA) and significant recovery on biomedical pairs (0.93--0.95). Counterfactual label flips show that the visible label causally shifts scores by more than 2 points in 53--99% of pairs (FSR), with sensitivity growing with model capability, a pattern we call the frontier model paradox: stronger judges are more label-sensitive, not less, yet also better label-free predictors. The direction of the shifts is mixed, so we report this as an observed pattern. A blinded two-annotator human evaluation (102 pairs, Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.902) provides a gold-label reference. We release the first biomedical EA benchmark (MeSH--SNOMED CT, 15K pairs) and a reproducible auditing framework for LLM judge reliability in EA. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Do Causal World Models Help Modular LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00012)**  `arXiv:2610.00012`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly operate in modular systems, where planning requires coordinating multiple functional components whose actions can causally change downstream states and behaviors. This makes intervention-aware prediction essential: an agent must anticipate not only what is likely to happen next, but what will happen as a consequence of its own actions. We find that purely observational world models can incur irreducible intervention-time error in such systems, even when they accurately model observational trajectories. To address this limitation, we propose CausalCompose, a causal world-model framework that equips modular LLM agents with causal structure for intervention-aware prediction and planning. We theoretically show that causal composition can overcome this intervention gap with sufficient evidence and controlled local error. Experiments across diverse modular agent environments validate these results: incorporating causal structure consistently improves intervention-sensitive reasoning and planning, with particularly clear gains in structured tool-use systems where causal dependencies directly govern action execution. Our results show when causal world models help modular LLM agents: when causal dependencies are identifiable and actionable for planning.
  </details>

- **[Heavy-Tailed Memory Traces in Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00010)**  `arXiv:2610.00010`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon language agents are becoming increasingly important for tasks that require persistent interaction, planning, and reasoning over extended trajectories. External memory plays a central role in these agents by preserving past states, transitions, and experiences beyond the limited context window. Existing memory systems, however, are typically evaluated mainly by task success and token cost, with much less attention to how memory usage is distributed across stored information. We show that repeated retrieval can produce heavy-tailed memory usage, where a small set of states is accessed frequently while many others are rarely retrieved and more prone to prediction errors. We study this phenomenon through a systematic tail audit across retrieval policies and graph structures. We find that memory concentration is reproducible but strongly policy-dependent, ranging from log-normal-compatible patterns under random walks to truncated-power-law-compatible heavy tails under semantic LLM policies. Motivated by these findings, we propose Core-Tail Memory Controller (CTMC), a rank-based memory controller that allocates prompt budget with a single exponent $\tau$ while preserving summarized information from the tail. Across Synthetic Graph World and ALFWorld, CTMC substantially reduces memory usage while preserving performance, and on LongMemEval it reduces prompt tokens by 24.48% while maintaining aggregate accuracy. These results show that heavy-tailed memory usage is both a measurable property of long-horizon agent memory and a practical signal for improving memory efficiency.
  </details>

- **[When Context Changes: Understanding Update Failures in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38866)**  `arXiv:2609.38866`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junyu Guo, Yuchen Fang, Shangding Gu, Costas Spanos, James Demmel, Javad Lavaei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As preferences, goals, and facts change, LLM agents must use the current state while earlier versions remain in context. Yet they can answer with an old value of the same variable, a failure that we call stale binding. To study when models use outdated information and why, we introduce Controlled In-Context Memory (CICM), a benchmark for tracking and using updated information in conversations and agent logs. We observe that even frontier reasoning models can fail to recover the current state. We find that in open-source models probes can still recover the updated value when the model answers with an old one, pointing to a failure to select information that remains available. Component tests in Qwen and Pythia identify a mechanism for this selection failure: attention drift, where attention favors old values over the current one when producing an answer. We study a one-layer transformer to mathematically understand how this phenomenon happens: when attention scores are similar, several old values can together receive more attention than the current value. Guided by this explanation, we redirect attention toward the current value without further training. When the current value is requested directly, adjusting this intervention for each input corrects most old-value errors across various model families while preserving nearly all initially correct answers. Reliable context management therefore requires more than remembering updated information: models must use it to guide their answers.
  </details>

- **[Search-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Component Query Understanding in Roblox Game Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30177)**  `arXiv:2609.30177`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nayoung Choi, Shengjian Chen, Xiaokai Wei, Wenzheng Zhang, Daiyao Yi, Rachit Pareek, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query understanding (QU) in production search systems has typically relied on separate models for each subtask, such as search intent classification, attribute extraction, and query rewriting. Large language models (LLMs) now make it possible to consolidate these subtasks into a single model that emits a unified QU output. This consolidation, however, introduces a new optimization challenge. A single model serves multiple components, each with a distinct downstream role within the search pipeline, and the utility of each depends on how the underlying search engine acts on it rather than on agreement with static labels. We present a search-aware reinforcement learning (RL) framework for multi-component QU based on a distill-then-RL paradigm. Teacher-student supervised fine-tuning (SFT) first yields a schema-compliant small policy. The RL stage then starts from this policy, executes each QU plan on the live production search engine, and optimizes every component with a reward tailored to its operational role, rather than relying on a single reward tied to the final search outcome. On a large-scale user-generated game platform, this component-specific optimization raises NDCG@20 by 8.9 points over the SFT policy and by 3.5 points over a single end-to-end reward. The resulting small, low-latency student models also surpass their much larger teacher, and our final model is being incrementally deployed in production.
  </details>

- **[World Model Science: Self-Organized Criticality, Weak Chaos, and Metastable Belief Dynamics in Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17419)**  `arXiv:2609.17419`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyuan Song, Zekun Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents must maintain and update a world model over extended sequences of observations, actions, decisions and tool calls. We study the dynamics of long horizon agents from three perspectives: self-organized criticality, weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics. Through extensive experiments on controlled puzzles, tool-use tasks, embodied environments, multi-hop retrieval, general-assistant reasoning, and Game of Life, we study how world-model errors develop over long interactions. We find that externally increased task pressure can sharply raise the probability of collapse, while naturally occurring pressure is only a modest predictor of later failure. Errors are temporally correlated rather than independent, and their propagation changes with dependency depth while remaining bounded in finite tasks. We also find that locally valid actions can continue after the agent's overall task state has become incorrect, that error structure depends on task topology and horizon, and that belief states in embodied tasks are shallow rather than strongly trapped in incorrect states. Larger horizons allow larger error cascades, but their scale remains constrained by the task and the model's capability. Overall, we observe power-law behavior, bounded divergence consistent with weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics across long-horizon agent interactions. We call this perspective world model science: the empirical study of self organized criticality in long-horizon agent world models.
  </details>

- **[NovGauge: A Fine-Grained Benchmark for Diagnosing LLMs' Capability in Paper Novelty Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11234)**  `arXiv:2609.11234`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guoqiang Zhang, Kexin Tan, Ming Zhang, Li Ju, Wenqing Jing, Zhonghan Yue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in peer review at major AI conferences, yet novelty remains a persistent weak point. Existing benchmarks assess novelty as a single holistic score, making it difficult to diagnose which dimension a model misjudges or whether its evidence is faithful. We present NovGauge, a human-anchored benchmark for fine-grained novelty assessment diagnosis. The benchmark contains 619 paper pairs and 50 multi-paper sets, drawn from two expert sources: ICLR reviewer overlap claims and survey co-citations. Instances are independently labeled along three dimensions: task, problem, and method, capturing application goals, technical challenges, and solution approaches. We propose a cascading diagnostic pipeline that verifies per-dimension correctness, evidence grounding, and logical support. Evaluation of 18 LLMs shows hallucination rates ranging from 0% to 39% across dimensions, and among non-hallucinated correct-positive judgments, over 70% cite evidence fails to logically support the stated reason. The best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves 43-72% Verified F1 across dimensions, while most models retain less than half of their raw F1 after faithfulness verification. These results suggest that current LLMs remain far from reliable scientific novelty assessment, particularly when correctness is conditioned on faithful evidence grounding.
  </details>

- **[Epistemic Warrant for LLM Recommendations: Characterizing the Basis for Reliance When Ground Truth Is Unavailable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04127)**  `arXiv:2609.04127`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shai Vardi, João Sedoc_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to support organizational decisions. Despite their rapid and widespread adoption, users often lack a principled basis for assessing whether to rely on a specific recommendation. We introduce \emph{epistemic warrant}, a decision-level construct that characterizes the scope over which a recommendation is supported. We operationalize this construct through a reliance certificate that uses behavioral evidence from four tiers of tests to assess a recommendation's underlying warrant and assign it to a warrant category. We assess this operationalization using complementary forms of construct-validity evidence, including transformation validity, known-groups tests, independent human consensus, discriminant analyses, and robustness across models and alternative implementations. The results show that the certificate behaves in theoretically expected ways: its transformations instantiate their intended relationships, certificate assignments increase across groups prespecified to represent increasing levels of warrant, and stronger assigned warrant is associated with greater independent human consensus. Certificate assignments also capture information beyond verbalized confidence and are not readily explained by decision difficulty. The main conclusions remain robust across models and operationalizations. These findings support the reliance certificate as an operationalization of epistemic warrant for assessing the scope of support for a particular LLM recommendation when ground truth is unavailable.
  </details>

- **[ATLAS: Dual-Horizon Diagnostic Evaluation for Tool-Use Agents in Meituan Xiaotua](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30685)**  `arXiv:2608.30685`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Chen, Peilun Zhou, Zhaoyu Hu, Jiajun Chai, Derong Xu, Zhongni Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based assistants on web-scale service platforms increasingly address local-service needs through multi-step planning and tool use. Under dynamic business conditions and evolving user needs, outcome-level evaluation offers limited evidence of where execution fails or how service drifts across continued interaction. We present ATLAS, a dual-horizon diagnostic evaluation framework for industrial tool-use agents. Within-turn evaluation uses trajectory-wise signals across thinking and reflection, tool and skill execution, and response generation to identify execution failures and unmet capability requirements. Across-turn evaluation uses user-wise signals spanning multiple lifecycles to diagnose failures in maintaining or updating user intent, constraints, and corrections. Fine-grained signals use rule-based checks and LLM judgments with explicit evidence scopes and decision boundaries. To support reliable diagnosis at production scale, LLM judges are calibrated against dynamically maintained references from business logs, and selected judges are distilled into compact diagnostic models. The resulting diagnostic profiles help developers attribute failures, prioritize targeted interventions, and reassess improvements. Applied in Meituan Xiaotuan, a million-scale DAU local-services assistant, ATLAS achieves high diagnostic fidelity across calibrated judges and distilled models. Blinded human auditing and a 14-day online A/B test with nearly two million users per arm confirm that the ATLAS-guided agent improves recommendation relevance, factual reliability, user engagement (+2.84% answer read-through rate), and order volume (+6.13% paid orders), leading to full production deployment.
  </details>

- **[Design the Feedback, Not Just the Reward: Environments for Open-Ended Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27906)**  `arXiv:2608.27906`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Jin, Jikai Chen, Chengyue Yu, Yihan Chen, Hao Zhou, Demin Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has advanced large language models on tasks with reliable verifiers, where checking an outcome provides a clear basis for reward. Extending this approach to open-ended tasks requires environments that can both evaluate complex, partially successful outputs and turn these judgments into effective learning guidance. We propose a reinforcement learning framework built around a training environment that integrates human domain knowledge with model reasoning. Human domain knowledge, encoded in rubrics, anchors evaluation to task intent, while model reasoning combined with hierarchical rewards distinguishes complex open-ended outputs and links diagnosed failures to likely responsible output regions. Furthermore, these diagnostic attributions guide token-level weights in the GRPO objective, thereby translating the ambiguous notion of success in open-ended tasks into concrete guidance for policy optimization. We instantiate this framework in interactive web application generation, a representative open-ended generation task. The resulting model achieves an average pass rate of 41.25\% on MiniAppBench and 76.19\% on ArtifactsBench. Ablation studies show the contributions of individual components, while additional out-of-distribution evaluations support transferable gains without degrading performance on other evaluated tasks.
  </details>

- **[From Profiles to Steering Vectors: Global Sparse Priors and Local Semantic Calibration for Personalized Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21620)**  `arXiv:2607.21620`  `cs.AI`  
  _Liuji Chen, Zeyu Zhang, Xinyuan Zhang, Shuai Nie, Qiang Liu, Shu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized text generation requires models to capture user-specific writing styles from historical data. Existing approaches based on retrieval, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, or activation steering either introduce inference and storage overhead or struggle to separate stylistic signals from semantic content. We propose GLASS, a training-free framework for personalized generation via Global-Local Activation Steering with Sparse priors. GLASS uses sparse autoencoders to extract a global user-style prior from historical responses and constructs local contrastive style vectors over clustered interaction scenarios. During inference, it jointly injects global and local vectors into different model layers, enabling context-aware personalization without retrieval or parameter updates. Experiments on LaMP and LongLaMP show that GLASS outperforms retrieval-, fine-tuning-, and steering-based baselines across ROUGE metrics and LLM-as-judge evaluations. Further analyses show that SAE-based representations are more robust to topic and length shifts, suggesting better disentanglement of stylistic information from semantic residue.
  </details>

- **[INFRAMIND: Infrastructure-Aware Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11440)**  `arXiv:2606.11440`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ahasan Kabir, Mengxin Zheng, Qian Lou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems spend additional computation on reasoning and collaboration, but shared serving queues can prevent that computation from producing answers before a deadline. This creates a coupled decision: the reasoning workflow determines the load, while changing load determines which reasoning choices remain affordable. We introduce INFRAMIND, a hierarchical policy that adapts workflow planning and execution to serving conditions. At arrival, a planner selects the collaboration graph using task information, load and the time budget. Before each agent call, an executor jointly selects the model and reasoning strategy using refreshed telemetry and remaining time. A shared latency penalty connects the two policies. Our analysis characterizes why observing load matters when congestion reverses the preferred computation. Across five benchmarks on a shared five-model deployment, INFRAMIND improves low-load accuracy by up to 7.6 percentage points over the strongest evaluated baseline. On 500 MATH questions with matched EDF scheduling and per-request deadlines of 10-300 s, it increases correct, on-time answers from 20.4% to 33.8%. With 15 models and Azure timings replayed on the same benchmark and deadline tiers, this fraction rises from 11.6% to 54.8% over MasRouter. These results support adapting reasoning decisions to the serving capacity available throughout a workflow.
  </details>

- **[AEM: Adaptive Entropy Modulation for Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00425)**  `arXiv:2605.00425`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Zhao, Songlin Zhou, Yuxin Zhang, Stephen S.-T. Yau, Wenyu Zhang, Lun Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has substantially improved the ability of large language model (LLM) agents to interact with environments and solve multi-turn tasks. However, effective agentic RL remains challenging: sparse outcome-only rewards provide limited guidance for assigning credit to individual steps within long interaction trajectories. Existing approaches often introduce dense intermediate supervision, such as process reward models or auxiliary self-supervised signals, which increases supervision and tuning complexity and may limit generalization across tasks and domains. We present AEM, a supervision-free credit assignment method that adaptively modulates entropy dynamics during RL training to improve the exploration-exploitation trade-off. Since in agentic RL the environment is typically affected by a complete response, rather than an individual token, our analysis lifts entropy dynamics from the token level to the response level, aligning uncertainty estimation with the effective action granularity of LLM agents and reducing sensitivity to token-level sampling noise. We further show that entropy drift under natural-gradient updates is governed by the interaction between the sampled-response advantage and its relative surprisal. Motivated by this result, AEM derives a practical response-level uncertainty proxy and uses it to rescale advantages, leveraging the evolving balance between positive and negative samples to naturally transition from exploration to exploitation. Extensive experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SWE-bench-Verified with models ranging from 1.5B to 32B demonstrate that AEM consistently improves strong RL baselines, including a +1.4\% gain when integrated into a state-of-the-art software-engineering RL training framework.
  </details>

- **[PRISM: Pushing the Frontier of Deep Think via Process Reward Model-Guided Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02479)**  `arXiv:2603.02479`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rituraj Sharma, Weiyuan Chen, Noah Provenzano, Tu Vu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  DEEPTHINK methods improve reasoning by generating, refining, and aggregating populations of candidate solutions, which enables strong performance on complex mathematical and scientific tasks. However, existing frameworks can still suffer from unreliable correctness signals during inference, which creates a population- enhancement bottleneck where deeper deliberation amplifies errors, suppresses correct minority solutions, and yields weak returns to additional compute. In this paper, we introduce a functional decomposition of DEEPTHINK systems and propose PRISM, a Process Reward Model (PRM)-guided inference algorithm that uses step-level verification to guide resampling, proposal acceptance, and solution aggregation. During refinement, PRISM uses PRM scores to guide population resampling and stochastic local refinement, which concentrates computation on higher-quality reasoning while preserving diversity. When paired with PRM- score-vote, a PRM-guided aggregation method, PRISM is competitive with or outperforms existing DEEPTHINK methods, reaching 90.0%, 75.4%, and 71.4% with gpt-oss-20b on AIME25, HMMT25, and GPQA Diamond, respectively, while exceeding gpt-oss-120b. Additionally, our analysis shows that PRISM produces consistent net-directional correction during refinement and maintains competitive accuracy when the initial population contains few correct candidates.
  </details>

- **[Agentic AI for Clustering and Relationship Discovery in Prediction Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02436)**  `arXiv:2512.02436`  `cs.AI`  
  _Agostino Capponi, Alfio Gliozzo, Brian Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prediction market platforms list thousands of contracts whose logical and economic connections are implicit in their resolution rules. We introduce a hierarchical agentic reduction (HAR) pipeline for constructing inspectable cross-event relation graphs and a behavioral benchmark for evaluating their alignment with market prices. This partitions a catalog of prediction markets, specified by question and resolution text, into topic clusters, applies typed LLM reasoning to small batches, and proposes signed relations with rationales between specific markets. We evaluate semantic relation graphs spanning eight monthly Kalshi catalogs (February to September 2026). Using data from 30.3 million trades, we compute a benchmark that compares linked pairs' signed price-change correlations with those of matched, unlinked controls from the same topic cluster on identical timestamps. Under full-sample and leave-first-week-out evaluation, relations identified by our pipeline exhibit higher signed Pearson price-change correlations, with statistical significance, than matched within-topic controls. This increase also appears under Spearman correlation and becomes larger among relations labeled as medium or high strength. This benchmark provides a criterion for comparing relation-discovery methods against observations beyond their input text, and our results indicate the ability of agentic AI and LLM reasoning to identify meaningful relations between individual markets in connected Web marketplaces.
  </details>
